User: why is the temperature scale used in most of the world not the one that starts at absolute zero
Joe: Absolute zero is too cold to be a useful everyday baseline, so scales like Celsius and Fahrenheit are designed to sit around the range people actually live in.

User: why does humidity sometimes make the air feel colder than the reading says
Joe: Moist air draws heat from your skin faster than dry air at the same temperature, so you feel colder than a thermometer reading would suggest.

User: why do red blood cells stack on each other in a test tube
Joe: Red blood cells have proteins on their surface that attract one another, so they form stacks called rouleaux when they are packed closely together.

User: why does blood look bright red in the arteries and dark in the veins
Joe: Oxygen-rich blood binds a bright red form of haemoglobin, while oxygen-poor blood leaves the haemoglobin in its duller darker state.

User: why do we need to breathe in oxygen when we burn sugar for energy
Joe: Respiration needs oxygen as the final step of the reaction, because sugar alone cannot be broken down into carbon dioxide and water without it.

User: why does breathing out more carbon dioxide than you breathe in
Joe: Cells turn sugar into carbon dioxide and water using oxygen, and the carbon dioxide then leaves through the lungs to be exhaled.

User: why does a cut finger heal faster in some animals than in humans
Joe: Many animals simply seal a wound quickly without rebuilding tissue, whereas humans rebuild skin, collagen and blood vessels, which takes longer.

User: why do you feel thirsty after exercise
Joe: Sweating loses water and salt, which lowers the blood's osmotic pressure, and the brain responds by asking for more fluid to put it back.

User: why does the heart beat faster during exercise
Joe: Working muscles need more oxygen and glucose, so the nervous system speeds the heart up to deliver that extra blood more quickly.

User: why does the heart have four chambers
Joe: Two pumps side by side keep oxygenated and deoxygenated blood completely separate, and two chambers on each side handle the pressure difference.

User: why does blood flow one way through the heart
Joe: Valves between the chambers open in one direction only, so the muscle can only push blood forward as it contracts.

User: why does a blood clot block a leaking vessel instead of the wound
Joe: Clotting proteins and platelets gather where a vessel is injured, and the clot seals the break so the vessel itself stays open.

User: why does a broken bone sometimes heal crooked
Joe: If the two ends are not held in line, the new tissue knits them together in whatever position they sat in, leaving the bone misaligned.

User: why does digestion start in the mouth
Joe: Chewing breaks food into small pieces and saliva wets it and begins breaking down starch, which makes the later work in the stomach easier.

User: why does the stomach churn rather than sit still
Joe: Muscular squeezing mixes the food with acid and enzymes and moves it along, which is what turns a meal into a workable liquid paste.

User: why do we have taste buds that sense bitter things
Joe: Bitterness in plants usually warns of something toxic, so sensing it early keeps you from eating something harmful.

User: why does the nose get blocked when you have a cold
Joe: Mucous membranes swell and make extra mucus to fight the infection, and that swelling narrows the air passages you breathe through.

User: why does spicy food feel hot but is not physically hot
Joe: Capsaicin in chillies binds to the same nerve receptors that report real heat, so the mouth is fooled into reading the chemical as temperature.

User: why do your ears pop when you fly
Joe: Cabin pressure changes faster than the pressure in your middle ear can equalise, and the pop is air moving through the tube to catch up.

User: why do you get hiccups
Joe: The diaphragm spasms out of rhythm, and the sudden intake of air against the vocal cords makes the closing click you hear.

User: why does your neck have more nerve endings than your arm
Joe: The neck carries a far denser sensory supply than the limbs, so it is unusually sensitive to light touch and pressure.

User: why do newborns recognise their mother's voice
Joe: They heard it clearly for the final months before birth, and that familiarity lets them pick the same voice out from others afterwards.

User: why does laughter spread so easily between people
Joe: Laughter is hard to produce alone, and it carries an emotional signal that mimics a response, so people around it tend to laugh as well.

User: why do we yawn when someone else yawns nearby
Joe: Yawning seems to come from noticing it in others, and the copying happens without any conscious decision.

User: why do humans lose baby teeth
Joe: Adult teeth are larger and stronger, and a second set replaces the smaller first set so an adult mouth can handle grown-up biting.

User: why does a cut on the scalp bleed so much
Joe: The scalp is densely supplied with blood vessels that lie close to the surface, so a shallow cut there severs more of them than one elsewhere would.

User: why does lying in bed sometimes make your nose run
Joe: Fluid drains down from the sinuses and the throat while you are flat, and gravity has nowhere to send it, so it collects at the front of the nose.

User: why does sitting in cold water wake you up faster than sitting in cold air
Joe: Water carries heat away from your skin far faster than air does, so cooling tissue reaches the temperature that triggers alertness much sooner.

User: why does the brain use so much energy for its size
Joe: Neurons keep sending signals and maintaining ion balances even when idle, and all that continuous work costs more energy than almost any other organ.

User: why does damage to the left side of the brain often affect the right hand
Joe: Most of the body is wired so that each side of the brain controls the opposite side, and the hands fall heavily into that crossed pattern.

User: why does alcohol make you clumsy
Joe: It interferes with the cerebellum, which is the part of the brain that times and coordinates movement, so control degrades before consciousness does.

User: why does fatigue make you clumsy rather than just sleepy
Joe: Attention and fine motor control run on the same tired circuits that keep you alert, so they fail together as those circuits tire.

User: why does the body feel a temperature change quickly but pain slowly
Joe: Touch and temperature signals travel on thick fast nerves, while pain travels on thinner fibres that also carry extra signals in both directions.

User: why does a splinter hurt while it is in the skin but not when it is gone
Joe: The thorn itself caused the initial pain, and the ache that lingers comes from the surrounding tissue reacting to the damage.

User: why do we need to blink
Joe: Blinking spreads a fresh film of tears across the cornea, clearing dust and keeping the surface wet enough to stay transparent.

User: why does staring at a screen cause dry eyes
Joe: Long glances cut blink rate sharply, and with less tear film spreading over the eye the surface dries and starts to sting.

User: why does washing your hair with cold water leave less lint
Joe: Cold water tightens the cuticle of each strand, so the hair lies flatter and less of it catches and holds loose fibres from the towel.

User: why do we shiver with cold before getting a fever
Joe: The body first ramps up shivering to raise temperature, and the rising heat later makes you feel hot even as the underlying infection takes over.

User: why does a fever slow some infections
Joe: Many germs are tuned to work best near normal body temperature, so raising it a couple of degrees pushes them toward conditions they handle badly.

User: why do antibiotics not work on viruses
Joe: Antibiotics target bacterial structures such as cell walls and ribosomes, and viruses have none of those to interfere with, so the drug has nothing to act on.

User: why does soap kill some germs and not others
Joe: Soap dissolves the oily membrane around many bacteria and viruses, which breaks it apart, while tougher coats like a bacterial spore shell shrug it off.

User: why do we need a new flu shot each year
Joe: The virus mutates quickly, and last year's antibodies may no longer match the strains circulating this season.

User: why does a headache often sit in a band around the head
Joe: The sensation comes from the membranes and blood vessels surrounding the brain, which are sensitive to pressure changes, and they wrap the whole head.

User: why is a bruise dark purple at first
Joe: Blood has leaked under the skin and broken down into breakdown pigments, and that deoxygenated blood looks dark purple before it fades to green and yellow.

User: why do veins look blue through the skin
Joe: Skin absorbs more of the red light coming back out than the blue, so what reaches your eye is the bluer fraction and the vein looks blue.

User: why does a strong light make you see spots for a moment
Joe: The cells that register light react strongly and briefly overload, and that afterimage lingers once the real light is gone.

User: why does the moon look flat and the sun look round
Joe: The sun is far enough away to be seen almost as a true disc, while the moon is close enough that its craters and uneven surface catch the eye first.

User: why can you not see stars in daylight
Joe: The sky is lit by scattered sunlight that swamps the faint light from the stars, so you need darkness to bring them above the glare.

User: why does a reflection in a calm lake look upside down
Joe: The water acts as a level mirror, and the reflection maps the scene so the sky appears below the shore and the trees appear hanging down into it.

User: why does a swimming pool look deeper than it is
Joe: Light bends as it crosses from water into air at an angle, so the apparent bottom shifts and the distance reads as greater than the real one.

User: why does a straw look bent in a glass of water
Joe: The bend comes entirely from refraction at the water surface, where the ray of light from the straw turns sharply before reaching your eye.

User: why does a coin at the bottom of a cup become visible when you fill it
Joe: Adding water adds a second surface with less bending at the top, so the net refraction from the coin to your eye becomes small enough to aim the line of sight right.

User: why does a spoon bent in water still look straight when far away
Joe: The bend depends on how far your eyes are from the surface, and from far away the two refracted rays land close enough together to look normal.

User: why do we see a green flash at sunset
Joe: Atmospheric dispersion spreads the colours of the low sun, and the green band is the one that survives the journey best at that particular angle.

User: why is a lightning flash brighter than it looks in photos
Joe: A camera exposure that captures the flash also overexposes the rest of the scene, so the foreground looks far darker than it really was.

User: why does thunder rumble instead of cracking when the storm is far off
Joe: Sound takes different paths through the shifting air of different density, and the different arrival times smear each crack into a long roll.

User: why does a bell ring differently on land and underwater
Joe: Water is denser than air, so it carries the vibration along faster and with less loss, and the pitch changes as the speed of sound in the medium changes.

User: why does a tuning fork stay quieter if you press its base against a table
Joe: The table acts as a much larger sounding board, so the fork's vibration energy goes into the table and is radiated away as sound instead of staying in the fork.

User: why does a struck glass sing when you run a wet finger around the rim
Joe: The finger repeatedly slips and sticks at exactly the rate matching the glass's natural vibration, and that repeated pattern pumps energy into one clean note.

User: why does singing with your finger on your nose feel funny
Joe: It applies a slight pressure to your sinuses and skull, and that small resonance change is enough to make your own voice sound strange to you.

User: why does the letter z sound buzzier in some accents than others
Joe: English z uses a buzzing lip sound in some varieties and a hissing one in others, and which one you use depends on where you learned the language.

User: why is it harder to understand an accent you have never heard
Joe: Your ear relies on stored knowledge of which sounds go together in a language, and unfamiliar sound patterns fall outside what you learned to expect.

User: why does music often stick in your head more than speech
Joe: Music repeats on a predictable loop and uses a limited set of notes, which keeps matching patterns of the brain busy long after it stops.

User: why does a rhythm track keep going in your head when nothing is playing
Joe: The brain's timing networks keep rehearsing the pulse on their own, which is why the beat goes on running without any outside input.

User: why does a piece of music sound sad when played in a minor key
Joe: Minor scales lean on intervals that sound more tense and unsettled to most listeners, so the same melody carries a sadder feel.

User: why does loud music seem to lose its bass when you turn it down
Joe: Your ears are more sensitive to soft low frequencies than to soft high ones, so at low volume the bass becomes harder to pick out than it is at high volume.

User: why does a heavy metal instrument ring louder than a light one
Joe: More metal means more surface to move, so the same strike shakes more air and the note carries further before dying away.

User: why does a guitar need to be in tune to every fret to sound right
Joe: Frets sit at mathematically exact intervals above the open string, and if the string drifts the notes stop lining up with those intervals.

User: why do bagpipes sound out of tune when you stop playing
Joe: A stopped bagpipe lets the air drain out through the reeds, and as the pressure drops the pitch sags and the note cracks away from where it was.

User: why does a choir need a conductor rather than just listening to each other
Joe: Each singer hears the others slightly delayed, and a conductor gives everyone the same timing reference at the same moment so the ensemble moves as one.

User: why does an opera hall need to be built so carefully
Joe: The shape of the walls and ceiling decides how sound is scattered and reflected, so fine control of reflections keeps voices clear instead of boomy.

User: why does a cathedral stay cool inside in summer
Joe: Thick stone walls take a long time to heat up, and the tall stone keeps heat out of the living space long enough to stay comfortable through the day.

User: why do some buildings sway safely and others collapse in an earthquake
Joe: Safe buildings flex and shed energy as they move, while brittle ones cannot bend and crack apart when the ground shifts under them.

User: why does a building sit on a base that can move with the ground
Joe: Isolation bearings let the structure slide slightly relative to the earth, so the movement happens below the building instead of tearing it apart.

User: why does a lightning rod on a church spire not invite strikes
Joe: It does invite them, and that is the point, because the metal carries the strike safely into the ground instead of through the building.

User: why is a lightning flash so much brighter than a spark from a key
Joe: The channel heats to around 30,000 degrees, far hotter than a key, so it glows far more intensely and briefly.

User: why does the heating effect of a lightning strike show up as a scorch mark
Joe: The extreme heat in the channel superheats the surface it touches, which scorches paint and burns wood where the discharge passed.

User: why do you smell ozone before a strike
Joe: The electric field before a discharge splits oxygen molecules, and the loose oxygen atoms recombine as ozone, which has a sharp smell.

User: why do you get ear pain in an aeroplane during descent
Joe: The pressure outside drops faster than the pressure in your middle ear, so the eardrum stretches inward and the pressure difference makes it ache.

User: why does a nosebleed stop when you lean forward
Joe: Leaning forward keeps the blood from running down your throat into your stomach, and it also lets you see whether the bleeding has actually slowed.

User: why does drinking cold water not cure a headache
Joe: Most headaches come from blood vessel changes and tension, and water helps only if dehydration is the cause, which is only sometimes the case.

User: why does walking in cold air wake you up more effectively than a cold shower
Joe: Cold air cools your face and hands while you move, and that gentle cooling raises alertness without the shock that dampens your nervous system.

User: why is the flu more common in winter
Joe: People crowd indoors when it is cold, so viruses pass between hosts far more easily than they would if everyone were spread out in summer.

User: why do you need to sleep to consolidate memory
Joe: During sleep the brain replays the day's experiences and transfers them from short-term holding into more durable long-term storage.

User: why can sleep-deprived people feel confident but be wrong
Joe: Tiredness weakens the brain's error-checking, so judgement suffers while confidence stays high, and mistakes go unnoticed as they happen.

User: why do some dreams feel impossibly real in the morning
Joe: Sleep carries the dream forward into waking, and when the waking mind takes over it can treat the dream memory like something that actually happened.

User: why does memory get worse for very old events the same way
Joe: Recalling something rewrites the trace of it, so each retelling changes the memory slightly and the earliest versions fade away.

User: why do you suddenly remember names more easily when you sleep
Joe: Sleep strengthens links between related memories, and a name paired with a face is exactly the kind of pairing that gets replayed.

User: why does practice help more than just repeating a thing
Joe: Repeating without feedback builds habit rather than skill, whereas noticing and correcting mistakes during practice is what actually improves performance.

User: why does a skill feel harder to relearn after a long break than the first time
Joe: The old memory trace is still there but no longer matches your current habits, and updating it takes more effort than building the trace from nothing.

User: why does recognising a face you know take almost no time
Joe: Visual recognition of familiar faces appears to run on a separate fast pathway, which is why you identify someone before you have read their features.

User: why do some words slip your mind just when you need them
Joe: Retrieving a word puts you in a temporary state where competing memories crowd it out, and a slight pause often lets the word reappear.

User: why does the brain find an unrelated word related to what you are thinking
Joe: Ideas are linked by shared features and by the stories you have lived, so searching for one concept pulls its neighbours along with it.

User: why do we say a word is on the tip of our tongue more often for proper nouns
Joe: Proper names are unique rather than repeated, so they are stored less often and are harder to reach than common words.

User: why do large numbers feel harder to picture than small ones
Joe: Small quantities can be sensed roughly without counting, while large ones have to be tracked through words, which is slower and less vivid.

User: why does a first impression stick so firmly
Joe: Early impressions are made before much evidence exists, and once formed they bias how every later detail gets read.

User: why is it hard to give a compliment about someone's appearance
Joe: It draws attention to something the person usually wants to be unremarkable, so the compliment can land as a statement about a flaw instead.

User: why does a sincere apology usually need no explanation
Joe: Apologies are mostly about taking responsibility, and long justifications tend to shift attention back to the person who is wrong.

User: why do people trust a stranger who admits small mistakes
Joe: Claiming to be flawless can suggest something is being hidden, whereas admitting a small error makes the rest of what they say more believable.

User: why does a practical step make a compliment land
Joe: It shows the compliment was thought about rather than thrown out as reflex, which makes it read as genuine.

User: why is it easier to criticise a plan than to write one
Joe: Pointing at a flaw needs only one observation, while a plan needs a full solution that satisfies every constraint at once.

User: why do good questions open up better conversations than good statements
Joe: A question hands the other person a role in the conversation, while a statement mostly asks them to agree and keep going.

User: why does silence after a question sometimes get a better answer
Joe: Filling the gap with your own words takes the turn back, and waiting leaves space for the other person to think and answer properly.

User: why does asking someone to explain something teach it to you best
Joe: Explaining it forces you to organise the pieces in order and to notice the gaps, which is exactly the work that turns recognition into understanding.

User: why do people overestimate how much others notice their appearance
Joe: People are far too busy thinking about themselves to track everyone else closely, so the spotlight in your own head is much brighter than it is from outside.

User: why does public speaking get easier as you do more of it
Joe: The prediction of disaster is much worse than the experience of it, so repeated exposure shrinks the gap between how it feels and what actually happens.

User: why do warnings before a message change how people read the message
Joe: A warning sets up what to expect next, so people read the message in line with it rather than judging the message on its own.

User: why does a polite request get a better response than a blunt one
Joe: Politeness signals respect and leaves the other person room to decline, so they are more willing to help rather than feeling cornered.

User: why do people comply with small requests more easily than large ones
Joe: Agreeing to something small sets up the pattern of helping, so a larger request later feels like a natural continuation of the same relationship.

User: why does reciprocity make people return favours
Joe: Receiving something good creates pressure to give something back, which keeps social exchanges roughly balanced over time.

User: why do people share information that makes them look good
Joe: Sharing flattering information raises their own standing in the eyes of whoever hears it, so the incentive favours telling the good version.

User: why do people remember unusual examples better than ordinary ones
Joe: Unusual events stand out from the background of ordinary ones, so they get a stronger memory trace and survive being recalled later.

User: why does a vivid story teach a lesson more easily than a summary
Joe: A story gives a concrete case to picture, while a summary has to be held abstractly, and the concrete version is far easier to apply again later.

User: why does the stomach lining not digest itself
Joe: The stomach secretes acid and pepsin, but a layer of mucus and bicarbonate protects its own wall, and any that breaches it is replaced quickly.

User: why does the small intestine look wrinkly inside
Joe: Folds and tiny villi multiply the inner surface many times over, and that extra area is what lets nutrients be absorbed so fast.

User: why do the large intestine absorb so much water
Joe: The body cannot afford to lose fluid, so the colon pulls water and salts back out of what is left, leaving solid waste behind.

User: why does eating fibre help digestion
Joe: Fibre holds water and adds bulk, so waste moves along more easily and the gut wall is exercised rather than going sluggish.

User: why do beans cause wind
Joe: Large molecules in beans reach the large intestine largely unbroken, and gut bacteria ferment them and produce gas as they work.

User: why does eating slowly help you feel full sooner
Joe: Signals from the stomach and gut need time to reach the brain, and eating fast outruns them, so you overeat before fullness registers.

User: why does bread go stale faster in the fridge
Joe: Starch in bread retrogrades into a firm form at cold temperatures, so refrigeration speeds up the process that makes it hard rather than soft.

User: why does toast stay crisp longer than fresh bread
Joe: Drying the surface removes the water starch needs to soften, and that crust slows fresh moisture from getting back into the crumb.

User: why does an apple brown after being cut
Joe: Cut cells are exposed to oxygen, and an enzyme in the apple reacts with it to form brown pigment, which heat speeds up a lot.

User: why does lemon juice stop an apple browning
Joe: Its acid lowers the pH the enzyme needs, so the reaction stalls and the cut flesh stays pale for longer.

User: why does milk sour in a warm room
Joe: Bacteria in the milk multiply quickly at warm temperatures and convert the lactose into acids, which is what makes it taste sour.

User: why does pasteurisation extend milk's life
Joe: Heating briefly kills most of the spoilage bacteria, and without them the milk stays sweet far longer without changing flavour.

User: why does hard cheese last longer than soft cheese
Joe: Hard cheese has lost most of its moisture and is densely pressed, and both conditions make it far harder for spoilage organisms to grow in.

User: why does blue cheese get its veins
Joe: A mould introduced into the curds grows through the cracks as the cheese ages, and the veins it forms are where that mould spread.

User: why does cooking eggs make them firmer
Joe: Heat unfolds the proteins in the white, and unfolded proteins bond to each other, turning the liquid white into a solid network.

User: why does overcooked rubbery egg white happen
Joe: Heat keeps squeezing water out of the protein network, so the white tightens, shrinks and squeezes water until it turns rubbery.

User: why does meat brown when seared
Joe: Heat drives a reaction between amino acids and sugars on the surface, forming the compounds that give seared meat its flavour.

User: why does resting meat after cooking matter
Joe: The juices are pushed out by heat, and letting the meat sit lets them redistribute, so cutting it straight away just lets them run out.

User: why does frozen food taste slightly different from fresh
Joe: Ice crystals rupture cell walls as they form, so on thawing some of the contents mix together and the original arrangement is partly lost.

User: why does a freezer burn the surface of food
Joe: Cold dry air pulls moisture out to the ice crystals, leaving the surface dehydrated, which is exactly the condition freezer burn needs.

User: why does sealing food slow it spoiling
Joe: Airtight packaging keeps out the oxygen and moisture that microbes need, and often the heat of the processing kills them as well.

User: why does salt in a preserve not have to be extreme
Joe: Salt draws out water and the bacteria cannot grow without it, so a modest concentration in an otherwise sealed jar is enough.

User: why does sugar preserve jam but not fruit in syrup
Joe: Jam is cooked until the water has gone and the sugar is high enough to stop growth, while syrup around whole fruit leaves more room for microbes.

User: why does vinegar pickling work without heating
Joe: The acidity itself is what stops spoilage organisms, so as long as the vinegar is strong enough it preserves on its own.

User: why is botulism a risk in home canning
Joe: A sealed jar creates an oxygen-free space, and that is exactly where the botulism bacterium can grow and produce its toxin unseen.

User: why does pressure canning get the jars hotter than boiling water
Joe: Sealing raises the pressure, which lets the water inside the jar reach a higher temperature than 100 degrees and destroy resistant spores.

User: why is it safer to cool home-canned jars slowly
Joe: Heat left in a sealed jar keeps spores warm long enough to grow, so cooling it in the open air gives them less time to act.

User: why does raw egg white stop a dressing from thickening
Joe: Uncooked egg white contains a protein that binds other proteins into a web, and that binding prevents the smooth thickening you want.

User: why does cooking egg white make a foam
Joe: Heat unfolds the proteins in the white, and the unfolded chains trap air bubbles in a stable mesh that holds the foam shape.

User: why does whipped cream hold its shape
Joe: Fat globules partly join up as the cream is beaten, forming a network that traps the air you have whipped into it.

User: why does butter churn into butter
Joe: The shaking breaks the fat globules in the cream and joins the fat into a solid mass, leaving the watery part behind as buttermilk.

User: why does grated chocolate melt faster than a block
Joe: Dividing it up exposes far more surface, so heat reaches the fat and cocoa butter far more quickly than it could sink into a solid slab.

User: why does chocolate turn white when stored warm
Joe: Heat pushes the cocoa butter to the surface as fat bloom, and that pale layer sits over the dark chocolate underneath it.

User: why does coffee stir better than milk
Joe: Coffee is mostly water so the grains disperse easily, while milk is a fat emulsion that keeps its particles together until it is broken up.

User: why does salt make coffee taste less bitter
Joe: Sodium ions suppress the bitterness receptors slightly, so the same cup reads as less bitter and needs less sugar to seem right.

User: why does lemon mask the bitterness of tonic water
Joe: The sourness of citrus competes with the bitter quinine, so the taste you notice is sharp rather than bitter even though the quinine is unchanged.

User: why does chewing gum keep the jaw from getting stiff
Joe: The repeated movement keeps the joint capsule and surrounding muscles active, which is the same reason gentle movement helps a healing joint.

User: why does a dead tooth sometimes ache more than a healthy one
Joe: Once the nerve dies there is no pain from the tooth itself, so any ache comes from the tissues around it, which may still be inflamed.

User: why do fillings fall out without any pain
Joe: The material is bonded to the tooth with no nerve involved, so its loss is silent, and decay under it is what finally causes the ache.

User: why does teeth whitening not work on old fillings
Joe: Whitening works on the natural structure of the tooth, and a filling or crown is a separate material that the bleach does not change.

User: why does gum disease come from plaque rather than from brushing hard
Joe: Bacteria in plaque harden into tartar at the gum line and set off inflammation there, and brushing harder does not remove what has set.

User: why does sugar feed the bacteria that cause decay
Joe: Bacteria ferment the sugar into acid, and that acid dissolves the mineral in the enamel, which is what opens the hole.

User: why does a missing tooth change how you speak
Joe: The tongue normally rests against the teeth for shape, so a gap lets the tongue spill forward and blurs certain sounds.

User: why do dentures look stiff when someone first wears them
Joe: The mouth has to learn a new shape and the muscles have to adapt, and for a while the tongue keeps catching the new surfaces.

User: why does a knocked-out tooth do best if it goes back in immediately
Joe: Within a short window the living root surface is still healthy, and putting it straight back lets the blood supply rejoin.

User: why does milk protect a knocked-out tooth better than water
Joe: The tissue around the root stays alive longer in a nutrient medium, whereas water is a poor choice because it lacks what the cells need.

User: why does a bruise change colour as it heals
Joe: The pooled blood breaks down into pigments one after another, so it passes through dark red, purple, green and yellow before fading.

User: why does a sprain swell so quickly
Joe: Torn blood vessels spill fluid into the joint, and it cannot get back out fast enough, so the swelling rises within minutes.

User: why does ice help a sprain but not a broken bone
Joe: Ice slows bleeding and swelling, which is the whole problem in a sprain, whereas a fracture needs setting rather than any reduction in fluid.

User: why does a plaster cast stay on for weeks
Joe: Bone knits by building new tissue slowly, and the cast holds the pieces aligned while that new tissue hardens into solid bone.

User: why does a bone heal faster than a ligament
Joe: Bone has a direct blood supply so cells arrive quickly, while a ligament is fed through its fibres, which repair much more slowly.

User: why do some bones heal crookedly without a doctor noticing
Joe: A bone that is only slightly out of line still knits, and the shape is only apparent on an x-ray when it matters for how the joint works.

User: why does running injure the knee more than walking
Joe: Walking has one foot down at all times, while running has an airborne phase where the leg has to absorb impact that walking never asks of it.

User: why do shoes with a raised heel cause problems over time
Joe: A raised heel shifts your weight forward, so the calf shortens and the load moves onto the balls of the feet.

User: why does swimming rarely injure the joints
Joe: The water carries your weight and lets you move without impact, so the joints move through their range without being loaded.

User: why is the air in a plane cabin kept dry
Joe: Dry air limits how much water the air can hold, which cuts both condensation on windows and the feeling of blocked ears.

User: why does a plane cabin feel easier to breathe in than a car
Joe: The cabin is pressurised to the equivalent of altitude, so the air has more oxygen in it than the air at road level in a car.

User: why do people feel drunk after a flight
Joe: Low cabin pressure very slightly alters the balance and taste of the air, which dulls your sense of your own state and your reaction to alcohol.

User: why does alcohol thin the blood and cause a headache
Joe: Alcohol widens the blood vessels and pulls water into the bloodstream, so dehydration and dilation together produce the headache.

User: why do you get a hangover headache
Joe: Alcohol inflames the vessels and the lining of the stomach, and the resulting dehydration and irritation together produce the pain.

User: why does coffee relieve a hangover headache
Joe: The caffeine narrows the blood vessels, which is the opposite of what alcohol did, and that alone brings some of the relief.

User: why do warm drinks help you feel better with a cold
Joe: Heat gives a small temporary lift to the nasal passages, and the steam loosens mucus, so a congested nose feels more open.

User: why does eating when you have a cold help more than it should
Joe: Your appetite drops partly because illness diverts energy to fighting infection, so eating restores fuel the body is using up.

User: why does chicken soup seem to help a cold
Joe: The hot liquid adds fluid, the salt loosens mucus, and the warmth eases a sore throat, which together explain most of the reported benefit.

User: why does being cold not cause a cold
Joe: Colds come from a virus, and cold air simply dries the airways and makes infection easier, so the chill itself is not the cause.

User: why do children catch colds so often
Joe: They meet viruses constantly at play, their immune systems are still learning the range of them, and close contact helps transmission.

User: why does a vaccinated person sometimes still catch an illness
Joe: No vaccine is perfect, so protection is high but not complete, and the illness that does get through is usually much milder.

User: why do some viruses change so quickly
Joe: Their copying process makes frequent small errors, and those mutations give the virus variants that can partly escape existing immunity.

User: why does a mosquito bite itch after a delay
Joe: Your immune system responds to the injected proteins rather than to the bite itself, so the swelling and itch arrive hours later.

User: why does scratching an itchy bite make it worse
Joe: Scratching releases more of the substance and spreads the local response, which sets up an itch scratch itch cycle that is hard to break.

User: why do some people get hay fever and others not
Joe: An immune response against harmless pollen, so most people breathe it with no reaction at all while others mount an allergy to it.

User: why does an insect bite need different treatment from a nettle sting
Joe: A nettle sting injects a chemical irritant, while an insect bite usually introduces protein, so one needs washing and the other an antihistamine.

User: why does a hot compress help a muscle cramp
Joe: Heat lets the tight muscle lengthen more easily and increases blood flow to it, which is what releases the painful contraction.

User: why does stretching before exercise not prevent all injury
Joe: Cold muscle is less extensible, and a hard static stretch briefly reduces the power it can produce, so it is not a guarantee of anything.

User: why does warm-up raise the temperature of the muscle
Joe: Movement increases blood flow to the working muscle, and that extra supply lets it contract more strongly and recover faster.

User: why does the body burn more fat when you exercise lightly than hard
Joe: Light exercise lets fat supply most of the fuel, while very hard efforts push the body towards using stored carbohydrate for speed.

User: why do muscles burn during unfamiliar exercise
Joe: The workload is new, so the fast-twitch fibres being recruited are unaccustomed, and they accumulate waste faster than they clear it.

User: why does that burn disappear after a few sessions
Joe: Repeating the movement grows and recruits more fibres, so the same load is now shared out and no single tissue is overloaded.

User: why do muscles get bigger when you lift
Joe: Lifting loads the muscle, and the body responds by adding contractile protein so the muscle can handle more next time.

User: why do you lose muscle when you stop using it
Joe: Unused muscle is broken down, because maintaining it costs energy and there is no longer a reason to keep it.

User: why does a muscle cramp happen during exercise
Joe: A muscle gets overworked and its fluid and ion balance is disturbed, which leaves the nerve signal switching it on when it should relax.

User: why do muscles need rest between sets
Joe: Recovery is when the tissue rebuilds, so training again immediately just interrupts it rather than adding more benefit.

User: why is breathing during lifting important
Joe: Holding your breath raises pressure in the chest, and the rise in pressure across the abdomen helps support the spine against the load.

User: why does the heart keep beating without being told to
Joe: Natural pacemaker cells in the heart make their own electrical signal, so the beat continues even when the nerve supply is cut.

User: why does a heart attack happen
Joe: A clot can block a coronary artery, so part of the heart muscle loses its blood supply and begins to die.

User: why does exercise lower the risk of heart disease
Joe: Regular activity keeps blood pressure and cholesterol lower and the arteries more open, which reduces the chance of a blockage forming.

User: why is high blood pressure dangerous without symptoms
Joe: It damages the lining of the vessels quietly over years, so by the time it causes trouble the damage has already built up.

User: why does diabetes raise the risk of eye and kidney damage
Joe: Persistently high blood sugar harms the small vessels that serve the retina and the kidneys, which is why both are checked.

User: why does insulin let sugar into a cell
Joe: Insulin makes transporters in the cell membrane carry glucose inside, and without it the sugar stays outside even when there is plenty of it.

User: why does type one diabetes happen
Joe: The immune system destroys the cells that make insulin, so the body loses the ability to control its own blood sugar.

User: why does type two diabetes develop slowly
Joe: The body first resists insulin and the pancreas compensates by making more, and the system only fails once it cannot keep up.

User: why does insulin become less effective as weight rises
Joe: Fat cells release signals that make the body less sensitive to insulin, so more of it is needed for the same effect.

User: why is a low blood sugar reading more urgent than a high one
Joe: The brain needs glucose constantly and has almost no reserve, so a rapid drop can cause confusion or collapse well before a high reading does any harm.

User: why does the body produce heat when it is cold
Joe: Shivering turns energy into heat through rapid muscle contraction, and the brain raises the body's thermostat to allow it.

User: why does a thyroid problem affect weight and temperature
Joe: The thyroid sets how fast the body burns energy, so too little makes you cold and heavy while too much makes you hot and thin.

User: why does iron matter when you lose blood
Joe: Haemoglobin is built around iron, so without enough of it the blood cannot carry oxygen properly and you tire easily.

User: why does vitamin d come from sunlight rather than from food alone
Joe: Skin makes it in response to ultraviolet light, and most diets do not supply enough, which is why it is unusual that way.

User: why do some people become deficient in a vitamin that everyone else gets from food
Joe: Either they absorb it poorly or they need more of it, and a genetic difference in absorption can be the whole cause.

User: why does too much of a vitamin cause harm if too little does too
Joe: The body stores what it does not need immediately, and past a certain level the stored amount itself starts to damage tissue.

User: why does the body need calcium every day rather than once in a while
Joe: Bone is constantly being rebuilt, and the calcium released that way has to be replaced, so a steady daily supply keeps the balance.

User: why is protein broken down before it can be used
Joe: Large protein molecules are too big to pass into a cell, so enzymes cut them into amino acids that can be absorbed and rebuilt.

User: why can you not use the protein you eat directly as your own muscle
Joe: It has to be broken into amino acids, taken apart and rebuilt according to your own instructions rather than the animal's.

User: why does bread made with yeast rise and not the bread you bake with baking powder
Joe: Yeast makes gas by fermenting the dough and needs time for that, while baking powder releases gas the instant it gets heat.

User: why does an old piece of bread need to be toasted to be edible again
Joe: Starch has set into a firm form that the mouth cannot break down easily, and heat turns some of it back into something digestible.

User: why does chewing bread long enough start it tasting sweet
Joe: Saliva contains an enzyme that cuts starch into smaller sugars, so by the time it reaches the back of your mouth it genuinely is sweeter.

User: why do sweets feel worse for your teeth than starchy food
Joe: Sugars are already simple enough to be absorbed directly, so plaque bacteria produce acid almost at once rather than working on a large molecule first.

User: why do sweets stick to the teeth while bread does not
Joe: Sticky sugars adhere to enamel and to plaque, so they stay in contact with the bacteria long enough for the acid to do damage.

User: why does a fizzy sweet feel colder than a still one
Joe: The dissolved carbon dioxide forms bubbles that irritate the nerve endings, and that irritation reads as cold just as menthol does.

User: why does sourness make you salivate
Joe: Sourness signals food that may not be safe, so the mouth produces more saliva to wash the taste away and prepare for other food.

User: why do onions make you cry
Joe: Cutting them releases an irritating gas that reaches the eyes and triggers a reflex to flush it out with tears.

User: why does chopping onions hurt less after being chilled
Joe: Cold slows the reaction that produces the gas, so less of it forms while you are cutting and less reaches your eyes.

User: why does garlic leave a smell on your hands for hours
Joe: The smelly compounds dissolve in skin oils and persist, and strong soap breaks some of them down but not all.

User: why does a metal spoon cut onion smell better than plastic
Joe: The reactive metal surface encourages the smelly compounds to break down, while plastic leaves them to soak into your skin.

User: why is aluminium cookware often coated
Joe: Bare aluminium reacts with acidic food and can spoil the flavour, while the coating keeps the metal from touching the food at all.

User: why does a wooden chopping board need oiling
Joe: Drying wood loses the oils that keep water out, so oiling restores the barrier that stops the board from swelling and cracking.

User: why does a wooden board cut differently from a glass one
Joe: Wood is slightly forgiving and dampens the impact, while glass is hard and rigid and transmits the impact straight through to the blade.

User: why do stone and concrete benches crack in winter
Joe: Water gets into the pores, freezes, expands, and pushes the material apart from inside, which is what splits it apart over time.

User: why does a water bottle crack in a freezer
Joe: The water expands as it freezes, and the rigid bottle gives it nowhere to go, so the pressure splits the plastic.

User: why does glass crack when cold water hits a hot pane
Joe: The inside of the glass expands faster than the outside from the heat, and that uneven expansion pulls the pane apart.

User: why is tempered glass stronger than ordinary glass
Joe: Heating and cooling it under controlled stress leaves the surface under compression, so cracks cannot easily start.

User: why do steel beams sometimes rust along the surface and flake
Joe: Rust forms as a loose, crumbly material with a much larger volume than the steel it replaced, so it lifts the paint and pushes outwards.

User: why does paint on a metal surface fail near the sea
Joe: Salt in the air draws moisture through the paint, which lets rust start underneath where you cannot see it until the paint bubbles.

User: why does rusting stop once a surface is coated in oil
Joe: Oil seals out both the water and the oxygen that rust needs, so the reaction cannot get started underneath.

User: why does bronze work better than copper for a bell
Joe: Adding tin makes the metal far harder and changes the note to a clearer ring, and it also resists corrosion.

User: why is brass used in instruments rather than pure copper
Joe: The zinc in brass makes it harder and more workable, so a thin, even tube can be drawn and played reliably.

User: why does a magnet lose its strength when you drop it
Joe: A hard knock can shake the domains in the metal out of line, and heating it strongly does the same far more thoroughly.

User: why does passing current through a wire make it warm
Joe: The moving charges collide with the atoms in the metal, and that friction turns the electrical energy into heat.

User: why does a thicker wire carry more current safely
Joe: More metal gives the charge more room to move, so the same voltage produces less heating for the same current.

User: why does a long wire drop more voltage than a short one
Joe: A longer wire has more resistance, and with the same current the extra resistance produces a larger voltage drop along it.

User: why does power get transmitted at high voltage
Joe: Sending the same power at a higher voltage means a lower current, and lower current means far less energy wasted heating the wires.

User: why do electric vehicles lose range in the cold
Joe: The battery is less efficient when cold and the heating of the cabin uses power, and both together cut how far the car can go.

User: why do electric bicycles have no gears
Joe: An electric motor supplies its own torque at any speed, so there is no need to change ratio to climb or to go fast.

User: why does a chain-driven bicycle need tension
Joe: The chain has to stay tight enough to grip the sprockets, and a slack chain slips or jumps instead of driving the wheel.

User: why do gears on a bicycle make climbing easier
Joe: A lower gear trades speed for turning force, which is exactly what is needed against a steep slope at low pedal speed.

User: why do trucks use many gears while cars use fewer
Joe: A loaded truck needs a wide spread of ratios between pulling away and cruising, and more gear steps provide that spread.

User: why does a tractor have a high gear for transport and a low one for work
Joe: Pulling a heavy implement needs a low ratio for force, while driving between fields needs a high ratio for speed.

User: why does a helicopter rotor need pitch control on the blades
Joe: Tilting the blades changes how much lift they generate, so collective and cyclic pitch changes let the pilot climb, turn and move forward.

User: why does a submarine have ballast tanks
Joe: Flooding them with water adds weight to push the boat down, and blowing the water out with air makes it rise again.

User: why does a submarine need to be heavy to sink but light to rise
Joe: It changes its own density by moving water in and out of its tanks, which is far simpler than changing the pressure of the sea itself.

User: why does a hot air balloon have to keep firing its burner
Joe: The balloon rises only while its air is warmer than the air outside, and it cools constantly as it climbs, so the burner has to keep working.

User: why does a weather balloon rise until it bursts
Joe: The pressure outside falls with altitude, so the gas inside expands until the envelope can no longer hold it.

User: why do pressure suits stay inflated in a vacuum
Joe: A vacuum pulls the air out of the suit, and the suit would collapse around the wearer without its own pressurisation holding it out.

User: why does a spacecraft need a heat shield rather than insulation
Joe: Re-entering at speed converts motion into heat, and a shield is designed to carry that heat away by burning away rather than letting it through.

User: why does a spacecraft window have several thick panes
Joe: Each pane spreads the load and takes a different share of the impact of debris, so the cabin stays sealed even if one is cracked.

User: why does a spacecraft have to be tested for vibration before launch
Joe: The rocket vibrates hard on the way up, and components that behave differently under vibration can shake themselves loose in flight.

User: why do artificial satellites need a stable temperature range
Joe: Electronics change behaviour as they heat or cool, so the outer surfaces are coated to reflect sunlight and radiate heat back into space.

User: why does a rover need to slow down over rough ground
Joe: Wheel slip and bumps can tip the vehicle over, so the more uneven the ground the slower it has to take each step.

User: why do factory machines have a safety cage around them
Joe: A human reaching in can be crushed by a moving part long before anyone notices, so the cage keeps hands out of the danger zone entirely.

User: why does a photocopier produce dark edges on a copied page
Joe: The drum light has a hard edge, and where the shadow falls on the page the toner does not transfer, leaving a pale stripe.

User: why does a tripod help more at slow speeds than at fast ones
Joe: The shake scales with how long the shutter stays open, so a very short exposure captures little movement and needs almost no support.

User: why does a lens get heavier as it becomes more powerful
Joe: More magnification needs larger and heavier glass elements inside, and that glass is what adds the weight.

User: why does a phone camera struggle with fast movement
Joe: Short exposures in weak light and little room for a large sensor mean a fast shutter cannot compensate, so movement shows as blur.

User: why do servers have two power supplies
Joe: So that one failing does not take the machine down, since the second supply carries the load on its own.

User: why does clearing the cache not speed a slow device up much
Joe: The cache saves network trips rather than speeding the device itself, and a device is slowed by its own memory and storage rather than by saved data.

User: why does a smartwatch measure steps but not always accurately
Joe: It infers movement from a small accelerometer rather than tracking position, so pushing a pram or riding a bus can read as walking.

User: why does a smartwatch need to be on the wrist
Joe: It reads pulse through the skin, and only skin contact with the sensor lets that signal be detected at all.

User: why do some smartwatches warn about an irregular heartbeat
Joe: They detect a rhythm that does not follow the expected pattern, which can flag a possible problem for a doctor to check but is not a diagnosis.

User: why does a smart speaker sometimes answer the wrong person
Joe: It reacts to a similar-sounding phrase, so in a noisy room it can be triggered by something that only sounded like the wake word.

User: why does a smart speaker mishear a quiet command
Joe: The microphone and its noise suppression are tuned for normal speech, and a quiet or distant command does not match what it expects.

User: why does a self-driving car need a map as well as cameras
Joe: Cameras see what is there now, while the map supplies what the road should look like and where it is safe to drive.

User: why do self-driving cars struggle in heavy rain
Joe: Rain hides lane markings and drops droplets on the lenses, and spray from other vehicles makes it harder still to see anything reliably.

User: why does a self-driving car slow down when it sees a pedestrian
Joe: Stopping distance grows with speed, so slowing early leaves far more room to stop safely if the pedestrian does step into the road.

User: why do self-driving cars need to know which side of the road to drive on
Joe: Oncoming traffic and parked cars assume a side, and getting it wrong puts two cars head-on, so the rule has to be built in.

User: why do wheeled robots avoid stairs
Joe: A wheel is round for rolling on a flat surface, and on a step it would jam or tip because it cannot find a second contact point.

User: why do warehouse robots drive on marked lanes
Joe: Marks give them a reliable position reference, which is far more dependable than trying to work out where they are from the room alone.

User: why do industrial robots have guards rather than sensors alone
Joe: A physical guard is the simplest way to be certain nothing can reach the moving parts, and it cannot be defeated by a sensor mistake.

User: why do robots struggle with shiny objects
Joe: Reflective surfaces confuse the cameras that detect shape and distance, because the light bounces rather than returning from the object as expected.

User: why does a sorting machine need to know the size of every item
Joe: It has to find somewhere suitable for each one, and that decision depends on weight and volume rather than just what the item looks like.

User: why does a dishwasher use hotter water than a hand wash
Joe: Heat and detergent together break down fat far faster, so the machine can clean heavily soiled dishes without any rubbing at all.

User: why does a dishwasher dry the dishes at the end
Joe: The heating element boils off the water left on the items, and the steam escapes as vapour once the cycle finishes.

User: why does a washing machine need to tumble the laundry
Joe: Water alone cannot clean heavily soiled fabric, so lifting and dropping the load rubs detergent and dirt through it repeatedly.

User: why does a washing machine wash at different temperatures
Joe: Hot water helps lift oil and kill microbes while cold water protects delicate fabrics and saves the energy needed to heat it.

User: why does detergent work better than soap on hard water
Joe: Detergent is built to work without forming the insoluble scum that soap produces, so it keeps working where soap gives up.

User: why does a tumble dryer wrinkle some fabrics more than others
Joe: Fibres that crease easily fold over on themselves as they are tumbled, and heat sets that fold in place quickly.

User: why does air drying leave towels stiffer than machine drying
Joe: Tumbling flexes the fibres as they dry, which keeps them loose, whereas air drying lets the fibres dry pressed together in clumps.

User: why does an iron press wrinkles out
Joe: Heat softens the fibres in a fabric and pressure plus moisture straightens them, and the fibres set in the new shape as they cool.

User: why does a steamer remove creases without pressing
Joe: Hot steam loosens the fibres and lets gravity and the movement of the head flatten them, with no direct pressure on the fabric.

User: why does a clean pan need less oil than a dirty one
Joe: Food residue holds oil away from the metal, so a clean surface lets the oil spread thin across it instead of gathering in patches.

User: why does a pan need oil at all
Joe: Oil keeps food from sticking directly to the surface, and it lets heat reach the food evenly instead of scorching it in one place.

User: why does a non-stick pan eventually lose its coating
Joe: The coating is a soft layer that scratches and wears away with metal utensils and heat, and once bare metal is exposed food begins to stick.

User: why does a cast iron pan need seasoning
Joe: Repeated heating builds up a hard polymerised layer on the surface, and that layer is what stops food from sticking to the bare iron.

User: why does a wetted wooden spoon not scorch as easily as metal
Joe: Wood does not conduct heat to the handle, so your hand stays cool and the utensil cannot transfer scorching heat into the food.

User: why does a microwave heat from the inside out in theory
Joe: The waves penetrate a short way and are absorbed all through that depth, so the heating does not rely on heat travelling in from the surface.

User: why does a microwave still heat the outside of food more
Joe: Heat then conducts from the warmer interior out to the cooler surface, so over a minute or two the outside catches up.

User: why does a plastic container survive a microwave but a metal one not
Joe: Microwaves pass through most plastics without heating them much, while metal reflects the waves and can spark at sharp edges.

User: why does a mug feel hot on the outside when the drink is very hot
Joe: Heat moves through the mug into the air and the surface, so the outer wall ends up warm enough to feel hot against your hand.

User: why does a vacuum flask keep a drink either hot or cold
Joe: The gap between the walls removes conduction, and the sealed space removes convection, so the temperature inside barely changes.

User: why does a paper cup keep a drink warm for a few minutes only
Joe: Paper conducts and lets heat escape into the air quite readily, and the layer of air inside it does the same, so the heat is lost steadily.

User: why does a wet cloth on the head feel cool
Joe: Water evaporating from it takes heat from your skin, and that heat loss is what you feel as cool.

User: why does a fan pointed at a hot room help if the air is already hot
Joe: It cools you through the evaporation from your skin, but it does nothing to the room itself unless there is water for the air to pick up.

User: why does an evaporative cooler not work well in a humid climate
Joe: Humid air is already close to holding all the water it can, so there is little room for it to take up the moisture that would cool it.

User: why does a refrigerator hum
Joe: The compressor is a motor and pump that vibrates as it works, and those vibrations are what the hum is.

User: why does a fridge need to be level
Joe: If it tilts, the compressor oil can move towards the coils and reduce their cooling, so the machine works harder and can wear out faster.

User: why does the back of a fridge get hot
Joe: The heat removed from the food plus the energy the motor used has to go somewhere, and it leaves out of the coils on the back.

User: why does a freezer burn food kept in a bag
Joe: Dry cold air takes moisture out of the food, and if that air is not returned the surface dries out and frost forms elsewhere.

User: why does a fridge make food go off faster than a cool cupboard
Joe: A fridge holds air well below the temperature at which many spoilage organisms multiply, which slows them much more than a cupboard does.

User: why does leftover rice need to be cooled quickly
Joe: Cooked rice supports bacteria at room temperature, and spreading it out and chilling it cuts the time it spends warm enough for them to multiply.

User: why does reheating rice not make it safe again if it sat out long
Joe: Heat kills most vegetative bacteria, but the toxins some of them leave behind survive reheating, so food safety is really about how long it sat.

User: why does a can of beans keep for years
Joe: Sealing and sterilising at the moment of canning removes the air and the microbes, so nothing is left to break the food down.

User: why does a dented can leak
Joe: The seal that kept air out is broken, so oxygen gets in and moisture collects around the food, which then spoils.

User: why does frozen food thaw fastest in cold water
Joe: Water carries heat away far faster than still air, and the moving water keeps replacing the thin warm layer around the food with colder water.

User: why is it safer to thaw food in the fridge than on the counter
Joe: The fridge stays just above freezing, so the food spends longer in a temperature range that microbes cannot use well.

User: why does a refrigerator keep food cold by moving heat rather than making it
Joe: Refrigeration transfers heat from the food into the room, so the room actually warms slightly while the food cools.

User: why does dry ice burn when touched
Joe: It is far colder than skin and absorbs heat so fast that it damages the tissue, which feels exactly like a burn.

User: why does dry ice fog appear when poured into water
Joe: The cold gas chills the air above the water, and that air can no longer hold its moisture, so it condenses into visible droplets.

User: why should dry ice never be sealed in a container
Joe: It keeps turning into gas that needs far more room than the solid, so a sealed container can build up pressure and burst.

User: why does a fire extinguisher hiss after use
Joe: The propellant gas escapes to push the extinguishing agent out, and that escaping gas is the hiss you hear.

User: why do most fire extinguishers need to be shaken before use
Joe: The powder inside settles and compacts over months, so shaking breaks it loose so it can flow out at all.

User: why does a fire door swing shut on its own
Joe: It is held open by a device linked to the alarm, and when the alarm sounds that device releases the door so its weight swings it closed.

User: why does a smoke alarm sound louder when there is more smoke
Joe: The chamber light scatters off the smoke particles, and the amount of scattering rises with how much smoke is present.

User: why do smoke alarms need to be on the ceiling rather than the wall
Joe: Smoke rises and collects along the ceiling, and that is the first place the sensor can detect a fire spreading overhead.

User: why do smoke alarms not go off when a fire is far below them
Joe: Smoke rises through the stairwell to the flat above before reaching a ceiling detector, which is why detectors are also fitted in hallways.

User: why is a peephole better than a door chain for seeing who is there
Joe: It gives a proper wide view of the caller with a wider angle, while a chain only shows a narrow slit and often not the face at all.

User: why should a house have a peephole fitted as well as a chain
Joe: The chain stops someone pushing in but shows little, so the peephole lets you judge the situation before opening the door at all.

User: why does a smart lock need the door open slightly to work
Joe: It powers from the small gap left as the door ajar, since a fully shut door has no way to feed power to the lock.

User: why does a fingerprint reader sometimes fail on a wet or dirty finger
Joe: It reads the pattern of ridges, and moisture or grime fills in the grooves and blurs the pattern enough to confuse the match.

User: why does a face unlock system struggle in bright sunlight
Joe: Very bright light floods the infrared sensor, and the extra light can wash out the patterns it uses to match your face.

User: why do monitors need a refresh rate above sixty
Joe: A higher refresh redraws more times per second, which reduces the flicker and the stutter you see in moving images.

User: why does a high refresh rate use more power
Joe: The panel has to drive more frames every second, and that extra work on the electronics adds to the power draw.

User: why does a projector look washed out in a bright room
Joe: Ambient light washes over the projected image and lifts the black level, so the contrast between light and dark parts shrinks.

User: why does a projector need a dark room to work well
Joe: The image is a light source competing with the light already in the room, and it only wins decisively when there is little other light.

User: why do blinds slats tilt rather than just going up and down
Joe: Tilting blocks a high angle of view while letting in some light, which is what stops a neighbour looking straight in.

User: why do fences have gaps at the bottom
Joe: A gap lets wind pass through instead of loading the fence, and it also allows drainage so the posts do not sit in water.

User: why should a wooden fence be stained rather than painted
Joe: Stain soaks into the grain and lets the wood dry out, while paint forms a film that cracks and peels as the wood moves.

User: why does a deck need to be spaced slightly apart
Joe: The gaps let water drain and air circulate, so the boards dry after rain instead of holding moisture against each other.

User: why does a pot need a drainage hole
Joe: Without a hole water collects at the bottom with no way out, and the roots sit in water instead of air and cannot survive.

User: why does a gravel layer at the bottom of a pot not improve drainage
Joe: The perched water table sits above the gravel rather than draining through it, so it simply wastes a layer of the pot.

User: why is it better to water deeply and less often
Joe: A thorough soaking reaches the whole root zone and encourages roots deeper in the pot, while frequent shallow watering keeps them near the surface.

User: why does humidity from a humidifier need a clean tank
Joe: Bacteria grow in the standing water and are blown into the room as mist, so a tank that is not cleaned turns the device into a source of germs.

User: why do solar panels generate less power on a cloudy day
Joe: Clouds scatter and absorb much of the light, so fewer photons reach the panel and it produces proportionally less current.

User: why do solar panels work best when the panel is cold
Joe: Panel efficiency rises as temperature falls, so a sunny cold day gives more output than an equally sunny hot one.

User: why do geothermal plants need hot rock
Joe: Water pumped down through hot rock returns as steam, and that steam is what drives the turbine that makes the electricity.

User: why does a nuclear plant need a large amount of cooling water
Joe: The reactor produces a great deal of heat, and the coolant must carry that heat away and into the condenser to be turned back into water.

User: why does a coal power plant produce more carbon dioxide than a gas one
Joe: Coal contains more carbon per unit of energy, so burning the same amount of power releases more of it as carbon dioxide.

User: why does a gas power station start up faster than a coal one
Joe: A gas turbine needs only a moment to reach speed, while a boiler has to heat a large mass of water and metal up to working temperature.

User: why do power lines hum in the wind
Joe: The current makes the conductors vibrate slightly, and wind makes that vibration audible as a hum that changes with the weather.

User: why does electricity travel through a pipe as well as a wire
Joe: Metal pipes carry current perfectly well, and they are used where running a separate cable would be awkward.

User: why does the electricity in a house need a fuse for each circuit
Joe: A fault on one circuit should only cut that circuit, and the fuse blows locally instead of taking the whole house down.

User: why does a residual current device trip when you touch a live wire
Joe: It compares the current flowing out with the current returning, and a difference means some of it is going through you, so it cuts the supply.

User: why does a circuit breaker trip rather than melt
Joe: It detects excess current and opens a switch mechanically, and it can be reset once the fault is cleared.

User: why should you never reset a breaker repeatedly
Joe: Each reset re-closes the circuit into the same fault, and doing it again and again risks starting a fire in the wiring.

User: why does an electric fan hum when its blades are slightly bent
Joe: A bent blade pushes unevenly on the air, so the motor works harder and vibrates in a way you hear as a hum.

User: why does a fridge compressor run in short bursts
Joe: It only needs to remove the heat that has entered since the last cycle, and running continuously would waste power once the temperature is reached.

User: why does a boiler need to be serviced annually
Joe: Combustion parts collect soot and scale over time, and servicing keeps the heat exchange clean and the combustion efficient and safe.

User: why is a gas flame blue rather than yellow in a healthy boiler
Joe: A blue flame shows complete combustion, while a yellow or sooty flame means some fuel is burning incompletely, which is both wasteful and dangerous.

User: why does a flue block become dangerous
Joe: It stops the harmful combustion gases escaping, and they then build up inside the house where they can cause carbon monoxide poisoning.

User: why is carbon monoxide dangerous
Joe: It is odourless and binds to haemoglobin more strongly than oxygen does, so it quietly replaces the oxygen your blood was carrying.

User: why is a carbon monoxide alarm mounted near the ceiling
Joe: Carbon monoxide is slightly lighter than air and mixes upward, so it collects near the ceiling where the sensor picks it up first.

User: why does a gas leak smell even though gas itself is odourless
Joe: An odourant is deliberately added so leaks can be detected by nose long before the gas reached a dangerous concentration.

User: why should you not switch a light on near a gas leak
Joe: Any spark from a switch could ignite the gas, and the airflow created by switching also spreads it through the building.

User: why does a fire extinguisher need different types for different fires
Joe: Water smothers a burning-paper fire but can spread burning-oil or electrical fires, so the extinguishing method has to match what is burning.

User: why does a see-saw rise on the side with no weight
Joe: The arm carrying the greater weight turns the plank down, so the lighter side lifts until the two moments about the pivot match.

User: why does a nut on a spanner need the right place on the handle
Joe: Turning force depends on how far the force is applied from the centre, so the same push further out produces more turning.

User: why does a plank bridge need support at both ends
Joe: Without a support the middle sags under its own weight, and that sag is what would eventually break the plank.

User: why does a tall stack of loose blocks stay up but a single tall block not
Joe: Each block supports the ones above and takes only its own weight, so the load is shared down rather than concentrated on one weak point.

User: why does a wedge cut by pushing harder rather than by a sharper edge
Joe: The wedge converts the push into a sideways force, and the harder it is driven in the larger that force becomes.

User: why does a dovetail joint hold even without glue
Joe: The angled faces stop the pieces sliding apart, so the mechanical shape itself carries the load rather than an adhesive.

User: why does a bolt need washers on both sides
Joe: They spread the clamping force so neither the nut nor the head bears on a small area that could dent or crush the material.

User: why does a scaffold tube need diagonal braces
Joe: A square frame can shear sideways into a parallelogram, and the diagonals stop that by making the shape rigid.

User: why does a ladder stand at an angle rather than straight up
Joe: A leaning ladder spreads some of its load into the wall, and without that the rungs alone would carry everything and slip.

User: why does a heavy box on a trolley tip less easily than the same box on a cart
Joe: A cart's wheels sit under the load and a trolley's handle is off to one side, so the same box sits lower and further back on the cart.

User: why does a violin string need to be tuned to a particular tension
Joe: The frequency a string produces depends on its length, thickness and tension, and only the right combination gives the intended note.

User: why does a shortened guitar string sound higher
Joe: A string vibrates faster when it is held at fewer points, so a fret pressed down acts as a new shorter string.

User: why does a thicker guitar string sound lower
Joe: A thicker string is heavier and stiffer, so it vibrates more slowly and produces a lower frequency.

User: why does a bridge on a violin have to be positioned carefully
Joe: It holds the strings at their exact length, and any movement there would change the pitch of every note on the instrument.

User: why does a piano string have more than one note per string
Joe: A very short piece of wire at one end makes the whole string ring faster, and two lengths on one string give two notes.

User: why does a bell ring at a definite pitch when struck
Joe: A bell has a set of natural frequencies fixed by its shape and size, and the strongest of them dominates what you hear.

User: why does a cracked bell still ring
Joe: A crack changes one of the natural frequencies, so that partial weakens and the remaining ones stand out with a different tone.

User: why does africa split away from the rest of the land masses
Joe: It sat over a hot spot in the mantle and the plates carrying it moved apart, which tore the continent into the pieces that became south america and the rest of africa.

User: why does africa have a bulge on its map outline
Joe: The plates underneath bulged upward as they heated and expanded, and that raised the middle of the continent above sea level.

User: why is antarctica covered in ice rather than snow turning to rock
Joe: For millions of years no plants have grown there, so there is no soil to form, and the ice just builds on more ice.

User: why is antarctica the driest continent despite being all ice
Joe: The interior sits in a ring of cold air that keeps moisture out, so almost no snow falls there even though what does fall never melts.

User: why does the antarctic ice sheet hold so much water
Joe: Snow has been accumulating for around forty million years without melting away, which has turned it into the largest reservoir of fresh water on earth.

User: why does the antarctic ice flow downhill despite the surface looking still
Joe: The ice is squeezed by its own weight, so it creeps slowly towards the coast, and the icebergs that calve are pieces of that same flow.

User: why is the greenland ice sheet important to sea level everywhere
Joe: If it melted entirely the sea would rise enough to flood every low coast on earth, so it is a reserve held out of the ocean by its own frozen state.

User: why is greenland called green when it is covered in ice
Joe: Early settlers saw the coastal fringe in summer, and the name stuck after the ice had spread across the rest of it.

User: why does the amazon river flow the wrong way across the equator
Joe: The river system is so large that it carries more water away from the narrowing south than can be replaced by the small north-flowing tributaries.

User: why does the amazon drain such an enormous area
Joe: The basin sits in a shallow saucer of sediment, so the rivers there flow gently and can wander a long way instead of cutting deep gorges.

User: why does the amazon have no obvious source in the way the nile does
Joe: Its tributaries gather across a wide plain and join slowly, and by the time they merge the water has already blended into one main channel.

User: why does the nile flow north rather than south
Joe: It follows the slope of the land south to north, carrying water from the highlands and lake region down towards the Mediterranean.

User: why does the sahara appear green at certain times of year
Joe: Rain from the tropical Atlantic reaches the southern edge in some years, and the grass responds fast while the bare ground between does not.

User: why does the sahara have such extreme day and night temperature swings
Joe: Dry air cannot hold the day's heat the way water vapour can, so the heat acquired by day radiates straight back out at night.

User: why is the arabian desert so dry
Joe: It sits around the latitude where the descending branch of the circulation brings air down and warms it, and that air has already shed its moisture.

User: why does the namib desert get its fog rather than rain
Joe: Cold air moving in off the sea at night meets warm ground and warms, and that fog is the only moisture the coast ever reliably receives.

User: why do coastal deserts often sit on the west side of a continent
Joe: The prevailing winds there blow along the coast rather than across it, so they arrive without having crossed any land to drop their moisture.

User: why does the interior of australia have such extreme heat
Joe: The land sits well inside the edge of the continent, far from any sea, so nothing cools it and the rock holds the day's heat well into the night.

User: why is australia so flat in the middle
Joe: It is the oldest land surface on earth, worn down over an enormous length of time until most of the relief had been planed away.

User: why does australia have animals found nowhere else
Joe: It split from the other southern land masses long ago and then drifted apart, so its populations evolved in isolation with no land bridge to share them.

User: why did australia lose so many of its large animals
Joe: They had evolved without people and without the large predators they faced elsewhere, so they could not cope with the arrival of humans and the animals humans brought.

User: why does the outback look red
Joe: The iron in the ancient rock has oxidised to rust, and that rusty coating gives the desert surface its colour.

User: why does australia have a reef right off its tropical coast
Joe: The seabed there sits shallower than the ocean beyond, and the corals need clear, sunlit, warm water, which is exactly what that shelf allows.

User: why does the great barrier reef sit on the edge of the continental shelf
Joe: Corals grow best where the water is shallow enough for sunlight and where warm currents flow, and the shelf edge gives both.

User: why is the antarctic peninsula so far north compared with the rest of antarctica
Joe: It juts out towards the southern end of south america, so the warm current down that coast reaches it and keeps it milder than its latitude suggests.

User: why does south america have the andes running its whole length
