Joe: The oceanic plate underneath is being forced under the continent, and that collision lifts the land into a long fold of mountains.

User: why is the andes so narrow compared with the himalayas
Joe: The andes rise where one plate slides beneath another without crumpling the continent, while the himalayas come from two continents colliding head on.

User: why does peru have both desert and rainforest on the same coast
Joe: The cold current along the shore holds back the rain, so the western slope is desert while the eastern side of the mountains catches the full force of the moist air from the east.

User: why does the amazon basin not have deserts despite reaching the equator
Joe: The trade winds carry moisture from the Atlantic across the basin, and rising air over the warm land keeps releasing that moisture as rain.

User: why are the andes volcanoes so violent
Joe: The subducting slab releases water into the mantle above it, and that lowers the melting point so magma forms and rises.

User: why do the galapagos islands sit in the middle of the ocean
Joe: They are volcanic rock pushed up from the sea floor, and they are far from any continent because they grew from the hot material beneath the pacific.

User: why do the galapagos animals differ so much from island to island
Joe: Each island is separated by deep water, so each population breeds only with its own and drifts apart genetically over time.

User: why is the amazon mouth so wide and murky
Joe: The river carries such a volume of fine sediment that it drops most of it near its mouth and spreads it across the delta instead of carrying it out.

User: why does africa have so few large natural lakes
Joe: The rivers that drain it rise on high ground and tend to run fast to the sea with rapids and falls rather than settling into long quiet stretches.

User: why does lake victoria hold so much water
Joe: It sits in a broad shallow basin formed where the land tilted, and that wide flat floor lets a very large volume collect at modest depth.

User: why is the sahel getting drier and drier
Joe: The rainfall belt has moved south, and the grazing and farming that once suited a wetter period now strip the thin soil faster than it can recover.

User: why does the sahel border look so sharply defined from the air
Joe: The vegetation changes from continuous grass cover to bare sand within a short distance, and that boundary is visible from high above.

User: why is the atlantic coast of europe warmer than the atlantic coast of canada
Joe: Warm water from the tropics crosses the ocean towards europe, and that heat goes into the air and onto the land on the eastern side.

User: why does western europe stay mild while labrador freezes
Joe: The warm current reaches western europe directly, while labrador sits on the far side of the continent with cold water flowing south past it.

User: why does the mediterranean climate suit olives and grapes
Joe: Mild wet winters and hot dry summers match how those crops grow best, and the dry summer also keeps many pests and diseases down.

User: why does the mediterranean dry out in summer
Joe: The subtropical high shifts north over the region then, and its descending air brings dry weather rather than the wet of winter.

User: why does california have a cold current along its shore
Joe: The eastern pacific carries cold water from higher latitudes southwards, and that cold flow keeps the coast cooler and drier than its latitude would suggest.

User: why does san francisco have so much fog
Joe: Warm inland air rises over the hills and pulls cool marine air inland through the gap, and that warm moist air condenses into fog as it cools.

User: why does death valley sit below sea level
Joe: The valley floor is the lowest point of a basin whose streams all drain inward, and the soil there is rich in salts because water evaporates rather than runs away.

User: why does the rocky mountains run north to south instead of east to west
Joe: The plates collided along their edge rather than head on, which squeezes the land into a long band of uplift aligned with that edge.

User: why is the colorado river able to cut a canyon through solid rock
Joe: It cut down over millions of years while the land rose, and a river holding sand and gravel at the bottom chews into the rock far faster than water alone.

User: why does the grand canyon have layers of different colours
Joe: Sediments of different ages piled up over a long period, and then harder layers resisted erosion while softer ones wore back.

User: why does the nile delta stick out into the sea
Joe: Where the river slows on reaching flat land it drops its sediment, and that build-up grows outward past the old shoreline.

User: why does the nile delta keep needing rebuilding
Joe: The sea and subsidence eat away at it faster than sediment arrives, which is why its coastline is defended and reshaped by works.

User: why does the ganges delta have so many channels
Joe: The river splits repeatedly in the flat, low land and each branch carries part of the flow, so the delta spreads into a maze rather than one outlet.

User: why is bangladesh so low and so easily flooded
Joe: It sits on the floodplain of three great rivers, and the land is barely above sea level, so a strong monsoon or a high tide reaches across it.

User: why does the arctic have permafrost
Joe: The ground stays frozen all year because the sun is low for much of it and there is no long warm season to thaw it.

User: why does thawing permafrost release carbon dioxide
Joe: The frozen ground holds organic material from plants that could not decompose, and once it thaws that material rots and gives off gas.

User: why do building foundations crack as permafrost thaws
Joe: Thawing turns frozen soil into wet loose ground that no longer supports the weight, so the building settles unevenly and cracks.

User: why is the arctic warming faster than the rest of the world
Joe: Losing bright snow exposes dark ground that absorbs more heat instead of reflecting it back, which amplifies the warming in that region.

User: why does methane from thawing permafrost matter so much
Joe: Methane traps far more heat than carbon dioxide over the same period, so even a modest release has a large warming effect.

User: why does russia have the largest coastline of any country
Joe: It stretches across eleven time zones and wraps around the far north, so its shorelines add up to a distance no other country can match.

User: why does siberia have such extreme cold
Joe: It lies far from any ocean and deep inside the continent, so there is little water vapour in the air to hold the day's heat into the night.

User: why does permafrost cover most of siberia but not western russia
Joe: The colder eastern side has stayed frozen since the last ice age, while the western part was scoured by ice sheets and later had time to thaw.

User: why does the amur river and the chinese border run in such a winding course
Joe: The border was fixed by where the river happened to run at the time, and a meandering river makes for a border that meanders with it.

User: why does the us and canada border follow a straight line so often
Joe: Surveyors set those lines by latitude and longitude rather than by any feature on the ground, which is why they cut straight across lakes and forests.

User: why did the 49th parallel get chosen as the canada-us border
Joe: It was easy to survey and describe, and the line runs through sparsely settled country, so it avoided the trouble of a border through towns.

User: why does the great lakes hold so much fresh water
Joe: They are enormous basins scoured out by ice sheets and filled with meltwater, and together they hold a large share of the world's surface fresh water.

User: why do the great lakes freeze at different times
Joe: Depth and current differ from lake to lake, and the shallower or more wind-exposed ones lose their heat faster and freeze first.

User: why is lake superior so much deeper than the others
Joe: Ice scoured its basin further, and the lake sits in a deeper trough, so it holds far more water below the freezing layer.

User: why does the niagara falls retreat upstream over time
Joe: The water erodes the softer rock under the harder cap, so the falls collapse backwards and leave a gorge behind them.

User: why are the great lakes connected by a sea
Joe: They were separate lowlands until the ice sheet scraped through, and the melting water filled the deeper basins until they joined together.

User: why does the st lawrence river matter so much for shipping
Joe: It links the great lakes to the atlantic through a navigable channel, and that short route carries a large share of a continent's freight.

User: why does the mississippi delta look like a bird's foot
Joe: Sediment builds up at the river's mouth faster than waves can remove it, so the land pushes out along the channels and leaves wetlands in between.

User: why does the mississippi flood despite all the levees built on it
Joe: Levees keep water off the banks but also stop the floodplain from absorbing the overflow, so the same volume of water is forced into a narrower space.

User: why does the gulf of mexico stay warm enough to feed hurricanes
Joe: The loop current carries warm tropical water up into the gulf, and that warm water is the fuel a hurricane needs to keep growing.

User: why do hurricane winds spin the way they do
Joe: The rotating earth sets the air turning as it moves in towards the centre, and that rotation is what organises it into a storm.

User: why does a hurricane eye stay calm
Joe: Winds converge from all directions at the very centre, so the air is rising there rather than being spun, and it has no cloud to shade the sun.

User: why do hurricanes weaken over land
Joe: Land is cooler than the sea and rougher, so the warm fuel that keeps the storm spinning is not supplied as quickly as it is used.

User: why do deltas form at the mouths of the ganges and nile but not at the amazon
Joe: The nile and ganges carry sediment that drops out as soon as they reach flat or still water, while the amazon's flow is strong enough to carry much of its load out to sea.

User: why do deltas keep changing shape over time
Joe: The river mouth shifts between channels as sediment builds up on one and the water finds another path, so the whole outline rearranges itself.

User: why does the sahara's dust reach the amazon
Joe: Winds lift fine dust from the desert and carry it thousands of kilometres westward, where it settles in the basin and feeds the soil there.

User: why does the amazon's deep soil come from that desert dust
Joe: The dust arrives as a fine mineral powder that weathers into clay, and that clay mixed with organic matter gives the basin its unusual fertility.

User: why does australia export so much iron ore
Joe: The iron is shallow and close together in enormous deposits, so it can be mined at scale far more cheaply than almost anywhere else.

User: why does saudi arabia have almost no rivers
Joe: Its climate is too dry for rivers to run for long, and the few that exist lose their water to evaporation and to sand long before reaching the sea.

User: why do oil reserves concentrate in the middle east
Joe: Millions of years of sediment deposited in warm shallow seas there was buried and cooked slowly into oil, and the rock above kept it trapped.

User: why does the persian gulf have so many tanker ships
Joe: It sits next to some of the largest oil fields on earth, so the shortest sea route for crude passes straight through it.

User: why does turkey sit between two continents
Joe: The land bridge it occupies separates the black sea from the Mediterranean, and that crossing has always been a route between Europe and Asia.

User: why do istanbul's two halves sit on different continents
Joe: The strait the city spans runs between two tectonic plates, and the line between them happens to run right through the middle of the city.

User: why does the black sea have almost no tidal range
Joe: It is narrow and long rather than wide and shallow, so the shape of the basin does not let a large tidal wave build up in it.

User: why is the caspian sea called a sea although it is a lake
Joe: It is enclosed by land and its water is fresh, which makes it a lake by definition, though it is salty and long held as a sea.

User: why does the dead sea sit so far below sea level
Joe: The land there is a rift valley that has dropped and cracked, so the floor lies hundreds of metres lower than the ocean surface.

User: why can a person float so easily in the dead sea
Joe: Its salt makes it far denser than ordinary water, so there is more upward force holding a body at the surface.

User: why does a river delta at the dead sea keep retreating
Joe: The lake level has fallen over the decades as its water is used and evaporates, and the exposed shoreline has turned into salt flats and mud.

User: why does the persian gulf coast have so many islands and sandbars
Joe: Shallow water and a gently sloping floor let sediment build up in long low ridges, some of which break the surface and became islands.

User: why does the qatar peninsula stick out as a narrow finger
Joe: A rise in the seabed shallows the water along that line, and sand piles up on the shallowest part until it emerges above the sea.

User: why does africa split into two plates at the east african rift
Joe: The continent is being pulled apart by a spreading zone running through it, which is why volcanoes and deep lakes line that valley.

User: why does the rift valley have volcanoes but the nearby old rock does not
Joe: The floor of the rift is new and thin, and the magma reaches it far more easily than it could reach through the thick old crust around it.

User: why does south america's coastline have few islands in its east
Joe: A broad shallow shelf lies offshore, where sediment settles and builds rather than the deep water that favours volcanic island arcs.

User: why does chile have more earthquakes than almost anywhere
Joe: It lies along the boundary where one plate is forced under another, and those boundaries are where crust is most often broken and shifted.

User: why does peru's coast have so many towns at the same height
Joe: The flat coastal plain came from one great landslide event, and the towns on it share a similar elevation above the shore.

User: why is the amazon mouth so far from the andes
Joe: The plain between is the sediment fill of an ancient basin, and the river has had to build all the way across it before it could reach the sea.

User: why do the caribbean islands form an arc
Joe: They sit above a line where one plate slides beneath another, and the line itself curves, so the volcanoes and islands trace that curve.

User: why do the hawaiian islands form in a straight line
Joe: They are a chain of volcanoes over a single hot spot, and the plate has moved west over it, leaving each new island further along the chain.

User: why are the hawaiian islands older in one direction
Joe: The hot spot stays put while the plate carries each volcano away, so the ones that formed first are now the furthest from the active end.

User: why does the island of hawaii still have volcanoes while its neighbours do not
Joe: It is still sitting over the hot spot, so it is still being fed, while the others have been carried off it and have gone quiet.

User: why do remote islands end up with many unique species
Joe: The sea and distance cut off the gene flow that would otherwise even out a population, so each island's population evolves on its own.

User: why did animals on the galapagos evolve such different beaks
Joe: Different islands had different foods available, so birds with beaks suited to those foods left more offspring and the differences compounded.

User: why does the moai on easter island stand up without much visible support
Joe: They were cut lying down and then raised upright, which is why their backs are flat and they appear to be standing rather than carved upright.

User: why is easter island so hard to reach
Joe: It is a small volcanic speck far out in the pacific, so nothing grew there naturally and every person and every crop had to arrive by sea.

User: why does hawaii's rainforest have species found nowhere else
Joe: The islands grew up from nothing in open ocean, so plants arrived and evolved without a mainland to compare with.

User: why does the sicily fault line matter for volcanoes
Joe: The fault marks where the crust is splitting apart and one block is moving past the other, which gives hot magma a path to rise.

User: why does iceland have both volcanoes and glaciers
Joe: It sits on a spreading ridge where magma reaches the surface easily, while its high latitude keeps it cold enough for ice to build up on the volcanoes.

User: why does iceland's horseshoe shape matter
Joe: The island straddles the ridge, and the spreading on the ridge side pulls the two halves apart, which keeps the young crust and its volcanoes close to the middle.

User: why does the mid atlantic ridge run the length of the ocean
Joe: It marks the boundary where new ocean floor is pushed out from the mantle, and it sits in the middle of the ocean because that is where the spreading is.

User: why is the atlantic getting wider while the pacific narrows
Joe: The atlantic has more crust being created along its ridge than is being consumed at its edges, while the pacific loses more to subduction than it gains.

User: why does the ring of fire have so many volcanoes
Joe: It circles the pacific where one plate is being forced under another almost all the way round, and that process feeds magma to the surface.

User: why is there so little ocean floor older than about two hundred million years
Joe: Ocean floor is recycled continuously, so old floor has long since slid under a plate and melted, leaving only the youngest sections at the ridges.

User: why is the antarctic continental shelf unusually deep for a coast
Joe: The ice that once covered it scoured the seabed and loaded it down, so the continent's edge now sits far below the surface of the surrounding ocean.

User: why is australia the only landmass with no active plate boundary near it
Joe: It drifted to the middle of the plate it rides on, so no boundary sits close by and the land has been quiet for a very long time.

User: why is new zealand so geologically active
Joe: It straddles the boundary between the pacific plate and the antarctic one, where the two are still grinding past each other.

User: why does new zealand have so many earthquakes in its south island
Joe: The alpine fault runs right through that island and carries most of the strain between the two plates as it slips.

User: why do the cliffs of the south island rise sharply from the sea
Joe: The fault has lifted a range of mountains close to the coast, so the land has little gentle margin before it drops into deep water.

User: why does japan have so many islands strung out in a line
Joe: They are an island arc above a subduction boundary, and the boundary is a long curve rather than a single point.

User: why does japan have so many earthquakes
Joe: The islands sit on an active boundary where one plate is forced under another, and that motion never fully stops.

User: why are the japanese islands rich in hot springs
Joe: Subduction drives water into hot rock, and where that water returns it boils and rises to surface as springs.

User: why does the philippines get typhoons rather than hurricanes
Joe: It lies just west of the pacific, where the same rotating storms form but are called typhoons, so it is only a naming difference.

User: why does the indian ocean monsoon blow the other way halfway through the year
Joe: The asian landmass heats faster than the indian ocean in summer and cools faster in winter, and that reversal of the temperature contrast reverses the winds.

User: why does the somali coast have cold water and very hot air
Joe: Cold upwelling water cools the air above it, while hot dry air blowing over it makes evaporation strong and the sea surface very cold.

User: why does the arabian sea coast of india get its heaviest rains
Joe: The southwesterly monsoon blows warm moist ocean air onto the windward side of the western ghats, and the orographic lift makes it rain heavily.

User: why is the deccan plateau dry on its eastern side
Joe: The western ghats take the moisture out of the monsoon air, so by the time the wind crosses to the leeward side it has little rain left to give.

User: why are the himalayas higher than ever before
Joe: The collision that began tens of millions of years ago is still lifting the range, and the rise has not yet stopped.

User: why do the himalayas have no active volcanoes
Joe: India is butting against Asia rather than sliding beneath it, so the crust is being crumpled and lifted instead of diving down where magma could form.

User: why does the karakoram have the highest average peaks in the world
Joe: It rose during the same collision but in a section where the crust was already thick, so the mountains built higher there.

User: why do the andes contain salt flats along their length
Joe: Rain falls on the high ground and runs off into enclosed basins, where the water evaporates and leaves the salt it was carrying.

User: why does the altiplano sit high above the surrounding lowlands
Joe: It is a thick block of crust lifted by the subduction process and then held up like a plateau above the basins around it.

User: why is the amazon river's mouth so wide and muddy as it enters the sea
Joe: The river slows as it meets the sea and drops most of its fine sediment there, which builds the delta out into the ocean.

User: why does the yukon have permafrost and very long winters
Joe: It lies far inside the continent at a very high latitude, so winter is long and cold enough to keep the ground frozen year round.

User: why is siberia's coldest point far from the ocean rather than on the coast
Joe: Continental interiors cool far more than coasts in winter, and the coldest places sit where there is no sea and no wind to bring any warmth.

User: why does the great plains of north america run north to south
Joe: They follow the line where the flat interior sediment meets the older rock of the shield to the north and the mountains to the west.

User: why is there no surface water in the middle of the great plains
Joe: The ground there is porous and drains water downwards, so rivers that form at the edges flow away and the middle stays dry.

User: why do the appalachian mountains look worn down and rounded
Joe: They are among the oldest mountains on earth and have been eroded for hundreds of millions of years until much of them is gone.

User: why do the appalachians run in a curve rather than a straight line
Joe: They formed from a collision long before the atlantic opened, and the opening of that ocean bent their northern end around.

User: why is the mississippi basin so large and flat
Joe: It drains an enormous interior lowland covered in soft sediment, and rivers crossing it meander freely instead of cutting down.

User: why do the great plains have such sudden weather changes
Joe: Nothing blocks the flat land between the gulf and the canadian border, so cold air from the north sweeps straight through with little warning.

User: why do tornadoes form in the middle of the united states rather than along the coast
Joe: Warm moist air from the gulf meets cold dry air from the west there, and that collision is what produces the rotating storms that spawn them.

User: why is florida prone to hurricanes
Joe: It sticks out into the warm sea where storms form, and that shape makes it the first land in the way of anything tracking west from the atlantic.

User: why does the gulf coast get more damage from storm surge inland
Joe: The coast there is very flat and low, so a surge driven by wind and low pressure can push water far inland over a wide area.

User: why do new orleans floods so easily
Joe: Much of the city sits below the level of the river and the lake beside it, so it depends entirely on levees and pumps holding the water back.

User: why is the everglades so flat and so wet
Joe: It is a very low limestone plain with almost no gradient, so water spreads out thinly across a wide area instead of draining away.

User: why does the everglades have a slow-moving river of water at its surface
Joe: Sheet flow across such a flat plain moves very slowly, and the sawgrass ridges force the water to seep through rather than run.

User: why are the great lakes and the everglades both threatened by drainage
Joe: Both depend on water moving slowly and naturally, and once canals and drains send water out faster the wetlands and shore habitats change or vanish.

User: why is the antarctic so important to global climate despite being empty
Joe: Its ice reflects a large share of the sunlight that reaches it, and its surrounding ocean absorbs and stores heat at enormous scale.

User: why does the southern ocean circle the whole continent
Joe: It is the only ocean that flows right around the globe, and its cold circumpolar current keeps the far south connected all the way round.

User: why does that circumpolar current matter for climate everywhere
Joe: It allows the far southern ocean to exchange heat with the other oceans, and because it carries cold water it sets the baseline temperature of the southern hemisphere.

User: why are there no ice shelves in the arctic comparable to antarctica
Joe: The arctic is made of land surrounded by ocean, so the ice floats on the sea rather than resting on a continent with its own thick sheet.

User: why does floating sea ice matter less for sea level than land ice
Joe: Sea ice is already displacing water, so melting it barely changes the level, unlike ice on land whose melt adds fresh water to the ocean.

User: why does the arctic lose more sea ice than antarctica loses land ice
Joe: The arctic is warming fastest and the ice there is thinner and younger, so it breaks up far more easily once the ocean warms.

User: why do icebergs drift away from where they calve
Joe: Wind and currents carry them off while they are still fresh water, and they keep drifting until they reach shallower water and ground down.

User: why is the gulf stream sometimes called a river in the sea
Joe: Because it is a warm flow of water strong and narrow enough to be followed, and it carries far more heat than a river of the same size on land.

User: why does california have a mediterranean climate instead of a tropical one
Joe: It sits at a latitude where cold coastal water and seasonal shifting of the wind belts keep summer hot and dry rather than wet all year.

User: why does chile's central valley get almost no rain in summer
Joe: The subtropical high moves over it then and brings descending dry air, which is the same mechanism that dries the sahara.

User: why is patagonia so dry to the east of the andes
Joe: The andes take the moisture out of the westerlies, and by the time the air descends on the eastern side it is warm and dry.

User: why do patagonian glaciers calve into lakes rather than the sea
Joe: The glaciers grew out towards the coast as the land was flooded after the ice age, and now they end in freshwater lakes.

User: why do the andes run so close to the coast in chile
Joe: The subduction zone there is unusually narrow, so the land is squeezed and uplifted almost right at the edge of the continent.

User: why does the amazon basin have such enormous biodiversity
Joe: It is a very large warm wet area that has been stable for millions of years, which gave species time to specialise and multiply.

User: why do the tropical forests of congo and amazon hold similar species
Joe: Both were part of the same forest belt when the continents were joined, and the forests survived in both places as they separated.

User: why is madagascar's wildlife unlike that of nearby africa
Joe: It split from africa long ago and its species evolved in isolation, so lemurs and fossas are found nowhere else.

User: why does australia have unique animals while new guinea has different ones
Joe: New guinea's animals are closer to those of southeast asia, showing a separate history, while Australia's isolated position gave its own line of evolution.

User: why does new zealand have so few native land mammals
Joe: No land bridge ever carried mammals across, so its native land animals are birds, insects and one bat, all of which got there by flying.

User: why did the kiwi develop such long whiskers and nostrils
Joe: It is a ground-dwelling bird that hunts by touch in the dark, and a long flexible bill lets it feel for food it cannot see.

User: why does the kakapo look so awkward on the ground
Joe: It evolved to climb rather than run, so its heavy body and short wings suit life in trees far better than walking along the forest floor.

User: why does maori culture treat the kiwi as important
Joe: The kiwi features in their creation stories and represents the new land itself, so it carries a meaning well beyond being an animal.

User: why is maori writing carved rather than drawn
Joe: The ronge alphabet is carved into wooden meeting-house panels, and those carvings function as both record and decoration.

User: why do polynesian voyagers navigate by stars and swell patterns
Joe: Open ocean navigation had no landmarks to follow, so the pattern of stars and the direction of ocean swells were their map.

User: why was hawaii settled so late
Joe: The islands are small and far apart, and the canoe technology needed to cross that distance developed over many generations of voyaging.

User: why did the caribbean lose so many trees after settlement
Joe: Settlers cleared land for sugar and timber, and once the forest cover was gone the thin tropical soil washed away within decades.

User: why does jamaica have so little forest left
Joe: Sugar plantations and later bauxite mining removed most of the original forest, and the cleared slopes eroded faster than they could recover.

User: why is barbados relatively flat and green
Joe: It is a sedimentary island built from coral and sand rather than volcanic rock, and it has been cleared and replanted with sugar almost throughout.

User: why are the bahamas so shallow and so flat
Joe: They sit on a wide shallow platform made of limestone, and that shelf keeps deep ocean water away from the islands.

User: why is the andros island barrier reef so far from shore
Joe: The reef grows along the edge of a very shallow bank, and where the sea floor is very gradual the coral growth stays far out.

User: why does florida's reef run parallel to the coast rather than out at sea
Joe: The continental shelf there is narrow and shallow enough that the reef grows along its outer edge instead of hundreds of kilometres offshore.

User: why does the red sea stay warm and clear
Joe: It is almost enclosed by desert and receives very little river water, so there is little silt and evaporation keeps the water unusually salty.

User: why does the dead sea's salt come mostly from rock dissolved by its own water
Joe: Rain that falls on the surrounding rock picks up salts and carries them into the lake, and with no outlet the salts have nowhere to go.

User: why does the mediterranean have such high salinity
Joe: It is almost enclosed, it is hot so evaporation is strong, and few rivers carry fresh water in to replace what is lost.

User: why do the oceans hold most of the planet's heat
Joe: Water takes far more energy to warm than land does, so the sun's energy ends up in the ocean rather than in the ground.

User: why does the top few hundred metres of ocean matter most to the weather
Joe: That is the layer the air above exchanges heat and moisture with, so it controls storm fuel and rainfall far more than the deep ocean does.

User: why does the deep ocean stay almost uniformly cold
Joe: It receives heat only by slow sinking from above, and without sunlight there is nothing to warm it further.

User: why is the deep ocean rich in oxygen despite being far from the air
Joe: Oxygen dissolved in the surface layer sinks with cold dense water, and that sinking is how much of the deep ocean gets its oxygen.

User: why do some parts of the deep ocean have no oxygen at all
Joe: Where sinking organic material is broken down on the way down, the microbes use up the oxygen faster than any new supply arrives.

User: why does the mediterranean water form so deep and sink so far
Joe: It gets very salty and very cold at the surface from evaporation and cooling, and that combination makes it dense enough to sink.

User: why does that sinking feed deep sea ecosystems elsewhere
Joe: The dense water travels along the sea floor and mixes with water from other regions, carrying oxygen and nutrients into parts of the ocean that would otherwise starve.

User: why do red tides happen
Joe: Warm, still, nutrient-rich water lets one kind of algae multiply fast enough to colour the sea and release toxins.

User: why does a red tide sometimes kill the fish
Joe: The algae can release toxins, and as the bloom dies it strips oxygen from the water as it decomposes, which suffocates the fish.

User: why has the rat reached every island where people have settled
Joe: Rats travel with ships and cargo, and once ashore they find no natural predators, so they establish quickly wherever people are.

User: why does the mongoose do so much damage when released on an island
Joe: It was introduced to control rats but had no natural predators of its own, so it hunted native birds as readily as rats and drove many to extinction.

User: why does removing a predator upset the rest of the ecosystem
Joe: The predator held certain species in check, and removing it lets those species grow, which then affects everything further up and down the food web.

User: why do some islands have species that cannot fly
Joe: With no predators to chase them and limited ground to cover, flight stops paying for itself, so the island populations lose it.

User: why is the galapagos shark population so different from other reefs
Joe: Few predators there hunt sharks, so the young stay and grow into adults in numbers they would not reach elsewhere.

User: why does the andes create a barrier between two very different ecosystems
Joe: It blocks the moist air from the east, so the western slopes are dry and the eastern slopes are wet, and the two sides develop different species.

User: why is the amazon so difficult to farm
Joe: Its soil is thin and leached by heavy rain, so most of the nutrients are held in the living trees rather than in the ground beneath them.

User: why does clearing amazon rainforest often lead to poor soil
Joe: Clearing removes the roots that hold the soil together, so heavy rain washes the nutrient layer away and what remains is hard clay.

User: why do the amazon lowlands flood so widely
Joe: The river rises as the rains increase upstream, and the flat floodplain has nowhere to send the extra water, so it spreads across country.

User: why does the brazilian plateau have wide plateaus with steep edges
Joe: Uplift lifted old hard rock, and erosion cut back the edges into cliffs, which leaves flat tops at a similar height.

User: why is the amazon mouth's tidal range small
Joe: The river discharges so much water that it dominates the mouth, and that steady flow keeps seawater from backing far upstream.

User: why does the north amazon coast have strong tides
Joe: There the river's discharge is smaller relative to the ocean's tidal push, so the sea tide is felt much further inland.

User: why does it take a long time to travel up the amazon in a small boat
Joe: The river is wide and shallow with slow currents and many bends, so progress is slow and paddling against the current costs more than it gains.

User: why is the trans amazonian highway called that
Joe: It was built to cut across the basin east to west, linking the far edge of the forest to the coast rather than following the river.

User: why does the brazilian highway network avoid the forest interior
Joe: The ground is wet, soft and unroadworthy for most of the year, so settlement and roads concentrated on the drier coastal plateau.

User: why does brazil have a car industry along the coastal plateau
Joe: The plateau is temperate, well settled and close to ports, which gave the industry a large workforce and easy access to shipping.

User: why is the amazon not navigable all the way to the andes
Joe: Large boats can reach a point far inland, but above that the river becomes shallow and broken by rapids, which blocks through navigation.

User: why does the madeira river fall in rapids rather than flowing gently
Joe: It drops off the edge of the brazilian plateau onto the lowland, and that sudden fall creates the rapids that block it.

User: why does the sao paulo region have so many dams on its rivers
Joe: The plateau's rivers fall steeply and in narrow gorges, which makes them well suited to generating power and to holding water for the cities below.

User: why did the nile valley settle so early
Joe: The annual flood left rich silt on the floodplain, and that reliable surplus meant people could stay in one place and grow more than they needed.

User: why did egyptian scribes record everything on papyrus
Joe: Papyrus was made from a plant that grew along the river in great abundance, and it was light, portable and could carry fine writing.

User: why did egypt build enormous temples rather than palaces
Joe: The state spent its surplus on buildings that made the gods and the ruler's power visible, which mattered far more than royal comfort.

User: why did the pyramids need such precise alignment
Joe: Each side was set by sighting along stars, and that accuracy made the shape stable and made the sun and star alignments inside it work.

User: why did a pyramid tomb need to be hidden and protected
Joe: Because it held everything the king owned and a hidden tomb could not be robbed, so the entrance was sealed and the site kept out of sight.

User: why did egypt stop building pyramids
Joe: Later rulers found the huge monuments too costly and turned to temples and tombs cut into rock in the western desert instead.

User: why did mesopotamia flood so damagingly
Joe: Its rivers rise close together with flat land between, so a flood in one easily spilled into the next and ruined the whole system of fields.

User: why did mesopotamia need irrigation ditches
Joe: The rivers carried water unpredictably, so ditches carried it out to fields that the river itself could not reliably reach.

User: why did the tigris and euphrates have to be kept in separate channels
Joe: Their courses lay close together, and if one broke its banks it would flood the other's fields, so the two were kept in separate artificial channels.

User: why did hammurabi's code get carved on a stone pillar
Joe: Publicly visible and hard to remove, so it stood as a permanent statement of what the king had ordered and what would be punished.

User: why did hammurabi's code deal mainly with property and trade offences
Joe: The settled farming and trading society around him produced disputes about land, debt and wages, so the law settled those rather than rituals.

User: why did the first cities in mesopotamia have thick walls
Joe: They sat on open plain with no natural defence, so a heavy mud-brick wall with towers was the only protection against neighbours and raiders.

User: why did mesopotamian cities grow taller rather than wider
Joe: Land was valuable and hard to irrigate further, so building upwards used ground already made fertile.

User: why did the indus valley cities have grid streets
Joe: The layout was planned from the start around straight streets and standard block sizes, which suggests authority rather than piecemeal growth.

User: why did the indus cities have covered drains
Joe: Flood-prone ground and dense housing made water removal urgent, so the drains were built in with the streets rather than added later.

User: why do we know so little about who ruled the indus cities
Joe: The script has never been fully deciphered, and no royal tombs or readable records of rulers have been found there.

User: why did the indus script remain undeciphered
Joe: It survives on very few inscriptions and no bilingual text has been found, so there is nothing to compare it against.

User: why did the harappans build the great bath at mohenjo daro
Joe: Its brick waterproofing and careful drainage show a society that organised public works to a high standard for something likely ritual.

User: why did the harappan civilisation decline rather than being conquered
Joe: The cities emptied gradually and writing stopped, with no sign of a single invading army, so changed river courses and trade shifts are usually blamed.

User: why did the shang dynasty leave oracle bones
Joe: They were heat-cracked cattle bones inscribed with questions, and the cracks were read as answers so the king could decide what to do.

User: why did the chinese dynasties claim a mandate from heaven
Joe: Calling the ruler's right divine made rebellion a violation of the cosmic order, which discouraged challengers even when they had the stronger army.

User: why did the chinese empire last so long as one system
Joe: Its size and the cost of crossing it made it hard for any challenger to hold the whole region, so each dynasty absorbed rather than destroyed what came before.

User: why did the great wall not stop the steppe peoples
Joe: It guarded a fixed line, but those peoples moved around its ends and across the frozen rivers where the wall stopped, so it never fully closed the frontier.

User: why did the wall get built in so many different materials
Joe: Each stretch was rebuilt by whichever dynasty held that part, and each used whatever stone and labour the local terrain and budget allowed.

User: why did the terracotta army get buried
Joe: It was placed with the first emperor to guard his tomb in the afterlife, and it was found by accident when a farmer digging a well hit it.

User: why did the first emperor unify the script across china
Joe: He saw that officials from different regions could not read each other's writing, and one standard script made every order and record legible everywhere.

User: why did the first emperor move the capital near the frontier
Joe: Facing the steppe threat mattered more to him than facing the old central plains, so he put his court where the danger was.

User: why did the tang dynasty's capital attract artists from abroad
Joe: Its size and openness drew traders and craftsmen from many regions, and that mixture shaped what the art of the period looked like.

User: why did the tang dynasty collapse after a long growth
Joe: Regional military governors gained their own power and their own troops, and when central control weakened they took over the regions they were meant to hold.

User: why did the song dynasty value civil exams so much
Joe: It made office-holding depend on study rather than birth, and that let men from ordinary families rise, which built a large educated administration.

User: why did the song not invent gunpowder weapons that the tang could have used
Joe: The invention came late in the dynasty, and the following period was too unstable for the new weapons to be exploited before the dynasty fell.

User: why did the mongol empire spread so fast across asia
Joe: Their horse archers could move faster than any message ahead of them, and the political divisions between rivals let them take each one in turn.

User: why did the mongols use terror as a deliberate tactic
Joe: Massacre of a city that surrendered sent a message to the next, so a few killings bought the surrender of many others at a fraction of the cost.

User: why did the mongol conquest of europe stall
Joe: Their supply lines across the steppe became too long, and their horse-based tactics fitted the open plains far better than european mud and walled cities.

User: why did the ming treasure voyages go out so far
Joe: To project power and trade, and they sent enormous fleets that reached as far as the east coast of africa with thousands of ships.

User: why did china turn away from ocean voyages after the ming
Joe: The court decided land defence mattered more and the cost of the fleets was too high, so the voyages stopped and the fleet was dismantled.

User: why did the maya build pyramids in the middle of cities
Joe: The temples were the centre of political and religious life as well as monuments, so the city grew up around them rather than the towers being placed apart.

User: why did the maya land in the lowlands after arriving in central america
Joe: The lowlands had far better soils for their staple maize crop, so that is where they settled and built most of their cities.

User: why did the maya cities fight each other
Joe: Each city-state ruled its own territory, and the shifting of farmland and the demands for tribute gave them constant reason to contest borders.

User: why did the maya use long numbers instead of zeros
Joe: Their system was based on twenty, with a symbol per level, and a separate mark for zero appears only in dates.

User: why did the maya calendar restart counting after long periods
Joe: Their system marked the end of one long era and the start of another, and the dates people recorded referred to where they fell in that cycle.

User: why did the aztec empire build causeways into its capital
Joe: The city sat in a lake, so raised roads gave food and people a way in, and they also served as flood defences and aqueducts.

User: why did the aztecs use chinampa agriculture
Joe: Floating gardens of piled lake mud gave them several harvests a year from land that would otherwise have been under water.

User: why did the incas build roads across the andes
Joe: They ruled a long thin empire along the mountains, and roads let messengers, troops and supplies move between valleys weeks apart.

User: why did the inca empire have no writing system in the ordinary sense
Joe: They used knotted cords for records instead, which could hold counts and categories but not the speech they represented.

User: why did the incas not use wheels for transport
Joe: The steep mountain roads made wheeled vehicles useless, and the llamas they did use carried loads over the terrain far more effectively.

User: why did the inca roads run in straight lines
Joe: They connected points of administrative importance rather than following terrain, and the engineers built causeways and ramps to keep the grade manageable.

User: why did the incas not have writing when they had such sophisticated roads
Joe: Cords, counting and a strong oral system served their administrative needs, and they were surrounded by peoples who kept written records they chose not to adopt.

User: why were roman roads often straight even over hills
Joe: They were laid for marching troops and wagons, and a direct line between two points was faster to build and to move along than a contour route.

User: why did the roman empire fall in the west but not the east
Joe: The west faced constant pressure from migrating tribes while the east had a stronger economy and a more defensible position, so it survived for another thousand years.

User: why did the roman empire need bread from egypt
Joe: The capital's grain supply depended on shipping tax wheat from the Nile valley, and without it the city could not be fed.

User: why was bread the main thing the government handed out in rome
Joe: Keeping a million city dwellers fed and loyal was essential, and free grain was the cheapest way to keep them content.

User: why did roman concrete outlast so many later buildings
Joe: Their mix used volcanic ash that reacts with water and sets hard over centuries, so Roman marine concrete hardens further even when submerged.

User: why did the pantheon still standing after two thousand years not be built any taller
Joe: It was capped by a single enormous dome, and going higher on that width would have needed supports that were not available in the time.

User: why did roman law get codified so thoroughly
Joe: The empire spanned many customs and many peoples, and a single written law let governors and judges decide cases the same way across all of them.

User: why did the byzantine empire keep roman law and administration
Joe: After the west fell the eastern court still held the records, taxes and legal tradition, so it simply carried the system on under another name.

User: why did byzantium sit on that strait in particular
Joe: The strait links the black sea to the mediterranean and the bosphorus, and holding it controlled trade between them as well as the approach to constantinople itself.

User: why did the constantinople land walls work so well
Joe: They were triple walls of stone faced with fired brick, and the theodosian land wall is thought to have stopped an attack by the avars in the seventh century.

User: why did the byzantine empire last so long in a hostile neighbourhood
Joe: Constantinople was hard to take and rich enough to pay for soldiers and walls, so they could outlast sieges and buy off threats others faced too.

User: why did the arabs spread so quickly after the prophet's death
Joe: They were fighting one of the richest and most settled regions of the time, and the existing empires were weakened by long civil wars against each other.

User: why did the arab empire split into separate states
Joe: It was too large to govern from one place, and the distance between its provinces meant each region went its own way as trade routes shifted.

User: why did the arab scholars translate greek works into arabic
Joe: They wanted the learning, and the libraries at baghdad and cordoba were funded by rulers who saw it as a mark of a civilised state.

User: why did european learning recover so slowly after rome fell
Joe: Very few people could read the surviving texts, and the material for writing, and the institutions that kept records, had largely stopped being produced.

User: why did monasteries keep books through that period
Joe: They were the few places with someone able to read and copy, and their libraries preserved texts that were otherwise lost entirely.

User: why did the printing press change how fast ideas spread
Joe: Copying by hand took months per book, while printed books took days, so the same text could be read by far more people in far more places.

User: why did the printing press help reform more than it helped rulers
Joe: Anyone with money could print, so arguments that once stayed in one place circulated widely and could not be quietly suppressed.

User: why did the printing press not print in chinese first
Joe: Woodblock printing had existed there for centuries and was already widely used, but separate character blocks suited a script that works differently from an alphabet.

User: why did the reformation break away from rome
Joe: Disputes over indulgences and the authority of scripture had built up, and printing let the arguments spread faster than the church could answer them.

User: why did the reformation cause so much war
Joe: Religious authority was tied to political power, so changing the church meant changing who ruled, and the disputes became armed conflicts between states.

User: why did the council of trent take so long
Joe: The reform arguments were tangled up with wars and politics between states, so the council kept being interrupted and had to be resumed.

User: why did the scientific revolution follow the printing press so closely
Joe: Printed diagrams and arguments could be compared across countries, and a finding no one had to see in person could still be checked and repeated.

User: why did copernicus hesitate to publish his heliocentric model
Joe: It contradicted what the church taught and what most scholars accepted, and he waited years before letting even a small part of it appear in print.

User: why did galileo's telescope settle so many arguments at once
Joe: It showed craters on the moon, moons around jupiter and moons too faint for the eye, so observations spoke louder than any authority could argue.

User: why did galileo run into trouble with the church
Joe: He used his observations to argue for ideas the church had ruled out, and the authorities who handled it treated that as grounds for discipline.

User: why did galileo write his last work as a dialogue with two speakers
Joe: Presenting both sides through characters let the arguments be published while the writer kept enough distance to avoid a direct prosecution.

User: why did the church accept some astronomy centuries later
Joe: Once the observations could not be explained any other way, keeping the old position became indefensible, so the change came from evidence rather than argument alone.

User: why did newton's law of gravity pull the moon into orbit rather than let it fly off
Joe: The moon is constantly falling, but it also travels sideways fast enough that the fall curves the path round the earth instead of pulling it straight down.

User: why did the royal society formalise experiments so carefully
Joe: Its members distrusted claims based on one person's observation, so repeatable tests with recorded results became the standard for accepting a finding.

User: why did the industrial revolution start in britain
Joe: It had coal, iron, capital, a skilled workforce and reliable institutions for backing ventures, and the other conditions were harder to gather in one place elsewhere.

User: why did coal matter more than wood for industry
Joe: Wood ran out faster and gave less heat, while coal burned hotter, was far more abundant and could be pumped from deep mines.

User: why did steam engines improve so much in britain
Joe: Newcomen and then watt's separate condenser cut the fuel needed per stroke, and that made pumping water out of mines profitable.

User: why did the steam engine change where factories could be
Joe: It no longer needed to sit beside fast-flowing water, so mills could go into towns where the workers and the market already were.

User: why did factories concentrate workers in cities
Joe: The machines were expensive and best run at full capacity with many people, so the work gathered into big buildings in one place.

User: why did the railways cause such a rush to build lines everywhere
Joe: They cut travel times so dramatically that cities and industries fought for connections, and the network spread far faster than anyone planned.

User: why did railway lines often go in straight routes across country
Joe: Gradients decide what a train can pull, so engineers chose the gentlest line they could rather than the shortest one.

User: why did railway timetables matter more than the rails themselves
Joe: A train following another on the same line must keep clear of it, so the whole system depends on coordinating movements between many trains.

User: why did the telegraph change news before it changed travel
Joe: It carried a message between distant cities in minutes instead of weeks by ship, so the news became current rather than history.

User: why did steamships overtake sailing ships
Joe: They did not depend on wind, so they could sail in any direction on schedule, and their engines made them independent of favourable winds entirely.

User: why did the refrigerated ship change the food trade
Joe: Refrigeration let meat and fruit be shipped from distant places, so food could be produced where it grew best and sold where it was wanted.

User: why did the telegraph cause problems for the stock market
Joe: Prices travelled faster than people could act on them, and traders had to rely on the telegraph to keep ahead of each other.

User: why did the british empire's naval power grow so consistently
Joe: Its ships could be built from abundant timber, its harbours were well placed, and its sailors stayed experienced because the navy was permanently employed.

User: why did britain colonise india partly by trading posts
Joe: East India Company traders first built fortified factories for trade, and those holdings turned into territory as rival European powers attacked them.

User: why did the east india company end up ruling rather than just trading
Joe: Competing with armed local rulers meant keeping private armies, and those forces were able to conquer neighbouring territory whenever trade was threatened.

User: why did the sepoy mutiny become a war of independence
Joe: Soldiers had been treated as one group and trusted as another, and those contradictions turned a mutiny into a much wider rising.

User: why did the british withdraw from india after that war
Joe: Governing such a large and distant territory was proving far more expensive than it earned, and business there was starting to be run locally.

User: why did the americans revolt over taxation without paying the taxes
Joe: The dispute was over the principle that parliament had no right to tax them without representation, and that argument mattered more than the amount.

User: why did the declaration of independence list grievances rather than one reason
Joe: Listing many abuses showed that the break was about a whole pattern of authority rather than a single dispute that could still have been settled.

User: why did the american revolution succeed against a much stronger army
Joe: The colonists fought on their own ground, knew the terrain, and were supported by a long supply chain of their own which the British could not match at that distance.

User: why did the french revolution start with a bread shortage
Joe: Bad harvests had pushed the price of bread so high that it hit the poorest hardest, and bread took most poor families' income, so hunger became political.

User: why did the french revolution remove the monarchy so quickly
Joe: The king had lost trust over the invasion of the royal palace, and once the crowd turned on him in paris the political authority collapsed almost at once.

User: why did the french revolution turn so violent
Joe: Food shortages, political fear and the threat of foreign invasion all raised the stakes, and the government responded to pressure with harsher measures.

User: why did napoleon's armies move so far so fast
Joe: His supply system was efficient, he moved with the season, and his dispatches reached his marshals far quicker than an army of the time could.

User: why did napoleon lose at waterloo
Joe: He had spent six months locked in a long stalemate with the allies in the north, and by the time he reached them his army was worn down.

User: why did the industrial revolution make britain the richest country
Joe: Its factories produced far more goods for the same labour, and that output meant it could afford far more imports than any other nation.

User: why did britain's wealth lead to arguments about free trade
Joe: Goods made cheaply at home were easy to sell abroad, and removing tariffs widened the market while cheaper imports became available at home too.

User: why did the corn laws try to protect farmers
Joe: Cheap imported grain had pushed farm prices down, and the laws raised them to keep British farming profitable.

User: why did repealing the corn laws cause a political crisis
Joe: The landed interest had dominated politics for decades and saw cheap food as a threat to their income, so the vote split the political class badly.

User: why did the irish potato famine happen when it did
Joe: A water mould that had arrived with potatoes from america spread through the fields, and the blight destroyed the crop two years running.

User: why did the irish famine kill so many people
Joe: The blight and a later failure of grain meant no food, and the export of Irish food continued through the worst of the famine because the market did not stop.

User: why did the irish famine force so many to emigrate
Joe: Work was gone and survival was not possible, so whole families left for britain and america where jobs were and land was not.

User: why did the crimean war come about over the holy places
Joe: Russia claimed a right to protect orthodox Christians in the region, and the french claim to protect catholic ones turned a local dispute into a european war.

User: why did the crimean war become famous for its conditions
Joe: The allied armies reached the field weeks before a supply system existed, and the deaths from disease at camp were far more than the deaths in battle.

User: why did lincoln's home address matter so much
Joe: It framed the war as about the survival of the country itself, and that gave the north something to hold onto through a long and bloody fight.

User: why did the american civil war change because of the railways
Joe: Both sides could move troops far faster over rail than on foot, and the north's far longer network gave it a lasting advantage in movement.

User: why did the north have more factories and the south more slaves
Joe: The north had invested in industry and immigration while the south's wealth sat in land and enslaved labour, so each built a different kind of state.

User: why did the emancipation proclamation not free every enslaved person at once
Joe: It applied only to areas still in rebellion, so those who had already been captured or abandoned by the confederacy became free under it earlier.

User: why did the confederacy run out of metal for weapons
Joe: Its rail network was destroyed and its foundries lost early, so it relied on imports that the northern blockade cut off as the war went on.

User: why did the abolition of slavery take so long in british india
Joe: The trade continued because it was profitable, and reform had to overcome resistance from the traders, the government and the plantation interests.

User: why did darwin's theory cause such a storm when it was published
Joe: It said humans had descended from other animals, which challenged the idea that people were set apart by creation, and that was a religious question as much as a scientific one.

User: why did darwin delay publishing his work for years
Joe: He was afraid of the reaction and of being scooped, and he spent years collecting more evidence before he finally brought out the book.

User: why did the debate over darwin turn on how evolution was driven
Joe: The mechanism mattered as much as the fact of change, and the argument between gradual selection and sudden jumps shaped everything after.

User: why did the theory of evolution by natural selection need so much evidence
Joe: It makes small predictions across many separate cases, and it needed decades of observed variation, breeding results and fossil finds to hold together.

User: why did the french school of the late nineteenth century become the centre of physics
Joe: France built a research system that paid scientists to work without teaching duties, and that freed them to attack open problems full time.

User: why did the wartime rise of radar change how wars were fought
Joe: It let fighters find targets through cloud and darkness, which removed one of the biggest advantages air power had relied on.

User: why did the penicillin discovery take so long to become useful
Joe: Producing the drug at scale and purifying it enough to use safely took years of work after the antibiotic effect was first noticed.

User: why did penicillin work so dramatically against infection
Joe: It stopped bacteria building their cell walls, and without a rigid wall to hold its shape the bacterial cell bursts.

User: why did germ theory meet such resistance from doctors
Joe: Many doctors had practised for decades without it, and some had reasons to doubt it, while the idea implied their patients had died through negligence.

User: why did statistics become central to medicine
Joe: Treatments have to be compared against a real chance of recovery to tell whether they help, and that comparison needs a careful count of outcomes.

User: why did the discovery of insulin change diabetes from fatal to manageable
Joe: It replaced the hormone the body could not make, and without it the blood sugar stayed so high that the body quickly wore out.

User: why did the discovery of insulin need two names on the paper
Joe: Two groups claimed the work almost simultaneously, and publishing under both names avoided a long dispute over who had got there first.

User: why did vaccination against smallpox work before anyone knew about germs
Joe: It relied on the observation that people who survived a mild infection did not catch it again, and that was enough to give protection.

User: why did the world health organisation aim to eradicate smallpox
Joe: It was a human-only disease with a stable virus and an effective vaccine, so stopping transmission meant the virus had nowhere left to go.

User: why was smallpox the first disease successfully eradicated
Joe: No animal reservoir kept the virus going, the vaccine was effective, and a distinct rash made cases easy to identify and report.

User: why did the first world war start in the Balkans
Joe: The region had competing nationalist claims and empires with no settled borders, and a single incident there set off the alliances that drew in everyone else.

User: why did alliances turn a local war into a world war
Joe: Each power had promised to back another, so one state's mobilisation pulled in everyone bound to it and the fighting grew to cover whole continents.

User: why did the trenches on the western front last so long
Joe: Modern weapons could destroy an attack before it crossed open ground, so the only way to advance was to dig in and move underground.

User: why did artillery become more destructive in the war than in previous ones
Joe: Shells could now burst on impact and spray fragments in every direction, and heavier guns brought them within reach of any position on the map.

User: why did naval power decide the outcome of the world wars
Joe: Britain relied on shipping to bring in food and war material, so the fight over that shipping decided who could keep fighting at all.

User: why did submarine warfare force a blockade in the second world war
Joe: Attacking ships without warning looked like an attack on civilians, so the united states agreed to stop it and joined the war instead.

User: why did the second world war start in europe
Joe: Germany's expansion into neighbouring countries went unchallenged for years, and when it reached poland the guarantees given to that country brought in the allies.

User: why did the allies invade normandy instead of across the english channel at calais
Joe: Calais was defended more heavily and was the obvious target, so the invasion was aimed at the less defended coast and the deception was meant to keep the reserves in the wrong place.

User: why did the bomb change the war so fast
Joe: It destroyed a city in a single strike, and the knowledge that more such weapons existed convinced japan that continuing was hopeless.

User: why did the cold war happen between countries that had been allies
Joe: Both sides emerged from the war needing security and each feared the other's, and their rival ideologies made that fear worse rather than better.

User: why did the united nations replace the league of nations
Joe: The league had no armed force and could not stop the war that followed, and the major victors wanted an organisation they themselves could not be excluded from.

User: why did the security council have only five permanent members
Joe: Those five had borne the cost of setting the organisation up, and they wanted a body no small group could be outvoted on.

User: why did the united nations need to act during the cold war
Joe: With the rival blocs unable to fight directly, struggles for influence were fought through it, and that gave the organisation a job that could not simply be ignored.

User: why did the european union grow out of the european coal and steel community
Joe: It began by pooling two industries that were easier to share than armies or foreign policy, and the economic tie it created made wider union easier to accept.

User: why did the maastricht treaty come after decades of gradual steps
Joe: Each stage agreed more integration, so by the time a single treaty set out the whole structure it was mostly putting existing practice into one legal form.

User: why does the european union face constant pressure over borders
Joe: It is a union of states with very different histories, and the free movement of people means the idea of a shared border has to work for all of them.

User: why did the declaration of human rights get written after the second world war
Joe: The atrocities of that war showed what states had done to their own people, and rights were set out as things no government could take away.

User: why did the indian independence movement succeed eventually
Joe: Repeated campaigns, the war and a change in the economic cost of holding india made continued rule more expensive than letting it go.

User: why did gandhi's campaigns work where armed revolt had failed
Joe: They made the issue impossible to ignore and hard to describe as an outside threat, so the government could not simply crush it as an insurgency.

User: why was india partitioned when it was independent
Joe: The two communities had been brought into separate electorates under separate leadership, so the transfer of power had to be planned along that line.

User: why did partition cause such enormous violence
Joe: Communities that had lived side by side had been divided by years of political division, and the move happened at short notice with no time for people to prepare.

User: why did china's revolution come after the war with japan rather than during it
Joe: The occupation had weakened the japanese state and the chinese state so badly that when the war ended the gap between them was very wide.

User: why did mao's campaigns to raise farm output fail
Joe: Compulsory quotas took grain away from the farmers at harvest, so they planted less the following year, and the harvest fell rather than rose.

User: why did china begin economic reforms under a different leader
Joe: The communes were clearly not producing the growth the leaders wanted, and the change came when a new leadership decided to try something else.

User: why did the shanghai free market succeed so quickly
Joe: It had a huge population of skilled low-cost labour and a real industrial base already in place, so investment flowed in as soon as it was allowed.

User: why does a special economic zone work differently from reform everywhere at once
Joe: Testing the change in one place limits the damage if it fails, and its success then makes the change easier to accept in the rest of the country.

User: why did the korean war end in stalemate rather than victory for either side
Joe: Both sides had built up enough forces that neither could push the other back, and neither was willing to accept a result that meant stepping back further.

User: why did the vietnam war last so long
Joe: The north was willing to keep sending forces south, and the south lacked the numbers to hold every position, so control kept shifting back and forth.

User: why did the cuban missile crisis bring both sides to the edge
Joe: Neither side knew what the other was about to do, and both had already agreed not to back down in principle, so a small misjudgement could have started a war.

User: why did the crisis end without a war
Joe: One side offered a quiet withdrawal of its missiles in return for a withdrawal of the other's, and that trade kept both sides out of it.

User: why did the berlin wall become a symbol rather than just a border
Joe: It was built overnight and stayed up for decades, so it became the visible proof that a whole population was divided from itself.

User: why did the wall come down when it did
Joe: Reform in the east, mass departures through it, and a struggling economy combined so that the pressure could no longer be held without force.

User: why did the soviet union break apart
Joe: Its economy became too rigid to adjust, its republics grew more self-reliant, and the political loosening in the west pulled the whole thing apart.

User: why did the united states and britain invade iraq in two different ways
Joe: The 1991 war was a limited one over united nations action, while the 2003 one was justified on a wider basis and was opposed by many allies.

User: why did the middle east remain unstable after the wars
Joe: The conflicts fought over borders and influence in the region, and resolving any one of them without settling the others was never going to be simple.

User: why does the indian subcontinent have such a complicated political map
Joe: Because two religions, one arriving by sea and one by land, grew in the same area at the same time, and the line drawn between them cut through mixed populations.

User: why did the british leave a partition rather than a single independent india
Joe: The political leaders of the two communities would not accept one another's majority in a united state, so power had to be divided between them.

User: why did the bengal famine happen during the second world war
Joe: Rice was shipped out and the boats then took food away from the coast, and the loss of Burmese rice plus inflation left the population unable to buy enough.

User: why was the irish famine so much less reported than the bengal one
Joe: The numbers relative to the population were different, and the press attention of the day followed events where the pressure for relief came fastest.

User: why did the abolitionist movement in britain grow so strongly
Joe: Enslaved people kept escaping and publicising their condition, and a growing religious movement made ending the trade a duty for many.

User: why did the americans take so long to abolish slavery
Joe: The south's entire economy and political power rested on it, and the compensation offered for freedom made many northerners hesitate about ending it outright.

User: why did the civil rights movement in america use nonviolent action
Joe: Winning force with force was impossible, but showing a crowd and a violent reaction to it was visible enough to change public opinion and force political action.

User: why did that movement succeed only after decades
Joe: The legal change had to be argued in public and in courts, and each small victory built the case for a larger one.

User: why has the united states never ratified a single world human rights treaty
Joe: The treaty bodies created by them were given no power to overrule national law, and several clauses could be read as applying to issues inside the country itself.

User: why did the suffragists argue that women's votes would change politics
Joe: They believed women being counted in elections would force governments to care about homes, children and health, not only about industry and war.

User: why did women's voting rights arrive in some countries far earlier than others
Joe: It depended on local political calculations, so it came early where the numbers worked out and late where giving women the vote threatened the dominant group.

User: why did the decolonisation of africa happen so suddenly after the war
Joe: European countries could no longer afford to hold their colonies, and the idea of self-determination they had used against each other became hard to refuse abroad.

User: why did many former colonies struggle with borders drawn by outsiders
Joe: The boundaries were made along lines convenient to the colonial power, and they cut across groups who had never been one political unit before.

User: why did ghana's independence become a model for others
Joe: It was achieved through organised political action rather than war, and it showed that a negotiated transfer was possible.

User: why did algeria's independence take a violent war
Joe: A large settler population made the settlers' position part of the political settlement, so a transfer of power had to involve a change that could not be agreed.

User: why did the congolese independence produce chaos soon after
Joe: Power went to leaders with narrow regional support and the resources to buy it, which left the state with an army answerable to a faction rather than a nation.

User: why did the nigerian civil war last so long
Joe: The eastern region had formed its own army after the split, and the federal side had to defeat a conventional military before any settlement could be reached.

User: why did the portuguese empire stay until the nineteen seventies
Joe: Portugal was small and poor, and the wars there tied down much of its own military and budget while the world economy was moving away from empire.

User: why did the somaliland famine of the nineteen nineties happen
Joe: Several years of failure of the short rains cut the harvest, and the collapse of the state meant there was no authority left to buy or import food.

User: why does sudan split into two countries rather than one
Joe: The north and south had been left separate by a civil war, and the boundary between them had become so clearly drawn that neither would share a state.

User: why has the palestinian question proved so hard to resolve
Joe: It involves land with deep religious significance, two populations who both claim a national right to it, and outside states with their own reasons for involvement.

User: why did the armistice of nineteen forty eight leave jerusalem divided
Joe: The line it drew ran through the middle of the city, and neither side would give up any of the ground it had taken, so it stayed between them.

User: why did the olympic games restart after a long gap
Joe: Because the games had been used as a political instrument twice before, and hosting them was a way of showing a state as peaceful and normal.

User: why did the games move around the world rather than staying in one country
Joe: Different countries paid for them and expected them to return, so a rotating host turned the event into a shared institution.

User: why are the modern games commercial as well as athletic
Joe: Broadcast rights and sponsorship pay for the event, and the need for that money changed how the games are organised and presented.

User: why did the world cup affect a country so much every four years
Joe: Qualifying runs for months and the tournament itself reaches a huge audience, so national pride and attention concentrate on it at that time of year.

User: why did the modern olympics grow so large
Joe: Adding events and letting more countries and athletes in made it more representative, and that growth pulled in more money and more coverage.

User: why do time zones exist at all
Joe: The earth turns once in twenty four hours, so different longitudes meet the sun at different times, and each country has chosen where to draw the line between them.

User: why does the date line sit near the middle of the pacific
Joe: That is the far side of the world from the main landmasses, so putting the change there keeps most inhabited places on roughly the same calendar day.

User: why did some countries adopt the date line only recently
Joe: They had followed a different local calendar because of a power that ruled them, and they changed when they became independent and set their own rules.

User: why does the british empire spread across so many time zones
Joe: It covered territories in every continent, and each kept the local time while the empire ran on a single clock in london.

User: why did railways force standard time on the world
Joe: A shared clock in each town was essential to running trains, and once that existed, agreeing a standard between neighbouring towns came next.

User: why did the world adopt a calendar from a single place
