Joe: It spread with the empire that used it, and because so many countries later kept the same calendar, it became the one used almost everywhere.

User: why is the week not tied to the moon any more
Joe: The seven-day cycle came from ancient tradition, and it stayed in use as the calendar became more precise while the lunar month changed constantly.

User: why do the dates of easter and other feasts shift each year
Joe: They are tied to lunar dates that fall at a different point in the solar year, and the rule that fixes them keeps shifting them around.

User: why did the nineteenth century see the fastest spread of railways
Joe: Steel rails, cheaper construction and finance all improved at once, so lines could be built faster, longer and further than before.

User: why is so much of nineteenth century history about empire
Joe: Industrial powers had more goods than their home markets could absorb, and overseas markets solved that while giving raw materials at low cost.

User: why did the telegraph undermine the old way of doing business
Joe: It made distance much less costly to cross, and businesses that had relied on distance to protect them found themselves competing in the same market.

User: why did the telephone grow so much faster after the exchanges were built
Joe: Switchboards let any phone reach any other, and that usefulness made a network worth joining rather than a curiosity.

User: why does a bank need a reserve of cash
Joe: Because people expect to withdraw on demand, and a bank that could not pay would fail at once even if its loans were perfectly safe.

User: why did the great depression spread from finance into industry
Joe: When banks failed, companies lost the credit they needed to buy materials, so production fell, and unemployed people then bought less, which fell again.

User: why did governments step in to break up monopolies
Joe: A firm that sets prices alone can keep them high and stall investment, and governments argued that competition served the public better.

User: why did the depression last so long
Joe: Banks and businesses stayed damaged, and with credit scarce and people afraid to spend, recovery had little to work with.

User: why did the new deal build so many public works
Joe: They gave work to the unemployed and improved the country at the same time, and the projects were visible enough to earn public support.

User: why did the welfare state grow after the second world war
Joe: The war showed what governments could do, and a generation expected that building would not simply stop when the fighting did.

User: why did the postwar period see so much growth
Joe: Reconstruction, new technology and a large workforce that had not been destroyed all fed into demand that kept rising for years.

User: why did postwar growth slow later on
Joe: The easy catch-up had been completed, and slower growth in the labour force and in new investment then became hard to offset.

User: why did japan recover from the war so quickly
Joe: Its factories and cities were destroyed but its skills, education and industrial base were not, and outside aid helped it rebuild fast.

User: why did west germany recover so quickly too
Joe: It received large aid, had an educated workforce, and benefited from being at the centre of the european market opening up.

User: why did the european union grow trade but restrict movement for decades
Joe: Goods, money and jobs moved easily while people were harder to admit, because the political cost of allowing people to move was much greater.

User: why did the abolition of slavery take different forms in different countries
Joe: Each had its own economy built on enslaved labour and its own political balance, so the pressure for change and the shape of it differed sharply.

User: why did it take a world war to create the united nations
Joe: Because the league had failed to prevent the last one, and the victors wanted an organisation with the power and the membership to stop the next.

User: why did the united nations keep a general assembly despite the veto
Joe: Every country has one vote there, and that matters to the many states with no other forum in which they are all equal.

User: why did the european union grow in membership faster after the cold war
Joe: Countries that had been behind an iron curtain could now join, and the union had already proved it worked for the members it had.

User: why did russia struggle to become a market economy
Joe: A command economy had no experience of price signals or private ownership, and institutions for enforcing contracts had to be built from nothing.

User: why did russia's transition cause such hardship
Joe: State support disappeared before private enterprise had formed, so factories closed and people lost the support they had relied on.

User: why did india liberalise its economy in the nineteen nineties
Joe: Its own experience showed that the controls had held growth back, and it faced the same slowdown the other economies had just come through.

User: why did china's reform begin in agriculture
Joe: Farming was the largest share of the population, and letting households farm for themselves raised output quickly with less disruption than industry reform.

User: why did the mexican economy change so much after joining trade agreements
Joe: Its industries could now sell to a much larger market, and that gave foreign firms a reason to build there rather than export from elsewhere.

User: why did the internet spread so fast after the nineteen nineties
Joe: It needed the personal computer and the telephone network that were already in most homes, so the infrastructure was in place to carry it.

User: why did email become so much cheaper than the post
Joe: Sending one costs almost nothing once a connection exists, and that difference in cost is why it took over so much routine business communication.

User: why did the world wide web change how people found things
Joe: Instead of searching known sources it let anyone publish and be found, so the amount of material available grew far faster than it could be organised.

User: why does search ranking decide which sites people see
Joe: There are far more results than anyone will read, so ordering them decides what is actually reached, and that makes ranking valuable.

User: why did social media change how news spreads
Joe: People share what they already read with people they know, so a story travels through social connections rather than through an editor.

User: why does the same speed of spread make it hard to check a claim first
Joe: A false claim can reach more people in an hour than a correction manages in a week, because the correction has to travel the same network twice.

User: why did the smartphone become the main computer for so many people
Joe: It combined a camera, a map, a phone and internet access in one device, which is most of what the tasks in daily life need.

User: why do people check their phone so often
Joe: Small bursts of checking give a quick reward, and the habit forms from repeating a simple action that is always available.

User: why is it hard to switch off notifications
Joe: Each alert asks for a moment of attention, and turning them off means accepting that someone may be waiting without you knowing.

User: why has the pace of work increased rather than decreased
Joe: Being contactable at all times makes stopping look like falling behind, and that expectation presses on every hour of the day.

User: why do some people work better with music on
Joe: Familiar music can cover distracting noise and give the hands a steady rhythm, so attention on the task itself may improve.

User: why does a long meeting lose people halfway through
Joe: Attention falls off without a change of activity, and a person can only follow an argument for long without something to hold onto.

User: why does a short agenda beat a long one
Joe: It names the decisions to be made, which lets everyone arrive prepared, and preparation is what makes a meeting run to time.

User: why do written proposals beat discussing everything from scratch
Joe: A written version can be read quietly and disagreed with on paper, and that is far less awkward than arguing it out in front of a room.

User: why do some workplaces do well without offices at all
Joe: When the work is judged on what is produced rather than when it is produced, location stops mattering and the cost of a building disappears.

User: why has remote work changed where people live
Joe: People no longer need to be near an office, so they can live where they have space, cheaper rent, or the climate they prefer.

User: why has that put pressure on city centres
Joe: The daytime population that supported shops, transport and cafes has thinned, and the money that followed it has moved with them.

User: why did the twentieth century see such a long peace between the major powers in europe
Joe: The damage of the two world wars and the nuclear threat made starting another one too costly for both sides, so deterrence held.

User: why did that deterrence work for so long
Joe: Both sides had enough weapons to destroy each other, and that made the threat credible enough that neither side wanted to be first to test it.

User: why did the cold war not become a hot war over cuba
Joe: The leader who did not back down was willing to trade rather than escalate, and that willingness alone stopped it from becoming a war.

User: why do arms races cost a country even when nobody fights
Joe: The weapons have to be built, stored and replaced continuously, and much of that spending produces nothing that can be used in daily life.

User: why is history mostly about trade rather than war
Joe: Because trade raises the value of keeping peace, while war destroys what both sides would rather be exchanging, so the incentive to keep talking usually wins.

User: why do borders change so rarely compared with how often people fight
Joe: Every border change costs more than it usually gains, and by the time a war ends the cost has usually convinced both sides to accept what they now hold.

User: why do rivers so often form borders
Joe: A river is a clear line that both sides can see from their own side of it, which makes it a far more practical boundary than anything people have to describe.

User: why do some borders run down the middle of a mountain range
Joe: The ridge is continuous and hard to cross, so it gives a natural line that both sides can follow without needing to survey it.

User: why is the border between ireland and northern ireland still contested
Joe: The island of ireland was one country until the war that ended in a partition, and the border it produced was drawn by that war rather than agreed.

User: why do many countries have enclaves inside another
Joe: Because borders were drawn and then redrawn without regard to where people already lived, so a group that moved or stayed behind ended up surrounded.

User: why is a country shaped like a ring hard to govern
Joe: The land inside the ring is cut off from the main territory, so reaching it needs to cross someone else's land or water.

User: why does panama matter so much for global trade
Joe: It cuts the narrowest part of central america, so the canal there lets ships move between two oceans without going the long way round south america.

User: why was the panama canal built at all
Joe: The long route round the southern tip added thousands of kilometres to every voyage, which cost more than digging through the isthmus.

User: why did the canal have locks
Joe: The isthmus is above sea level, so ships have to be lifted into a raised lake and lowered again the other side.

User: why did the canal narrow so much in recent decades
Joe: Its dimensions were set when it opened and it has never been widened, so it cannot take the largest ships now built.

User: why does the suez canal matter for the price of oil
Joe: It is the short route between the red sea and the mediterranean for a large share of oil leaving the middle east, so closing it raises costs quickly.

User: why does gibraltar matter so much geographically
Joe: It is the narrow gap between spain and morocco that connects the atlantic to the mediterranean, and every ship using it must pass through.

User: why did britain want to keep gibraltar
Joe: It controls the entrance to the mediterranean and sits on the sea route to india and onwards, which was worth far more than the land itself.

User: why do the chagos islands matter to the indian ocean
Joe: They sit in the middle of the ocean, and controlling them gives a position close to the shipping lanes between the middle east, africa and asia.

User: why does the malacca strait carry so much trade
Joe: It is the shortest sea route between the indian ocean and the pacific, so almost all shipping between them passes through it.

User: why do hong kong and singapore have such large ports
Joe: They sit right where goods from china and southeast asia meet the sea routes, and they offer the handling and finance that shipping needs.

User: why is the english channel so narrow yet so important
Joe: It is the gap between england and france, and the shortest crossing means heavy traffic between two of europe's busiest coasts.

User: why is the bosphorus strait under a bridge
Joe: The two continents either side are so close that a bridge could span them, and the strait's position between the black sea and the mediterranean makes it busy.

User: why does a small island like singapore have so much influence
Joe: Because it sits at that crossing point, and controlling it means being at the centre of the trade rather than at the edge of it.

User: why did the united states build the panama canal and then give it back
Joe: It saw the canal as its own security link, and when a distant canal run by another country became less useful to it, control became a liability rather than an asset.

User: why is hong kong's status such a continuing subject
Joe: It was administered under a treaty that was due to expire, and what replaces it has never been settled by agreement between the two sides involved.

User: why did macau follow a different path from hong kong
Joe: It stayed in portuguese hands for longer and changed hands by negotiation rather than by a long military occupation.

User: why does taiwan's status remain unresolved
Joe: It was governed by the losing side in a civil war and has been self-governing ever since, with the other side never having accepted that arrangement.

User: why did singapore separate from malaysia after a short time together
Joe: The two governments disagreed on quite different things, including the balance between ethnic groups and how the economy should be run.

User: why did singapore's government restrict things so tightly
Joe: It was a small city-state with no resources and serious ethnic tension, and the leaders judged that tight control was what kept it stable and prosperous.

User: why is singapore so clean compared with its neighbours
Joe: Fines for littering are severe and enforcement is consistent, so the street has to be kept clear to avoid the penalty.

User: why does singapore keep such strict rules about what is allowed
Joe: The leaders have treated social order as the condition that makes a small state viable, and they judge the strictness worth what it costs ordinary freedoms.

User: why is japan's economy so different from most western ones
Joe: It has fewer natural resources and a large, skilled, relatively uniform workforce, and its firms have tended to cooperate with each other rather than compete away profits.

User: why did japan's post-war export economy grow so fast
Joe: It invested heavily in machinery and training, and its exports competed on cost and quality in a world with plenty of demand for cheap manufactured goods.

User: why did japan's growth eventually slow
Joe: Its neighbouring economies caught up and developed their own industries, so the advantage of being the low-cost new exporter disappeared.

User: why did the japanese asset bubble form
Joe: Land and company shares had risen for years on cheap credit, and the expectation of further rises was treated as reasonable rather than as a warning.

User: why did the japanese economy barely grow in the nineteen nineties
Joe: The collapse of the bubble left banks holding bad loans and companies carrying far too much debt, and neither would spend or lend to recover.

User: why did south korea grow so quickly
Joe: It directed credit towards manufacturing, invested heavily in education, and kept export industries under pressure to cut costs and improve quality.

User: why did the tiger economies depend so much on exports
Joe: Their domestic markets were small, so growth had to come from selling abroad, and that also meant they felt every downturn in the world market.

User: why did china develop so quickly along its coast first
Joe: The coast had ports, a concentration of investment and proximity to hong kong and taiwan, so it was the easiest place to start connecting to world trade.

User: why did the japanese occupation of china leave such deep resentment
Joe: It involved invasion, mass killing and economic exploitation, and that history is taught in china as a national matter rather than a distant event.

User: why did korea split into two countries
Joe: The country was occupied after the second world war, and the occupying powers set up rival governments with a border that has remained ever since.

User: why did the two koreas still end up trading heavily in recent decades
Joe: One had cheap labour and the other had capital and technology, and trade between them benefited both despite there being no formal relationship.

User: why did vietnam and india both grow through foreign investment
Joe: Both have a large workforce available at low cost and their own growing markets, and foreign firms use that combination to serve both at once.

User: why did india's growth really take off in the last twenty years
Joe: Its economy had been growing slowly through the period before that, and the combination of a young workforce, information technology work and rising demand finally showed up.

User: why is india's software industry concentrated in a few cities
Joe: It needs engineers and universities and the communications infrastructure to serve clients abroad, and all of that exists mainly around a few large cities.

User: why does india manage its rivers so differently from the rest of the world
Joe: Its rivers are shared across borders, so building anything that affects the flow upstream becomes a matter between states rather than a local decision.

User: why does a browning reaction need high heat
Joe: Browning needs molecules to move and react, and only at high heat do they move fast enough to link up into the new compounds you recognise as browning.

User: why does caramelised sugar harden as it cools
Joe: Heat breaks sugar molecules apart and links them into new chains, and once cooled those chains set into a solid that no longer flows.

User: why does adding an acid to milk make it curdle
Joe: Acid shifts the balance of charges that keep milk proteins apart, so they clump together and trap the liquid inside them, which is what you see as curds.

User: why does adding an acid to soup make it thicken
Joe: Acidity loosens some of the plant cell walls in root vegetables, so water escapes and the mixture thickens into a smoother sauce.

User: why does a squeezed lemon dull the smell of fish
Joe: Citric acid suppresses the volatile compounds that make fish smell strong, and it also masks a lot of the odour that water alone would leave behind.

User: why does salt keep meat from spoiling
Joe: Salt draws water out of the cells of spoilage microbes, so they cannot grow, and the drier surface also blocks the moulds that need damp conditions.

User: why does sugar preserve fruit
Joe: Sugar binds the water that microbes need to grow, and at high enough concentration it draws water out of their cells just as salt does.

User: why does oil stop metal from rusting
Joe: Oil coats the surface and seals out both water and oxygen, and rust cannot form without both of them reaching the metal.

User: why does vinegar clean a kettle
Joe: Acetic acid dissolves the limescale that builds up from hard water, breaking the mineral deposit loose so it can be rinsed away.

User: why does bicarbonate of soda work as a mild cleaner
Joe: Its alkaline nature and fine crystals give it gentle scrubbing power, and it neutralises a little acid, which helps lift greasy film off a surface.

User: why does bleach smell so sharp in a bathroom
Joe: Bleach reacts with organic material and gives off a pungent compound, and the sharp smell is a warning that something is actively reacting there.

User: why do cleaning products not get mixed together
Joe: Mixing two cleaners can produce a gas that is genuinely dangerous, because one common pair gives off chlorine, which is severely irritating at any concentration.

User: why does dish soap cut through grease
Joe: Soap molecules have a water-loving head and a grease-loving tail, so they surround a blob of fat and suspend it in water where it washes away.

User: why does anti-bac soap not kill every germ
Joe: Soap only affects germs whose outer surface dissolves in grease, and plenty of bacteria and viruses have coats that shrug it off entirely.

User: why does hand sanitiser need alcohol rather than soap
Joe: Alcohol dissolves and denatures the proteins of a germ quickly without water, so it can kill microbes on dry hands where washing is not possible.

User: why does a soft drink with caffeine keep you awake
Joe: Caffeine blocks the brain's adenosine receptors, so the brain does not register tiredness and the feeling of needing sleep is pushed back.

User: why does coffee stain a mug permanently
Joe: The dark compounds in coffee bond to the ceramic and resist ordinary washing, so a build-up over time needs something abrasive to shift it.

User: why does tea sometimes stain a cup a pale green
Joe: Tea leaves contain compounds that stick to glazed ceramic and slowly build up a film that ordinary washing does not remove on its own.

User: why does a microwave heat food unevenly
Joe: Microwaves only enter a limited distance and are absorbed unevenly by different parts of a dish, leaving cold spots between hot ones.

User: why does a microwave have a turntable
Joe: Moving the plate through the field so different parts spend time in strong and weak spots averages out those cold and hot regions.

User: why is metal foil dangerous inside a microwave
Joe: Metal reflects the microwaves instead of absorbing them, and sharp edges can spark and start a fire against the oven walls.

User: why does an induction hob not heat the pan directly
Joe: A changing magnetic field in the base induces currents in the metal pan, and the pan itself becomes the heating element, so no heat goes into the air.

User: why does a gas hob still work when the pan is ceramic
Joe: A gas flame heats whatever sits on it by direct contact and hot rising gas, which works for any material, unlike induction that needs metal.

User: why does an oven door have a double layer of glass
Joe: Two panes with trapped air between them slow heat moving outward, so the door stays cooler to touch and far less heat escapes the oven.

User: why does boiling water sometimes not dissolve instant coffee fully
Joe: The granules are coated to flow easily, and in water just off the boil that coating can trap a dry core inside and refuse to wet through.

User: why does tea need a brewing time rather than boiling water poured in
Joe: Boiling water on the leaves extracts bitter compounds fastest, so a few minutes at a slightly lower temperature gives flavour without the harshness.

User: why does coffee need pressure rather than just hot water
Joe: Coffee grounds are mostly insoluble, so pressure forces hot water through the packed grounds and dissolves oils and flavour compounds into the brew.

User: why is an unbleached paper fancier than a bleached one
Joe: Bleaching removes the lignin that holds brown plant fibres together, and the leftover lignin gives unbleached paper its softer colour and rougher feel.

User: why does writing paper absorb ink while a photograph does not
Joe: Paper has an open fibre structure with gaps for ink to sit in, while the smooth emulsion on a photograph has no pores to absorb it.

User: why does glue stick to paper and not glass
Joe: Glue wets and soaks into a porous surface like paper and locks into it on setting, while glass is smooth and non-porous so there is nothing to grip.

User: why does a wet sheet of paper tear more easily than a dry one
Joe: Water loosens the bonds between cellulose fibres, so the sheet loses strength and tears with far less force.

User: why does paper become brittle as it yellows
Joe: Cellulose breaks down over time into acids, and the acids weaken the fibres and yellow the sheet as they do it.

User: why does a wet book warp and rip
Joe: Water swells the paper unevenly depending on which side got wet and how long it stayed wet, and that uneven swelling buckles the pages.

User: why does a metal spoon conduct heat from a hot drink straight to your hand
Joe: Metal carries heat quickly and with little loss, so the spoon reaches nearly the temperature of the drink itself within seconds.

User: why does a plastic spoon stay cooler in the same drink
Joe: Plastic conducts heat far more slowly, so the spoon warms only slowly and your hand barely feels the drink's temperature through it.

User: why does a wooden spoon survive a hot pan better than a metal one
Joe: Wood does not conduct heat towards the handle, so the part you hold stays cool even when the tip is sitting in a very hot pan.

User: why does a sharpened knife cut easier than a blunt one
Joe: A sharp edge concentrates the same push onto a tiny area, so it presses past the surface tension of the material far more easily.

User: why does a saw cut in both directions but a knife mostly one
Joe: Saw teeth are set alternately to either side, so they cut on the outward stroke, while knife teeth lean forward and only bite one way.

User: why does a wire cut more easily with a diagonal blade than a straight one
Joe: A diagonal blade wedges the material apart as it pushes, so the wire is forced onto both blades and sheared rather than merely squashed.

User: why does a camp axe split wood along the grain
Joe: The wedge shape drives the fibres apart along their length, and wood is far weaker across its fibres than along them.

User: why does drilling need to clear chips out of the hole
Joe: The cut material has to go somewhere, and if it packs into the flutes it jams the bit and stops the cut from getting anywhere.

User: why does a screw thread stop it coming loose
Joe: The helix of the thread converts turning into a push along the axis, and friction on that shallow slope resists any backward movement.

User: why does a nail have a flat head
Joe: The wide head spreads the driving force over a large area, so the head cannot pull through the surface it is holding down.

User: why does a wall plug stop a screw pulling out of plasterboard
Joe: The plug spreads the load across a wide area behind the wall, so the pull is shared by far more material than the screw alone would hold.

User: why does a wing nut need washers to work
Joe: The washer spreads the pressure so the nut does not dig into the surface, which is what stops it loosening again under vibration.

User: why does a bolt head have a hexagonal shape
Joe: A hexagon gives enough flat faces to grip with a spanner while keeping the head small, and its corners let a firm grip from several angles.

User: why does a hinge need oil
Joe: The pin and knuckles rub metal on metal, and oil forms a film between them so the turning force drops and the surfaces stop wearing away.

User: why does a bicycle chain need lubrication
Joe: The chain links pivot on small pins and slide against each other, and dry metal on metal there quickly wears grooves and grinds.

User: why does a wheel need to be balanced
Joe: An off-centre mass shifts inward and outward as it turns, and that repeated imbalance wastes energy and makes the wheel bounce instead of running level.

User: why does a sundial work only on sunny days
Joe: Its shadow comes from sunlight, and under cloud the light scatters in every direction so the shadow disappears or blurs.

User: why does a compass need calibration after iron nearby
Joe: Iron and steel draw in magnetic field lines, so a phone, a bike frame or a steel table can distort the reading near the needle.

User: why does a barometer fall before a storm
Joe: Rising warm air ahead of a low pressure system lifts the air above, so that column carries less weight and the mercury column drops.

User: why does humidity falling before a storm come from cold air arriving
Joe: Cold air holds far less water vapour than warm air, so when it moves in and warms slightly the relative humidity drops even without losing any actual moisture.

User: why does a hygrometer use a human hair
Joe: Hair length changes predictably with humidity, and it is cheap and stable enough to move a light mechanism reliably.

User: why does a rain gauge need a level opening
Joe: Rain falls nearly vertically when the air is calm, so a level wide mouth catches the same amount regardless of which side you tilt it towards.

User: why does snow often fall in warmer places first and later
Joe: The melting level drops as a cold front moves through, so rain turns to sleet and then snow as the column cools beneath it.

User: why is hail heavier than rain and hurts more
Joe: Hailstones are frozen layers that grew by cycling through freezing air, so a stone of the same size carries far more mass than a drop of rain.

User: why does thunder sometimes crack and sometimes rumble
Joe: A close strike gives one sharp report from a short path, while a distant strike spreads the sound along many paths and arrives smeared out as a rumble.

User: why does wind feel colder on a bike than on foot
Joe: At speed the airflow past you sweeps away the warm layer your body is producing, so you lose heat faster than when you are standing still.

User: why does a fan make a room feel cooler
Joe: It raises evaporation from your skin by moving the damp air away, and evaporation is what actually takes heat off you.

User: why does a ceiling fan alone not cool a room
Joe: It only circulates the air already there, and since it does not remove heat, the room temperature stays the same however long it runs.

User: why does a swamp cooler work best in a dry climate
Joe: It works by evaporating water into the incoming air, and dry air can take up much more of that moisture than humid air already can.

User: why does a dehumidifier collect water in a tank
Joe: It cools a coil below the dew point so moisture in the passing air condenses and runs off the coil into the tank.

User: why does a dehumidifier make a room noticeably warmer
Joe: Heat taken out of the air by the coil is dumped back in the room by the condenser, so the net effect on the room is warming as well as drying.

User: why does a carpet trap dust that a hard floor does not
Joe: Fibres give dust a surface to land on and hold onto, while a hard floor leaves it loose and easily wiped away.

User: why does vacuuming seem to make more dust appear
Joe: It resuspends settled particles into the air so they can be sucked up, and the newly mobile dust looks more obvious than it did lying flat.

User: why does a feather duster push dust rather than collect it
Joe: Static charge from rubbing makes the fibres repel the dust, and switching to a damp cloth lets the dust stick instead.

User: why does a wet mop leave the floor looking dirty
Joe: A damp cloth loosens grime and moves it around, so it only looks clean once the floor dries and no longer has the reflective film of water.

User: why does soap scum build up in a bathroom
Joe: Hard water leaves mineral deposits when soap mixes with it, and those deposits trap dirt until they need an acid cleaner to dissolve them.

User: why do grout lines go dark in a shower
Joe: They hold moisture and soap residue, which gives mould and algae a damp place to grow that the tile surface itself will not allow.

User: why does a squeaky door hinge improve with oil
Joe: Oil replaces the metal-on-metal friction with a slippery film, so the hinge stops grabbing and the door swings quietly.

User: why does a wooden floor need to breathe
Joe: Wood expands and shrinks with the humidity in the air, and a floor sealed on all sides traps that movement until it buckles.

User: why does fresh concrete need to be kept damp while it cures
Joe: Hydration keeps the cement setting at the right rate, and letting it dry out too fast stops the reaction before the concrete reaches full strength.

User: why does cement cure rather than dry
Joe: It hardens by reacting with water to build new crystals, so it is a chemical reaction that sets, not simply the drying out of wet powder.

User: why does a brick wall let a house breathe better than a sealed wall
Joe: Brick holds and releases moisture, which keeps the wall from trapping damp inside it where it would rot the timber and raise the indoor humidity.

User: why does a cavity in a wall need a damp proof course
Joe: Ground moisture rises through porous masonry, and a horizontal barrier stops it climbing to the level of the timber floor.

User: why does loft insulation save so much energy
Joe: Heat rises and collects at the top of a house, so insulating the roof cuts the single biggest escape route for warmed indoor air.

User: why does double glazing reduce noise as well as heat loss
Joe: Two panes separated by trapped air form a gap that breaks the direct path for sound, so less vibration reaches the inside pane.

User: why does draught excluder tape work along a window edge
Joe: It closes the gap where warm indoor air would otherwise leak out past a frame that never quite fits the opening.

User: why does a chimney in a fireplace make a draughty room colder
Joe: It connects the room directly to the outside, so air rushes out to replace whatever the fire is drawing in, and the fire has to work harder for it.

User: why does a boiler keep radiators hot from one pipe
Joe: Hot water circulates through a sealed loop, absorbing heat from the burner and giving it back to the radiators as the water cools and returns.

User: why does a radiator need to be bled
Joe: Trapped air sits at the top and blocks water from reaching that part of the radiator, so bleeding lets it escape and the metal warms up fully.

User: why does an immersion heater sit near the top of a tank
Joe: Hot water rises, so the heater at the top heats the water that collects there and the warmer water sinks to give heat to everything below.

User: why does a heat pump deliver more heat than the electricity it uses
Joe: It moves heat rather than making it, so the heater output includes both the electricity supplied and the warmth taken from the air outside.

User: why does insulation reduce noise between rooms
Joe: Sound passing through the wall has to travel through insulation instead of air, and a dense mass of material absorbs much of that vibration.

User: why does soft furnishing absorb high frequencies better than low ones
Joe: Heavy soft materials turn vibration into heat most effectively for the shorter wavelengths, while long bass waves pass through them almost untouched.

User: why does a bank vault feel solid
Joe: A thick door and a heavy frame contain almost no vibration, so no rattling sound reaches your hand when you move it.

User: why does a lock need different strengths for different parts
Joe: The shackle resists being cut and the bolts resist being forced, so the material is chosen for whichever attack each part is most likely to face.

User: why does a combination lock reset itself after a wrong turn
Joe: An error stop drops a bar into the shackle, and only turning the dial correctly past the mistake can lift it and allow the bolts to move again.

User: why does a safe have a thick door but a thin back
Joe: The front faces an attacker with tools and time, while the back only faces a thief who already has the safe open elsewhere, so the effort goes where it matters.

User: why do banknotes have raised printing
Joe: Paper and metal threads show up under angled light, so counterfeit notes lack the fine raised detail that real banknotes carry.

User: why do coins have ridges along their edges
Joe: The ridges stop the edge being easily smoothed off in a pocket, and they make it possible to grip and count a stack quickly.

User: why is a coin round rather than square
Joe: A round coin has no corners to wear down over time, so it keeps its smooth edge and is much harder to cut or file down fraudulently.

User: why do cheap versions of a product fail first
Joe: Cheaper parts use thinner material and fewer fasteners, so they reach the limits sooner under the same everyday use.

User: why does a phone get slower as its storage fills up
Joe: With little free space the system cannot cache or organise data as freely, so operations that should be instant start waiting on the drive.

User: why does a laptop fan get loud when working hard
Joe: The chip generates heat under load, and the fan runs fast to push that heat out before the processor throttles its own speed.

User: why does a phone that is hot also charge slowly
Joe: Charge circuitry deliberately slows down when the battery is hot, because pushing current into a hot battery can damage it.

User: why does a cable bent sharply near the plug fail
Joe: Repeated bending breaks the inner conductors inside the cable where it is least supported, so the connection there goes intermittent.

User: why is a lithium battery different from a lead acid battery
Joe: Lithium cells hold much more energy for their weight, they charge far faster, and they do not need the liquid electrolyte a lead battery does.

User: why do rechargeable batteries slowly lose capacity
Joe: Every charge cycle makes small irreversible changes inside the cells, so after many cycles less of the original material is still active.

User: why does a power cut reset a computer
Joe: It has no battery to hold its settings, so when the mains supply drops everything not written to disk is lost as it shuts down.

User: why does a disk platter spin down gradually
Joe: The motor is told to reduce power in steps, and friction keeps turning the platter through each lower speed instead of stopping it abruptly.

User: why does a mechanical hard drive feel warmer than a solid state one
Joe: A spinning motor and moving heads do mechanical work that turns into heat, while a solid state drive only moves data and makes very little waste heat.

User: why is a backup drive not a copy of a file you can just look at
Joe: It records every change in a running log, and reconstructing a past version from that log is a separate operation from reading the current file.

User: why does a cloud service keep several copies of the same file
Joe: It keeps copies in separate data centres so that losing one building to fire or flood does not take the file with it.

User: why does a password manager make long passwords practical
Joe: It generates and stores them so the length and complexity no longer have to be remembered or typed by hand for every account.

User: why does typing a password into a fake login page still be dangerous
Joe: Whatever you type is sent to whoever controls that page, so the leak happens before the site can tell it is pretending to be the real one.

User: why does a password become insecure when it is reused
Joe: If one site is breached, the same password gives access everywhere else you have used it, so a single leak becomes many.

User: why does a phone need to be restarted to clear a stuck program
Joe: A stuck program can hold resources that nothing will release on its own, and restarting the whole system clears every one of them at once.

User: why does a computer slow down when you have many tabs open
Joe: Each tab holds its own memory for the page and its scripts, and once all available memory is used the system starts swapping things to disk.

User: why does a spreadsheet use references rather than copied numbers
Joe: A reference points at the original cell, so changing a number once updates every formula that depends on it.

User: why does a circular reference cause an error
Joe: A formula that depends on its own result has no starting value to calculate from, so there is nothing the program can work out.

User: why does copying a formula change its cell references
Joe: The references are relative offsets from the cell, so moving the formula moves everything it points at along with it.

User: why do spreadsheets put dollar signs in some references
Joe: A dollar sign fixes the row or column, so a reference like that stays pointing at the same cell no matter where the formula is copied.

User: why does sorting a list of numbers move them as a group with their labels
Joe: Sorting has to carry each label along with its value, or the list would end up with numbers paired to the wrong descriptions.

User: why does a search engine return results so quickly
Joe: Crawlers index pages ahead of time, so a query looks up a stored table instead of searching the whole internet at that moment.

User: why do search results rank some pages above others
Joe: Ranking combines signals like whether a page matches the words, who links to it, and how useful visitors seem to have found it.

User: why does a URL need a scheme
Joe: The scheme tells the browser which protocol to speak, and without it the browser would not know whether to fetch a page, open an email, or dial a number.

User: why does a cache speed up repeat visits to a page
Joe: A cache keeps a copy of the response, so a repeat request is answered from that copy instead of fetching everything again from the origin.

User: why is a download resuming useful on a shaky connection
Joe: It asks the server for the next slice rather than starting over, so the lost part of the connection costs only the part in transit.

User: why does streaming video buffer rather than download the whole file
Joe: It downloads slightly ahead of playback, so normal pauses and small slowdowns are covered by data already in hand.

User: why does video quality drop when a stream slows down
Joe: The player requests smaller versions when it detects it is not keeping up, because a watchable picture matters more than a sharp one.

User: why does an image look blocky when enlarged
Joe: Pixels cannot be invented beyond what was captured, so stretching a small image spreads each pixel over a larger square and the edges become visible.

User: why is a photograph that looks sharp small but soft when printed large
Joe: The detail available is fixed by the sensor, so a bigger print gives each recorded detail more room to show its own pixel edges.

User: why does a microphone placed close to a mouth pick up plosives
Joe: Air rushes out in a short burst at the start of certain consonants, and close placement catches that burst at full force as a click.

User: why do headphones of different prices sound different
Joe: Cheaper ones use smaller and lighter drivers and simpler housing, so the frequency response and the control of resonance are both less accurate.

User: why does an echo canceller use two microphones
Joe: One picks up your voice and the other the playback, and comparing them tells the system what to subtract from the outgoing signal.

User: why does noise cancelling work best on low rumbles
Joe: Low frequencies change slowly, so the cancelling signal can match their timing closely, while fast high frequencies drift out of alignment.

User: why does a video call freeze while audio continues
Joe: Audio is a small continuous stream, while video waits for a whole frame to arrive, so losing data hits the picture first.

User: why does an old phone lose battery quicker than a new one
Joe: Older hardware runs less efficiently and often cannot sleep as deeply, so it draws more current for the same work.

User: why does a device show a battery percentage that is never exactly right
Joe: The charge is estimated from voltage rather than counted exactly, and the estimate drifts with temperature, age and how the battery has been used.

User: why does a battery swell in a phone
Joe: Chemical changes over time can trap gas inside the cells, and the gas has nowhere to go in a sealed battery, so the case is pushed outward.

User: why does a microwave stop working at all when the oven above it is on
Joe: The microwave listens for a specific signal to confirm the button press was real, and a leaking oven signal can be mistaken for that reply.

User: why does a smoke alarm chirp when the battery is low
Joe: It tests its own sensor with a real beep now and then, and a weak battery makes that test beep too quiet to check properly, so it warns you instead.

User: why does a carbon monoxide alarm sound like a pattern rather than a tone
Joe: It has to be recognisable as a warning rather than mistaken for a normal appliance beep, and a repeated pattern is far less likely to be ignored.

User: why does a fire door slow the spread of a fire
Joe: It seals the opening automatically so smoke and flame cannot pour into the stairwell that people are using to escape.

User: why does a sprinkler head release water at a set temperature
Joe: A sealed bulb or a wax-pellet link holds the nozzle shut, and heat softens the seal at a chosen point so the water starts flowing by itself.

User: why does an escape plan need two ways out of a room
Joe: Fire blocks whichever route the fire started near, and a single exit can be cut off, so a second route gives people a chance.

User: why do lift doors reopen when something is in the way
Joe: Sensors across the doorway detect the obstruction and reverse the doors, so nobody is caught and cut as they close.

User: why does an escalator have a moving handrail on the step side
Joe: The handrail travels at exactly the same speed as the steps, so a person holding it is not dragged off balance when they step on or off.

User: why is the gap between a train and the platform dangerous
Joe: The carriage overhangs as it turns into the platform, so the space narrows as you approach and the step is not aligned with the edge.

User: why do airport runways have grooves cut into them
Joe: Water on the surface slows a tyre, and the grooves let it drain away so a plane can brake and steer properly in the rain.

User: why do parachutes sometimes fail to open fully
Joe: If a canopy snags on air and collapses, the lines lose tension and the chute cannot inflate, which is why training and line management matter so much.

User: why does a hot-air balloon rise only when the burner fires
Joe: Only heating the air above makes it less dense than its surroundings, so the balloon climbs in bursts rather than smoothly.

User: why do pressure suits need to hold their shape
Joe: Fabric would crumple and pinch the wearer in a thin air layer, so stiffening keeps the volume constant and the pressure inside working.

User: why is water used as a coolant in nuclear reactors
Joe: It absorbs a great deal of heat without boiling away, and it can be routed around the reactor where it never touches the radioactive fuel.

User: why does a nuclear reactor use control rods
Joe: Absorbing material slides into the core to soak up neutrons, and the position of the rods sets exactly how fast the reaction runs.

User: why is a nuclear reactor kept from overheating by water alone
Joe: If the cooling is lost the fuel keeps generating heat, so the design has to shut the reaction down on its own before the metal can fail.

User: why does radiation damage show up years later
Joe: It harms cells that keep dividing, so the effect appears when those damaged cells finally reproduce, often long after the exposure.

User: why is the ozone layer important for life
Joe: It absorbs the damaging ultraviolet light from the sun, which would otherwise burn living tissue and damage the DNA of everything exposed to it.

User: why is the greenhouse effect not the same as global warming
Joe: Greenhouse gases naturally warm the planet by trapping outgoing heat, and global warming is the extra warming caused by adding more of them.

User: why do volcanoes erupt more after rain
Joe: Water can flash to steam inside the magma, and the expanding steam shatters the rock around it, forcing a violent eruption.

User: why does a geyser erupt in bursts rather than continuously
Joe: Water fills the narrow pipe and heats to boiling, and the pressure drop after the first burst lets more water flash at once, so it repeats in pulses.

User: why do earthquakes rarely happen at the top of a volcano
Joe: Magma moves deep underground, where the weight of rock above hides the pressure, so the ground at the summit rarely reacts directly to it.

User: why do we measure earthquakes with two readings from different stations
Joe: The gap between the arrivals tells you how far away the source was, and only two positions let you work out where it actually was.

User: why do buildings survive an earthquake better with a deep foundation
Joe: It locks the structure to stable rock below the moving soil, so the shaking is transferred down rather than left to rattle the walls.

User: why do underground pipes burst in an earthquake
Joe: Ground movement shears the pipe at its joints, and the pressure pushes the break wide once the seal is gone.

User: why does a tsunami lose energy as it reaches shallow water
Joe: The wave slows in shallower water but the same water has to pile higher, which is why the wave grows taller right before it hits land.

User: why does a tsunami have to be generated by something moving a lot of water
Joe: A large vertical displacement of the sea floor, such as an undersea quake or a big collapse, is what shifts the whole water column above it.

User: why do coral reefs need clear shallow water
Joe: Corals build their skeletons from calcium carbonate that the polyps produce in sunlight, and murky water cuts off the light they depend on.

User: why do coral reefs bleach when the water warms
Joe: Heat stress makes the coral expel the algae living in its tissue, and without that partner the coral loses its colour and its food supply.

User: why do mangrove trees protect a coastline
Joe: Their tangled roots slow moving water and hold sediment, so waves lose energy before they reach whatever is behind them.

User: why do wetlands filter water
Joe: Dense plant growth slows the flow and traps sediment and dissolved nutrients, so the water leaving a wetland is cleaner than the water entering it.

User: why do deserts get cold at night
Joe: Dry air holds little water vapour, and water vapour is what normally traps heat near the ground after the sun goes down.

User: why do mountain tops freeze even in the tropics
Joe: Air thins with altitude, and thinner air holds less heat, while the thin atmosphere also cannot block the heat radiating away at night.

User: why does altitude make boiling take longer
Joe: Water boils cooler up there, so it stays below the temperature food needs to soften and cook properly.

User: why do some valleys have a permanent cloud layer
Joe: Moist air rising over the ridge cools until it condenses into a cloud that sits in the same band each afternoon.

User: why do monsoon winds reverse direction across the year
Joe: The continent heats faster than the ocean in summer and cools faster in winter, and the shifting pressure between them swings the winds.

User: why is the desert coast of Chile one of the driest places on earth
Joe: The Andes block any moist air from the Amazon, and cold Humboldt Current water cools the air so it cannot hold that moisture.

User: why does the Amazon rainforest act as its own rainfall supply
Joe: Transpiring trees release vast amounts of water vapour that rises, cools and rains back down, so the forest helps generate its own weather.

User: why does a rain shadow form behind a mountain
Joe: Air drops its moisture climbing the windward slope, and by the time it crosses the ridge it is dry, so the far side stays in shadow.

User: why do coastal areas have milder winters than inland areas
Joe: The sea releases stored heat slowly over months, and that warmth keeps the air above it far milder than air over land at the same latitude.

User: why does the Gulf Stream keep western Europe warmer than its latitude suggests
Joe: It carries warm tropical water northeast across the Atlantic, and that heat goes into the air above it, warming the winds that blow onshore.

User: why do the deepest ocean trenches stay so inaccessible
Joe: The pressure rises by roughly one atmosphere every ten metres, so by the time you reach the bottom it is like being in a hundred cars stacked on you.

User: why do divers get the bends if they surface too fast
Joe: Pressure dissolved nitrogen into their blood, and dropping pressure too quickly lets it form bubbles in the tissues and joints.

User: why is the deep sea cold and pitch dark
Joe: Sunlight warms only the top few hundred metres, and below that the water is near freezing with no light at all to reach it.

User: why do some deep sea creatures glow
Joe: Most live where no sunlight reaches, so light produced by their own bodies is used to attract prey, hide in shadow, or signal to each other.

User: why is the deep sea full of life despite the darkness
Joe: Organic material sinks from the lit surface layer, and that constant fall of food supports a whole food web below it.

User: why do sea creatures sometimes rise to the surface
Joe: Some are following food, some are riding currents, and others such as whales come up to breathe.

User: why does a whale strand sometimes happen in shallow bays
Joe: Deep channels and echo sounding can confuse the animal's navigation, and shallow bays offer no escape once it has turned in the wrong direction.

User: why do fish in a school stay together
Joe: Each fish benefits from what the others' eyes and movement reveal, and staying together also confuses anything trying to pick one out.

User: why does a fish use a swim bladder
Joe: It fills or empties the bladder with gas so the fish can hold its position without swimming hard, which saves energy.

User: why do sharks need to keep swimming
Joe: Most sharks have no swim bladder, so they get lift from their constantly moving fins and stop drifting if they stop swimming.

User: why do flatfish lie flat on the seabed
Joe: Both eyes migrate to one side as they mature, so they can watch and hunt while lying buried in the sand.

User: why do octopus change colour so quickly
Joe: Layers of pigment in their skin are stretched and squeezed by muscles, and they can also alter the texture of the skin behind it.

User: why do octopuses seem so intelligent
Joe: They have large brains, solve puzzles, open jars and escape tanks, and they can change colour instantly to match any background.

User: why do some fish lose their colour when they are stressed
Joe: Stress releases hormones that pull pigment back into the cells beneath the skin, leaving the fish pale in a moment.

User: why does the jellyfish not have a brain
Joe: It has a simple nerve net rather than a brain, which is enough to sense touch and food and coordinate the pulsing of its bell.

User: why does a jellyfish sting
Joe: Specialised cells fire a tiny harpoon with a thread of venom when they are touched, and that harpoon stays anchored as it injects.

User: why do crabs grow by shedding their shells
Joe: A shell cannot grow with the animal inside it, so the crab forms a soft new shell underneath and breaks out of the old one.

User: why does a crab have claws of different sizes
Joe: The bigger claw crushes prey and the smaller one pinches and cuts, so the asymmetry lets one crab do two different jobs at once.

User: why do hermit crabs take on empty shells
Joe: Their own abdomen is soft and unprotected, so a borrowed hard shell becomes both home and armour they can carry with them.

User: why does a lobster grow a bigger shell as a juvenile
Joe: Shells do not stretch, so a growing lobster must repeatedly moult and replace its armour with one that fits its larger body.

User: why does a sea turtle dig a nest on a specific night
Joe: The tides and moon phase shape when the sand is soft and high enough for her to dig, and she lays at the season when hatchlings will have warmth and food.

User: why do sea turtles return to the beach they hatched on
Joe: Earth's magnetic field registers in their nervous system as a map, and hatchlings imprint on the magnetic signature of their home beach.

User: why do penguins huddle together
Joe: Huddling traps a layer of warm air in the middle of the group, and the birds on the outside take turns rotating into the centre.

User: why do penguins stand on their heels
Joe: Their weight rests back on the tail and feet, which lets them lean and slide, and their flippers work like wings underwater.

User: why does a bird's feather keep it dry
Joe: Feathers interlock into a surface so tightly that water beads on it and rolls off, and preening restores that interlocking after rain.

User: why do birds have hollow bones
Joe: Air-filled bones keep the skeleton light enough for flight while still resisting bending, which saves weight without losing strength.

User: why can a bird not simply chew its food
Joe: Birds have no teeth and instead rely on a beak to tear pieces off and on a gizzard with grit to grind them, which is why they swallow food whole.

User: why do some birds stand on one leg
Joe: Tucking one leg up cuts heat loss from the unfeathered foot while the bird rests, which matters most in cold water.

User: why do migrating birds know which way to go
Joe: They use the sun, the stars, the Earth's magnetic field and landmarks together, and the route is learned and passed down within a species.

User: why do birds sing at dawn
Joe: Dawn is when the light is often clearest and when songs carry furthest, so it is the time a territory is best worth defending.

User: why do some birds mimic others
Joe: Mimicking rivals or the local species helps a bird hold territory or draw in a mate, and it is often learned young from what it hears.

User: why do owls hunt so quietly
Joe: Fringed feathers break up the airflow instead of cutting it, so the wingbeat makes far less noise than it would on other birds.

User: why does an owl turn its head almost upside down
Joe: Its neck joints allow an enormous range of rotation, letting it fix on a sound behind or above it without moving the whole body.

User: why do bats catch insects in the dark
Joe: They send out calls and listen to the returning echoes, which gives them a detailed sound picture of the space around them.

User: why do some frogs survive being frozen
Joe: Their bodies fill with ice, but glucose acts as a protectant that stops the cells rupturing, so they thaw out alive afterwards.

User: why do frogs often sit still while hunting
Joe: Frogs eat moving insects, so sitting still and striking fast is far more effective than chasing, and their tongues snap out further than the eye can follow.

User: why does a snake shed its skin
Joe: The old skin does not grow with the animal, so it is split along the face and peeled away, leaving the new skin underneath to grow with it.

User: why do snakes flick their tongue out
Joe: The forked tongue collects chemical particles from the air and ground into two separate samples, which the scent organ compares to find the trail.

User: why do some snakes constrict rather than bite
Joe: Constricting wraps around prey until it can no longer draw breath, which is a steady method that does not require a venom dose.

User: why do geckos stick to walls
Joe: Their toe pads carry millions of microscopic setae, and each one splits into thousands of even smaller tips that grip rough surfaces through molecular attraction.

User: why do chameleons change colour to blend in
Joe: Colour change comes from rearranging crystals in the skin, and it helps with both camouflage and signalling mood to other chameleons.

User: why do chameleons have two eyes that move independently
Joe: Each eye can swivel on its own, so one stays on prey while the other scans behind them for danger or a rival.

User: why do elephants have enormous ears
Joe: Blood vessels run close to the surface, and flapping the ears sheds heat, which is how a large animal cools down in a hot country.

User: why do elephants need so much food
Joe: Their digestive tract is built for bulky low-energy vegetation, so they must eat a very large share of their body weight in plants every day.

User: why do elephants walk so quietly
Joe: Their feet have soft pads that absorb the impact of each step, and the weight sits on the toe and heel bones like a cushion.

User: why do elephants mourn their dead
Joe: They return to the bodies and bones of herd members repeatedly and touch them gently, which looks very much like grief for what they lost.

User: why do giraffes have such long necks
Joe: The neck reaches leaves other animals cannot and also gives a high vantage point, and the animal manages its blood pressure to avoid fainting when it lowers its head.

User: why do giraffes sleep standing up
Joe: They only lie down for a short time because getting up from the ground is slow and hard, so most of their sleep happens standing.

User: why do hippopotamuses look harmless in water
Joe: Their eyes and nostrils sit high on the skull so they can watch and breathe while almost the whole body stays submerged and out of the sun.

User: why do hippos sweat blood
Joe: They have no true sweat glands, so instead they secrete a red oily fluid from the skin that acts as both sunscreen and an antibacterial.

User: why do camels have long eyelashes
Joe: They keep sand out of the eyes, and a second row of lashes plus closable nostrils protect the face from blowing sand in a desert.

User: why do camels survive so long without water
Joe: Their body temperature varies with the day, delaying sweating, and their blood and tissues can tolerate large swings before needing to drink.

User: why do penguins huddle even when it is not that cold
Joe: Huddling cuts wind exposure and gives them a place to breed and rest together, so it does more than just reduce heat loss.

User: why do bees dance
Joe: A returning bee moves in a figure that tells colonymates the direction to food and roughly how far away it is, by angle against the sun and by the dance's duration.

User: why do bees produce honey
Joe: They collect nectar, add enzymes, and fan it until water evaporates, leaving a thick sugary store that survives through winter.

User: why are honeybees vital to many crops
Joe: Moving between flowers to gather nectar, they carry pollen between plants and enable fruit and seed formation in many species.

User: why do ants follow each other in a line
Joe: A scouting ant leaves a trail of scent, and others following it reinforce the trail, so the shortest route to food ends up the strongest.

User: why do ants appear to lift far more than their own weight
Joe: Small animals are limited by muscle force, while being tiny means they can lift many times their body weight relative to larger animals.

User: why does a ladybird taste bad
Joe: When disturbed it releases a bitter yellow liquid from its leg joints, which makes predators spit it out and learn to leave it alone.

User: why do butterflies taste with their feet
Joe: Chemoreceptors on the tarsi check whether a leaf is the right plant for the female to lay eggs on.

User: why do moths fly towards light
Joe: They navigate by keeping a fixed angle to the light's direction as a shortcut for the moon, and a lamp breaks that rule by putting the light far too close.

User: why do fireflies glow
Joe: Light in the abdomen signals to a potential partner, and the flash patterns are species specific so the right kind finds the right kind.

User: why do beetles have hard wing cases
Joe: The thickened forewings form a shield over the flying wings, which lets a beetle push through soil or under bark without tearing them.

User: why do spiders build webs
Joe: The silk captures flying insects, but the web also works as an extension of the spider's senses because vibrations in it tell it where something landed.

User: why can a spider wrap prey in silk before eating it
Joe: Prey that struggles can injure a spider or escape, so silk restrains it and the spider can inject its digestives in safety.

User: why do tarantulas flick hairs at a threat
Joe: The barbed hairs irritate the skin and airways of an attacker, giving the spider a chance to get away using its own irritating defences.

User: why do millipedes curl into a ball
Joe: Curling protects their soft underside and head, and their hard shell plates are made effective as a defence in exactly this shape.

User: why do worms come to the surface after rain
Joe: They breathe through damp skin and would drown in waterlogged soil, so heavy rain drives them up to the surface where oxygen is available.

User: why do worms enrich soil as they move through it
Joe: They eat organic matter and pass out the remainder as castings, which aerates and enriches the ground along their tunnel.

User: why do snails carry their house
Joe: The shell protects them from drying out and from predators, and it can be sealed with mucus during dry or cold weather.

User: why do slugs not need a shell
Joe: They stay in damp places and secrete mucus that stops them drying out, so they trade armour for flexibility.

User: why does a slug leave a silvery trail
Joe: The slime it secretes protects the soft body from drying and rough surfaces, and it leaves that trail behind as it moves.

User: why do earthworms pull leaves into their burrows
Joe: The leaves are food that they drag back to where they are safe to eat, and decomposing leaves feed the surrounding soil as well.

User: why does a mole have claws shaped like spades
Joe: They are built for digging, pushing loose soil aside like a plough rather than cutting into it.

User: why do bats hang upside down without falling
Joe: Their feet have hooked claws that lock onto a branch, and the small size of the body makes the grip strong enough to hold their weight.

User: why do dolphins sleep with half their brain awake
Joe: One eye stays open and one half of the brain keeps enough running to breathe and surface for air, so they never fully lose awareness.

User: why do whales blow water from the top of their head
Joe: The blowhole leads to the lungs, and a powerful exhale carrying warm wet air is what makes the visible spray at the surface.

User: why do grey whales travel so far each year
Joe: They feed on bottom-dwelling animals in high-latitude waters in summer and travel to warmer shallows to breed and give birth in winter.

User: why do elephants live longer than most large mammals
Joe: Very large animals age more slowly across nearly every part of the body, and being large appears to buy extra years.

User: why do small mammals live such short lives
Joe: They burn energy fast and mature early, and that fast life strategy trades longevity for quick reproduction.

User: why do birds and mammals control their own body temperature
Joe: Being endothermic lets them stay active in cold conditions, though it costs a lot of energy and requires constant food to fuel.

User: why do fish in cold water live longer
Joe: Cold slows their metabolism, and slower metabolism means fewer growth-related changes and more energy for repair over the years.

User: why do some animals regrow a lost limb
Joe: Their cells keep dividing through adulthood and can rebuild the missing bone, muscle, nerves and skin from scratch.

User: why do starfish regrow arms but not most animals
Joe: Starfish keep most of their organs spread through the arm, so an arm with a piece of the central disc can grow into a whole new animal.

User: why do cuttlefish change texture as well as colour
Joe: They control papillae in the skin, so rough patches can be raised or smoothed to match whatever surface they are sitting on.

User: why do creatures that live in the deep sea often glow the same blue-green
Joe: Blue-green travels furthest through seawater, so a glow in that colour carries a long way and is hard for a predator to trace back.

User: why do elephants and rhinos need ivory that is so hard
Joe: Teeth have to survive years of grinding fibrous food, and enamel is among the hardest tissues in the animal body.

User: why do rodents keep growing their incisors
Joe: Their front teeth are worn flat by gnawing, so they must keep lengthening to replace what gets worn away.

User: why do elephants' molars come through in sequence
Joe: As the animal ages, worn back teeth are replaced by new ones moving forward from the jaw, so the grinding surface stays usable.

User: why do dogs wag their tails
Joe: A wagging tail signals that a dog is friendly and excited, and dogs read each other's tails to judge intention before they meet.

User: why does a dog sniff another dog before anything else
Joe: The chemical information in urine and scent glands tells a dog who the other dog is and how it is feeling, far faster than sight.

User: why do cats rub their faces on a person
Joe: They leave scent glands from their cheeks and forehead on the surface, marking the person as theirs and gathering scent from them.

User: why do cats sleep so much
Joe: A cat's natural hunting pattern is short bursts with long resting periods, and sleeping keeps them ready to spring when movement appears.

User: why do cats bring back dead prey
Joe: In the absence of kittens to teach them, the behaviour often continues as a way of practising or sharing a catch they cannot eat themselves.

User: why do some dogs bark more when left alone
Joe: Separation leaves them alert and calling for the pack, and the barking can become a habit that continues even when company returns.

User: why do horses have large eyes on the sides of their heads
Joe: The wide placement gives an almost panoramic view behind and beside them, which is what lets a prey animal spot danger early.

User: why do cows chew for so long each day
Joe: Cows are ruminants with a four-part stomach, so partly digested food is brought back up and re-chewed to break down plant fibre further.

User: why do cows produce milk
Joe: After calving, the mammary glands are stimulated to make milk for the calf, and that production continues as long as the calf keeps nursing.

User: why do pigs roll in mud
Joe: Mud cools them down and the dry crust then blocks insects and parasites that would otherwise get into their skin.

User: why do goats climb so well
Joe: Their hooves have soft pads that grip rough rock and hard edges that catch ledges, and their balance and strength suit steep ground.

User: why do sheep struggle to get up after lying down
Joe: Their leg joints fold beneath them in a way that makes standing awkward, which is also why a sheep on its back can suffocate.

User: why do giraffes eat with a long prehensile tongue
Joe: It lets them strip leaves from thorny branches while the tough bark protects the mouth from the thorns.

User: why do hummingbirds hover
Joe: Their wings beat fast enough to push air down continuously, which lets them hold a fixed spot in the air and even fly backwards.

User: why do hummingbirds feed so often
Joe: Their metabolism is extremely fast, so they burn through energy quickly and must top up on nectar every few minutes or they risk falling.

User: why do penguins huddle when moulting
Joe: During the moult they cannot go into the water, so staying together conserves heat and protects them while they wait out the loss of waterproof feathers.

User: why do sea otters hold hands
Joe: So they do not drift apart while sleeping at sea, and a mother wraps a pup in kelp to anchor it while she dives for food.

User: why do beavers fell trees
Joe: They need wood to build dams and lodges, and their incisors can cut through young trunks in a single night.

User: why do prairie dogs build burrows
Joe: The burrows give protection from predators and from weather, with separate chambers for sleeping, nursery and food storage.

User: why do lemurs sun themselves
Joe: Basking warms them through cold mornings, which matters because their activity and digestion rely on body temperature staying up.

User: why do kangaroos not walk backwards well
Joe: Their large feet and long tail make a backward step unstable, so forward movement and a fast turn are the only comfortable options.

User: why do koalas eat almost only eucalyptus
Joe: Their gut holds a specialised microbiome that can digest the toxic leaves, and almost no other animal can do that.

User: why do polar bears have black skin under white fur
Joe: Black skin absorbs whatever sunlight reaches it, and the fur is translucent and hollow rather than actually white.

User: why do camels have wide feet
Joe: Broad padded feet spread their weight on loose sand and stop them sinking in, much as snowshoes do on snow.

User: why do penguins have wings they cannot fly with
Joe: Over millions of years the same front limbs were reshaped into stiff flippers that work as underwater wings instead of in the air.

User: why do hummingbird feathers shimmer brightly
Joe: Their structure creates iridescence, reflecting different colours depending on the viewing angle, which makes the bird flash as it turns.

User: why do parrots mimic human speech
Joe: Their vocal control and social nature mean sounds learned early get attached to people and repeated when it draws attention.

User: why do crows leave gifts
Joe: Some drop bright objects near people who feed them, and delivering something earns a share of the attention and food that follows.

User: why do elephants show mourning behaviour
Joe: They show interest in the bodies and bones of their dead, revisit them often, and seem to recognise what happened to the herd member.

User: why do flamingos turn pink
Joe: Their diet is rich in carotenoids, and those pigments build up in the skin and feathers over time, so the pink deepens with age.

User: why do some insects glow green in the dark
Joe: A chemical reaction in special organs releases light with very little heat, and green is the colour human eyes are most sensitive to.

User: why do fish schools move like a single body
Joe: Each fish reacts to its immediate neighbours in simple rules, and those local responses combine into one synchronised movement.

User: why do ants leave trails that other ants follow
Joe: A returning ant lays down scent, and the concentration of that scent marks a route worth following, which grows stronger with more traffic.

User: why do bees return to the same flower
Joe: They learn the colour, shape and scent of profitable flowers, and the waggle dance passes that location on to the colony.

User: why do some flowers open only at night
Joe: Night-opening plants target the moths and bats that pollinate them, and these pollinators are drawn to pale flowers with strong evening scent.

User: why do plants droop when they are not watered
Joe: Losing water reduces the pressure inside the cells, so they can no longer hold themselves stiff and the leaves sag.

User: why do cacti have spines instead of leaves
Joe: Spines cut water loss and can also collect condensation, while the flattened stem takes over the work of photosynthesis.

User: why do desert plants open their stomata at night
Joe: Cooler night air holds moisture better, so opening the pores then loses far less water than opening them in the heat of the day.

User: why do some plants become poisonous when threatened
Joe: Under attack they release toxins that deter the plant-eating animals, since being eaten costs them far more than making the toxin.

User: why do plants cooperate with fungi at their roots
Joe: Fungal threads reach far more water and minerals than roots can, and the plant supplies the fungus with sugar in exchange.

User: why does the fungus in a mycorrhizal partnership get sugar from the plant
Joe: Plants make sugar from sunlight, and fungi cannot, so the plant supplies it in return for the fungus digging up nutrients.

User: why do trees drop leaves in autumn
Joe: Below a certain temperature and light level, keeping broad leaves costs more water than they repay, so the tree seals them off and drops them.

User: why do evergreens keep their needles
Joe: Narrow needles have a small surface area, lose less water, and photosynthesise whenever conditions allow, even through winter.

User: why do mangrove leaves grow near the roots
Joe: Those extra leaves are actually aerial roots that take in air directly, since waterlogged salt soil leaves normal roots unable to breathe.

User: why do conifer cones open only when it is dry
Joe: Scales that hold the seeds closed swell when damp and shrink when dry, so cones open in dry wind and reseal in the wet.

User: why do ferns not have seeds
Joe: They are older than seed plants, reproducing by spores on the underside of their fronds instead.

User: why do mosses need water to reproduce
Joe: Their sperm have to swim through a film of water to reach the egg, so they are restricted to damp places.

User: why do algae bloom so suddenly
Joe: Warm nutrient-rich water lets one species multiply far faster than anything eating it, and the population can jump within days.

User: why do mushrooms appear all at once after rain
Joe: The underground network was already growing invisibly, and rain supplies the moisture it needs to push fruit bodies up in a single flush.

User: why do mushrooms grow in rings
Joe: They spread outward from one starting point, exhausting the nutrients behind as they go, which leaves an arc growing at the expanding edge.

User: why is a mushroom not a plant
Joe: It has no chlorophyll, so it cannot photosynthesise, and it feeds by absorbing digested matter from other organisms instead.

User: why do lichen grow on bare rock
Joe: The fungus part holds the lichen against the surface and takes in minerals, while the algae or cyanobacteria inside photosynthesise its food.

User: why do lichens indicate clean air
Joe: They absorb whatever is in the atmosphere with no way to get rid of it, so they only flourish where the air carries little pollution.

User: why do sunflowers track the sun when young
Joe: Growth hormones make cells on the shaded side lengthen faster, so young stems and buds bend toward the light through the day.

User: why do mature sunflowers stop moving and face east
Joe: As the flower matures it stops tracking, and facing east gets it warm early, which warms the visitors it needs to come and pollinate it.

User: why do water lilies float their leaves on the surface
Joe: Their stalks are flexible enough to hold leaves at the water surface, where they reach the light and air while the root stays anchored below.

User: why do pitcher plants trap insects
Joe: They grow in poor soil, and digesting prey supplies the nitrogen and minerals they cannot otherwise get enough of.

User: why do venus flytraps snap shut
Joe: Trigger hairs on the trap register repeated touches, so a moving insect closes the trap while a raindrop or single touch does not waste it.

User: why do sundew plants have sticky leaves
Joe: Sticky droplets on curling tentacles hold prey and hold digestive enzymes at once, so the plant digests as the leaf closes over it.

User: why do plants at high altitude grow slowly
