Joe: Cold shortens the growing season and slows the chemistry of photosynthesis, so the plant takes far longer to build the same amount of tissue.

User: why do succulents store so much water in their leaves
Joe: Thick tissues hold a reserve that fills during any rain, letting the plant survive long dry spells without opening its stomata.

User: why do green algae on a pond block sunlight from plants below
Joe: The algae are photosynthesising too, and the dense green layer cuts the light reaching the submerged plants, which then cannot photosynthesise enough.

User: why do trees planted in cities struggle more than those in countryside
Joe: Compacted soil, restricted roots and limited water around pavements all hold back the growth a tree gets in open ground.

User: why do hedges keep soil from washing away on a slope
Joe: Their roots hold the soil in place and their leaves break the fall of rain, so less water runs loose down the slope.

User: why do trees planted on a hillside still die of thirst
Joe: On a slope, water runs off quickly and deeper soil stays dry even after rain, so roots never reach the moisture they need.

User: why do mushrooms need dead wood rather than living trees
Joe: They feed on already-dead material that has lost its protective compounds, and the strong living tree would outcompete them if it could.

User: why do some trees live to be thousands of years old
Joe: They grow slowly and rebuild damaged wood continuously, so their cell division stays even and their trunks survive centuries of wear.

User: why do trees have growth rings
Joe: Each year's early wood has large cells for spring growth and later wood has thick walls for winter, so the two look different as a ring.

User: why does the width of a ring tell you about a year's weather
Joe: A wide ring means the tree had good conditions and made a lot of wood, while a narrow one points to drought, cold or damage that year.

User: why do trees grow taller but not much thicker when young
Joe: Height comes from the shoot tip pushing upward, while thickness comes from adding cells inside, which only makes the trunk stronger as it matures.

User: why do trees lose their tops and survive
Joe: The living tissue that matters sits in the trunk and roots, so a damaged crown can be replaced from buds below the loss.

User: why do trees grow a dense knot where a branch breaks
Joe: Healing tissue seals the wound, and new growth around it leaves a raised ring of grain that never merges cleanly with the trunk.

User: why does a knot in wood exist at all
Joe: A branch that was buried by later growth leaves its enclosed grain inside the trunk, which is what reads as a knot when the board is cut.

User: why do palms grow straight without woody trunks
Joe: Palm stems lack the secondary thickening that lets other trees widen, so they must grow tall and thin quickly rather than building a trunk.

User: why does a tree's roots spread so widely near the surface
Joe: Most of the fine feeding roots sit near the surface where air and water are available, and only the tap root or trunk anchors the plant.

User: why do trees on a beach stay put in shifting sand
Joe: Root systems that reach deep and wide anchor the tree, and the fact that loose sand lets water through keeps the roots from suffocating.

User: why does an electromagnet go weak when you switch off
Joe: Its field exists only while current flows, so the magnetism disappears the moment the supply is cut.

User: why does a petrol engine need a starter motor
Joe: An engine needs a strong push to begin turning, and the starter supplies that first push before the engine can generate its own power.

User: why does a diesel engine glow when it gets old
Joe: Incomplete combustion in worn injectors leaves hot particles burning in the exhaust, and that glow is what shows at the tailpipe.

User: why can a robot arm not feel a break in a glass it is holding
Joe: It has position sensors at the joints but no sensors in the glass, so unless the arm's own position changes it has no idea the glass cracked.

User: why does a printer paper jam more often when the paper is damp
Joe: Damp sheets cling together and swell slightly, so they resist feeding cleanly and the rollers grip more than they should.

User: why does a document scanner give a clearer result than a photograph
Joe: It moves a very bright narrow light across the page while holding the paper flat, so nothing blurs and the illumination is even.

User: why does a digital camera shake blur the picture more at slow speeds
Joe: A longer exposure gives the camera more time to move, so a hand tremor shifts the image during the whole time the sensor is collecting light.

User: why does a larger sensor gather more light
Joe: It collects photons over a bigger area, so more signal reaches the amplifier and the image holds up better in dim conditions.

User: why does a mirrorless camera not need a mirror inside
Joe: The sensor itself shows what the lens sees, so the light does not have to bounce off a mirror and a screen to reach you.

User: why does a video compress so well that detail is lost
Joe: It only stores the change from one frame to the next, and when that change is small almost no data is needed to store it.

User: why does a higher compression ratio make a smaller file but a worse picture
Joe: More detail is thrown away to save space, so the smaller file is a poorer approximation of the original.

User: why does a streaming service buffer when the internet drops briefly
Joe: It has a buffer of already downloaded video, and a short interruption is absorbed as long as the buffer has not run out.

User: why does a video call freeze up rather than go blurry
Joe: The picture waits for a whole frame to arrive, so a small slowdown in data causes a delay rather than a drop in quality.

User: why does a laptop run hot even when idle
Joe: Power still flows through the processor and chipset to keep them responsive, and even that steady trickle becomes noticeable heat.

User: why does a fan cool a computer by more than it warms it
Joe: Moving air carries heat from the chips to the vents, and the heat leaves with the exhaust rather than staying inside the case.

User: why does a computer need airflow from the front as well as the back
Joe: It needs a steady supply of cool air to pass over the hot components, and a blocked front makes the back exhaust warm instead.

User: why do laptops get hot under the keyboard rather than under the base
Joe: The heat sinks sit beneath the keyboard to keep the palm rest cool, so the heat is deliberately moved away from where your hands rest.

User: why does a desktop have more fans than a laptop
Joe: A desktop has room for larger heat sinks and bigger fans that can move much more air quietly than a laptop's small ones.

User: why does a server rack need more power than a laptop
Joe: It runs continuously with redundant parts for reliability, and every component running flat out generates far more heat than a laptop.

User: why does a data centre need cooling at all
Joe: Every piece of equipment releases heat as it works, and electronics fail sooner as their temperature rises, so the heat has to be carried away.

User: why is a data centre placed where it is
Joe: It needs large amounts of electricity and reliable network links, and often plentiful cooling water nearby, which decides where it can go.

User: why is a copper cable preferred over wireless for a data centre link
Joe: It carries far more data with less interference and lower delay, and inside a building the path is short enough for the loss to be acceptable.

User: why does a fibre optic cable carry far more than a copper one
Joe: Light travels through glass with almost no loss over long distances and across a huge range of frequencies, so the same cable carries vastly more.

User: why does light bend in a fibre optic cable
Joe: The cladding around the core reflects light back into the core by total internal reflection, so pulses stay on their path down the cable.

User: why does a long fibre cable need repeaters
Joe: Light weakens as it travels, and the signal has to be reshaped periodically so it can still be read at the far end.

User: why does a network switch make a local network faster than a hub
Joe: A switch sends a frame only to the port leading to the right device, so the available bandwidth is not shared as it would be on a hub.

User: why does a router slow things down compared to a switch
Joe: A router examines each packet closely enough to decide where to send it, which costs more processing than simply forwarding it to one port.

User: why does a wired connection feel faster than wireless even with the same bandwidth
Joe: Wireless shares a channel and deals with interference and retransmissions, so its real throughput is well below its stated rate.

User: why does a signal get weaker through walls
Joe: Walls absorb part of the energy, and thicker or denser walls absorb more of it, so the signal arriving on the other side is lower.

User: why does a repeater improve a weak wireless signal
Joe: It listens to what it receives and transmits that again with more power, so it overcomes the loss that happened between the original source and the repeater.

User: why does a firewall not make a computer invulnerable
Joe: It only examines the traffic passing through it, so anything already inside the machine or arriving through another route is untouched.

User: why can a computer be infected through an email attachment
Joe: Opening the attachment runs code with your own permissions, and that code can then reach everything you can reach.

User: why should software be kept up to date
Joe: Updates often close the specific weaknesses that attackers exploit, so staying behind leaves a known way in open.

User: why does antivirus software slow a computer down sometimes
Joe: It scans files as they are opened, which means reading through a great deal of data before letting anything run.

User: why is a strong password better than a long weak one
Joe: Length multiplies the number of guesses needed, so a long password holds up far longer even with an unusual mix of characters.

User: why does two-factor authentication help even if a password leaks
Joe: The second factor comes from something the attacker does not have, so the stolen password alone is not enough to get in.

User: why is public wifi risky even with a password
Joe: Everyone on the same network can potentially see traffic that is not encrypted, and the shared password does not protect you from the other users.

User: why does a bank website matter if any site can ask for your card details
Joe: The look of a genuine site can be copied exactly, so the only reliable check is the address in the bar and the padlock beside it.

User: why should you not plug an unknown USB stick into a work computer
Joe: A device can run code as soon as it is connected, so an unknown stick can install something long before anyone notices.

User: why does a smartphone need updating just to fix security
Joe: Some faults let data leave the device without the owner pressing anything, so a patch closes that route even though nothing visible was wrong.

User: why does turning your phone off occasionally help
Joe: It clears the memory and closes every open program, so a device that has been running for months gets back a clean start.

User: why does more storage space make a phone faster
Joe: A nearly full drive spends most of its effort deciding where to put things, so leaving room free cuts that overhead.

User: why does closing background apps help battery life
Joe: Anything running keeps checking for updates and holding memory, and closing it stops that work happening in the background.

User: why is a phone warmer while charging
Joe: The charging circuit itself generates heat, and the phone may also be running at the same time, so both add warmth to the same case.

User: why does a phone lose charge faster in a cold car
Joe: A cold battery cannot deliver its rated charge, and the phone may work harder to stay responsive, which uses more of what it has.

User: why does a screen in sunlight become hard to read
Joe: Sunlight overwhelms the screen's own backlight, so the display has to be turned much brighter to compete with it.

User: why do phone cameras struggle to focus in low light
Joe: Focus needs detail to lock onto, and dim light means a longer exposure that blurs that detail away as the camera searches.

User: why does a phone camera make moving subjects blurry in low light
Joe: The longer exposure the low light demands gives the subject time to move within the shot, which smears the image.

User: why do front and back cameras on a phone show different views
Joe: One is mirrored and wide for the user, while the other points away from them and is not mirrored, so the two appear opposite.

User: why does a video filmed at night flicker under some lights
Joe: The camera adjusts its exposure many times a second to match the light, and if that cycle clashes with the light's own frequency it shows as flicker.

User: why does a phone get hot while recording video
Joe: Recording runs the camera sensor, the processor and the screen all at once, and together they generate enough heat to feel through the case.

User: why does a smart speaker need a network connection
Joe: Most of its processing happens on remote servers rather than in the device, so a working connection is needed to hear and answer at all.

User: why is a robot that walks on two legs harder to balance than one on wheels
Joe: Balance has to be actively maintained as the feet move, and any error in that correction grows quickly into a fall.

User: why does a robot arm stop instantly in an emergency
Joe: The stop cuts power to the motors, so gravity and inertia bring it down instead of the arm continuing towards whatever it was about to hit.

User: why does a robot need calibration before it can work precisely
Joe: Sensors and joints always have small errors, and calibration measures them so the control software can subtract them out.

User: why do robot hands have so many small motors
Joe: A useful hand has many joints, and each one needs its own motor to move independently with enough control to grip an object.

User: why does a robot gripper use rubber pads
Joe: Rubber increases friction and gives a little give, so an object is held firmly without the gripper crushing it.

User: why does a modern robot use cameras to correct itself while working
Joe: The position of the parts shifts slightly from run to run, and checking with a camera lets the robot adjust rather than fail.

User: why does a burglar alarm need a battery backup
Joe: An intruder often cuts the power to make the alarm look broken, and the battery keeps it working regardless.

User: why does a security camera need a good lens more than a high resolution sensor
Joe: A poor lens throws light unevenly and softens detail, and the sensor cannot restore what the lens never captured.

User: why does a camera mounted high see less detail at a doorway
Joe: A high angle looks down over the head of anyone passing, so the face and any distinguishing marks are hidden from view.

User: why does a doorbell camera sometimes miss someone approaching from the side
Joe: A narrow field of view only covers straight ahead, so someone walking in from the side can pass outside what the lens sees.

User: why does a safe deposit box need both the key and a code
Joe: Neither works alone, so losing one still leaves the contents protected even though the other is in your possession.

User: why does a fingerprint work on a phone but not on a very smooth key
Joe: The sensor reads ridges, and a smooth surface has none for it to match, while a finger has them even when the surface underneath is smooth.

User: why do passwords fail on a keyboard with a different layout
Joe: The characters are placed differently, so the same key press produces a different symbol than the one intended.

User: why does a mechanical keyboard last longer than a membrane one
Joe: Each key is a separate switch that closes a contact, while a membrane presses a flexible sheet, and the switches tolerate far more presses.

User: why do laptop keys feel shallow compared to a desktop keyboard
Joe: A laptop has to be thin, so the travel is short and the mechanism is small, while a desktop key can use a taller switch with more travel.

User: why does a computer keyboard have a key that does nothing
Joe: It often exists on laptops to fill a gap in the layout, and on desktop keyboards it can carry a symbol used by a shortcut.

User: why does a monitor show flicker on camera even if it looks fine to the eye
Joe: The camera samples the display at its own rate, so any mismatch with the monitor's own cycle appears as banding or flicker.

User: why does a curved monitor reduce glare
Joe: The curve means the screen is angled slightly away from a straight line between your eye and a window, so reflections land elsewhere.

User: why does a matte monitor screen look duller than a glossy one
Joe: A glossy coating reflects more light back at you and adds a clearer image, while the matte layer scatters that light instead.

User: why does a solar panel face the equator in most places
Joe: Tilting toward the equator keeps the panel facing the sun through the middle of the day, when the sunlight is strongest.

User: why does a wind turbine stop in a storm
Joe: Above a set wind speed the blades are braked, because the loads on the blades and tower grow faster than the extra power can justify.

User: why do turbine blades taper towards the tip
Joe: The blade needs less stiffness further out, where it moves less, so tapering it captures the wind with less material and weight.

User: why does a hydroelectric dam need a height difference
Joe: Water falling further releases more potential energy, and that drop through the turbine is what turns the generator.

User: why does a dam's reservoir flood land upstream
Joe: To keep a steady water supply the river has to be held back, and the valley behind the dam floods as the level rises.

User: why does a cumulus cloud look puffy at the top and flat at the base
Joe: It rises until the air around it is the same temperature, and that level is uniform across the cloud, so the base forms flat while the top keeps billowing.

User: why do clouds turn dark at the bottom when it is about to rain
Joe: A thick cloud blocks the light from reaching its base, so the underside sits in shadow while the top stays lit.

User: why does a lenticular cloud hold its shape while everything else changes
Joe: A steady wind over a mountain sets up a standing wave, and the cloud forms and evaporates in the same place each time.

User: why does a cloud over a mountain cap look different in shape
Joe: Rising air squeezes over the summit, and moisture condenses in exactly that rising stream, so the cloud copies the shape of the airflow.

User: why does dew form on grass in the early morning
Joe: The ground radiates its heat away overnight and the air above it cools to dew point, so the water in the air condenses onto the cold surface.

User: why does frost form when the air temperature is below freezing
Joe: Water vapour deposits straight onto a surface cold enough to freeze it, so the crystals grow as ice rather than first forming dew.

User: why does a cold car window fog on the inside
Joe: Your breath is warm and damp and the glass is cold, so the vapour in the air condenses on it rather than dispersing.

User: why does fog form over water and not over land in the early morning
Joe: Water loses heat slowly overnight, so the air just above it stays close to its dew point, and land cools much faster and loses its moisture instead.

User: why does radiation fog form in valleys rather than on hills
Joe: Cold air sinks and pools in the low ground overnight, and that pooled air can reach saturation where the air above the hill never does.

User: why does a cold front arrive as a line of cloud rather than evenly
Joe: The steeper slope of the advancing cold air lifts warm air sharply along a narrow boundary, and the cloud forms along that line.

User: why does a warm front bring cloud ahead of the rain
Joe: The warm air rides up over the cold ahead of it, and it rises gently and far ahead, so the cloud sits in front of where the rain reaches the ground.

User: why does thunderstorm cloud build so tall
Joe: Strong rising currents in the warm humid air feed the updraught, and the cloud grows until it reaches the level where the air stops warming.

User: why do storms form more often in the afternoon than the morning
Joe: Ground heating during the day builds an unstable warm layer, and that instability is released as rising air only once it is strong enough.

User: why does a shelf cloud look like a rolling shelf
Joe: It is the leading edge of the storm's downdraft spreading out ahead of the storm and lifting the warm air in front of it.

User: why does the air go still and the light turn green before a storm
Joe: A heavy shelf cloud spreads out overhead and blocks most of the light while the still air shows that the downdraft has arrived.

User: why does a mesocyclone spawn tornadoes
Joe: Winds turning at different speeds in a rotating storm leave a tighter rotation inside it, and that tighter rotation can reach down to the ground.

User: why are tornado warnings issued for a whole area rather than a point
Joe: The storm moves fast and the tornado itself is small, so warning only where it is would leave people outside the warning line unprepared.

User: why does a tornado do more damage to one street than the next
Joe: Its path is narrow and it weakens and strengthens as it goes, so houses in the strongest part take far more damage than those at the edge.

User: why does wind speed at the centre of a hurricane drop so much
Joe: The eye is filled with sinking air that is warmer and calmer, and the strongest winds are in the ring of thunderstorms around it.

User: why do hurricanes not form over the open ocean far from the equator
Joe: A hurricane needs the rotating effect of a large turning system to start it spinning, and that effect goes to zero at the equator.

User: why does the sahel produce less rain as the Atlantic warms
Joe: Warming the Atlantic increases evaporation and strengthens the rain over the ocean, which leaves less moisture falling over the land further inland.

User: why does an increase in sea surface temperature strengthen a storm
Joe: Warmer water supplies more water vapour to the air feeding the storm, and more vapour means more energy available when the air rises.

User: why can one hurricane change the whole season
Joe: A single storm can cool the sea surface over a wide area, and that cooler water suppresses the development of everything that comes after it.

User: why do storms avoid the middle of the atlantic
Joe: The surface is too cool there for storms to build, because cold water sinks beneath it and the warm layer above is too thin to hold the energy needed.

User: why does an atmospheric river deliver so much rain at once
Joe: A long band of moisture carries water from the warm ocean into the atmosphere, and when it hits the mountains the whole band is lifted at once.

User: why does the sahara appear on maps as a labelled desert rather than a green area
Joe: The rainfall is below what trees need, so maps show it as desert and the few oases stand out as the only permanent settlements.

User: why does a desert get wind-blown sand rather than round pebbles
Joe: Wind carries the finest grains easily, so the sand moves and the heavier pieces are left behind where they fell.

User: why is dust picked up only when the ground is dry and bare
Joe: Vegetation holds the soil in place and damp binds the grains together, so wind only lifts dust where both are missing.

User: why is a dust storm harmful even far from the source
Joe: Fine particles travel long distances and enter the lungs, where they irritate the airways and can carry fungi and toxic substances.

User: why do storm drains flood so easily
Joe: Once rain falls faster than the pipes can carry it, the water has nowhere else to go, and it rises out of the drains into the street.

User: why is flood water most dangerous in a valley
Joe: Water gathers there from the whole area above and can rise far faster than the people at the bottom expect.

User: why do some rivers flood only in one particular season
Joe: Their basin is fed by snowmelt or seasonal rain, so all the water arrives at once when the temperature rises or the rains begin.

User: why does a flash flood happen with little warning
Joe: A small steep catchment fills far quicker than a large one, so the rise comes with little time to react.

User: why is a floodplain so useful for farming
Joe: It is flat, deep in soil and watered by the river's own overflow, which is what makes it the most productive land along a river.

User: why do people build houses on stilts in flood-prone areas
Joe: It lifts the floor above the level the flood water reaches, which protects the building without needing to hold back the water itself.

User: why is a levee a double-edged solution
Joe: It keeps water off one side of the land but also prevents that land from soaking it up, so a bigger flood then arrives where it is not expected.

User: why does a mangrove forest reduce storm damage
Joe: The trees break the force of the waves and drag the water down as it moves through them, so less of the wave energy reaches the buildings behind.

User: why do mangrove trees survive being flooded with salt water
Joe: They filter the salt out at their roots and can expel the excess through their leaves, so they can live where other trees cannot.

User: why does a salt marsh turn over a lot of organic matter
Joe: Tides bring in plant material and the wet soil goes without oxygen, so decomposition is slow and much of the carbon stays stored in the mud.

User: why are wetlands such good carbon stores
Joe: Because the waterlogged soil excludes oxygen, and organic matter breaks down far more slowly without it.

User: why does draining a wetland release so much carbon
Joe: Once the water goes the soil gets air, and everything stored in it begins to rot and give off carbon that had been kept there.

User: why do peat bogs take so long to form
Joe: Peat builds up where dead plant material rots only partly, and it takes thousands of years for a metre of it to accumulate.

User: why is cutting a peat bog for fuel bad
Joe: The peat has stored carbon for thousands of years, and burning it releases all of it at once along with the extra it had been keeping out of the air.

User: why does a river delta need sediment as much as water
Joe: Sediment builds the land the delta is made of, and without a new supply the land simply erodes away into the sea.

User: why does a dam eventually cause a delta to shrink
Joe: The dam traps the sediment that used to feed the delta, so the land stops being built up and starts being washed away.

User: why do oysters build reefs that are hard to see
Joe: The reef is the accumulated shells of many generations, and each new oyster settles on the shells of the ones that came before.

User: why does an oyster reef help the shore
Joe: The living shells break up incoming waves and hold sand in place, so the beach behind it survives storms it would otherwise lose.

User: why do oysters filter so much water
Joe: They pump water over their gills to feed on what it carries, and in doing so they strain out plankton and particles from a very large volume.

User: why does clean water mean more oysters in a bay
Joe: They cannot survive the sediment and contaminants that arrive with dirty runoff, and murky water also stops the tiny food they filter reaching them.

User: why do coral bleaching events follow a heat spike exactly
Joe: The heat damages the algae living in the coral tissue, and the coral sheds it, leaving white bone showing through.

User: why does a bleached coral not simply regrow its algae
Joe: The heat or other stress may still be present, and the coral is also short of the food that algae normally supplied while it recovers.

User: why do corals compete for space
Joe: Space on a reef is limited, and corals that cover each other outgrow them, so they fight for the same patch of rock.

User: why do corals have a specific temperature range they tolerate
Joe: Their algae partner only functions within a narrow temperature band, and outside it the partnership breaks down.

User: why do coral reefs support so many fish when the coral looks lifeless
Joe: The coral structure creates the shelter, and the living tissue is what the small organisms that feed the reef eat, so removing it empties the reef.

User: why is sea grass meadow so important on a shallow coast
Joe: It slows the water, holds sediment and provides shelter for young fish, and it also takes up nutrients that would otherwise feed algae.

User: why does a sea grass meadow help the water stay clear
Joe: The plants take the nutrients out of the water themselves, which starves the algae that would otherwise cloud it and smother them.

User: why do kelp forests grow so quickly
Joe: Kelp is a fast-growing brown alga that can add length in a single day, and the cool nutrient-rich water of a coast suits it well.

User: why do kelp forests need rocky seabed to attach to
Joe: They have a holdfast that grips the rock, and without a hard surface they would simply be washed away before they could grow.

User: why does an urchin barren form where kelp used to grow
Joe: When the predators of urchins are removed, the urchins multiply and eat every kelp holdfast, and the forest cannot recover.

User: why is an urchin considered both friend and foe of a reef
Joe: It grazes the algae that would otherwise take over the rock, which helps corals, but in large numbers it destroys the kelp that shelters the reef.

User: why do lobster and crab populations fall when urchin numbers rise
Joe: The urchins remove the kelp that those animals shelter and feed in, so the food and cover they depend on disappears with it.

User: why do seaweed farms work well in cold clean water
Joe: The species grown need cool water with strong currents and plenty of light, and those conditions are found on clean exposed coasts.

User: why does a shark's fin soup take so many sharks
Joe: A fin grows slowly on a fish that matures late, and only a few fins are used, so each bowl requires an animal that took decades to reach that size.

User: why are sharks slow to reproduce
Joe: They mature late, have few young at a time and carry them for a long time, which is exactly the pattern that collapses quickly under fishing pressure.

User: why is a whale's fall such an important event on the seabed
Joe: A dead whale supplies food for years, and a whole community of scavengers, worms and bacteria grows on it that would not otherwise be there.

User: why does a whale skeleton sink so slowly
Joe: The blubber and the body hold it up for a long time, so it drifts down to the seabed over days rather than sinking straight away.

User: why do bones on the seabed become a habitat of their own
Joe: They offer a hard surface in a soft sediment world, which lets organisms that need one attach and grow.

User: why is a sperm whale so hard to study
Joe: It spends most of its time deep underwater on long dives, and it is large and slow to approach, so almost everything known came from strandings.

User: why do whales strand in groups
Joe: They are social animals that travel together, and if one goes wrong the others often follow, so a single navigational error strands many.

User: why can human sonar disturb whales badly
Joe: It floods their hearing with sound at their own frequency, and the noise can be intense enough that they lose their bearings or panic-dive.

User: why do shipping lanes have to avoid whale grounds
Joe: Collisions with ships are a major cause of death for whales, and slowing or rerouting ships reduces the strikes.

User: why do shipping routes pass through whale grounds at all
Joe: Because those channels often give the shortest passage, and the routes were fixed long before the risk to whales was understood.

User: why do plastic bottles end up in the stomach of seabirds
Joe: Floating plastic pieces look like the small fish seabirds eat, so the birds swallow them, and the plastic blocks the stomach so they feel full and starve.

User: why is plastic that breaks down into small pieces worse than large pieces
Joe: Small fragments are eaten by more animals and are far harder to remove from the environment once they are released.

User: why do microplastics end up in drinking water
Joe: They are small enough to pass through normal filtration and they persist, so even treated water can contain a few particles.

User: why does soap scum from washing dishes reach the ocean
Joe: Wastewater treatment removes much but not all of what goes into it, and the rest is discharged to rivers that carry it on to the sea.

User: why does a wastewater plant need so much oxygen
Joe: Bacteria doing the work need air to grow on, and without that oxygen they die and the plant simply stops treating anything.

User: why does a treatment plant leave a nutrient-rich sludge
Joe: The bacteria remove the organic matter and much of the nitrogen and phosphorus, and what is left has to be dried and handled rather than returned.

User: why does farming river silt lead to flooding downstream
Joe: Sediment that used to settle on the land is carried past instead, and when it settles in the channel the riverbed rises and the flood level rises with it.

User: why do concrete sea walls kill the beach they protect
Joe: The wall removes the beach the waves would otherwise absorb energy against, so the water reaches the wall with more force and scours more sand out.

User: why do rock revetments look gentler than sea walls but behave the same
Joe: The loose rock absorbs wave energy better, but the slope still removes the natural beach that would have absorbed most of it.

User: why does building a groyne starve the beach next door of sand
Joe: It stops sand moving along the shore, so the sand that would have reached the neighbouring beach is trapped behind the groyne instead.

User: why does a harbour need constant dredging
Joe: Rivers keep bringing sediment and the harbour traps it, so without dredging the channel silts up and becomes too shallow to use.

User: why do robots struggle with transparent objects
Joe: Light passes through rather than reflecting, so a sensor gets almost no signal back and cannot tell an object is there at all.

User: why did the romans build roads across the empire
Joe: To move legions, tax, and messages quickly, and once the roads existed merchants used them too, which fed the trade that paid for them.

User: why does a fridge need to be defrosted
Joe: Ice around the cooling coil insulates the pipework, so the fridge has to work harder to get cold air into the compartment.

User: why do horses sleep standing up
Joe: A stay in the leg tendons locks the legs rigid, letting them rest without falling over, though they do lie down for deep sleep.

User: why does the sahara dust fertilise the amazon
Joe: The dust carries minerals that the amazon's own heavy rain leaches out of its own soil, so it arrives as a nutrient the forest was short of.
